copy. Then `ResetChangeFeedIndexUnsafe()` (`:1017-1024`), so this process rebuilds on its next read.
Resetting rather than patching in place is the provably-correct choice and costs one re-parse of a
file that just got smaller. A compaction that would drop nothing skips the rewrite entirely, which
makes repeat calls cheap and the operation idempotent.

**`FileShare.Delete` on the three change-log read opens.** Every read open in this provider uses
`FileShare.ReadWrite` and not `FileShare.Delete` — `:838` (index refresh), `:790`
(`ChangeFeedEndsWithNewlineAsync`) and `:1076` (`ReadChangeFeedLinesAsync`). On Windows,
`File.Move(…, overwrite: true)` over a destination that any process holds open **without**
`FILE_SHARE_DELETE` fails. In-process readers are serialized by `_changeFeedGate`, so the process
this breaks is exactly the cross-process reader the log exists for. The change is three flags and it
belongs to this item, not to a tidying pass: it also gives Windows the POSIX behaviour the reader
already assumes, where an open handle keeps reading the old, unlinked file.

**Trigger.** A method the operator or host calls, with a result carrying `LinesBefore`, `LinesAfter`,
`BytesBefore`, `BytesAfter` and a dry-run flag — which is what makes `ROADMAP.md:32`'s "filesystem
recovery *and* compaction tooling" honest rather than half-shipped. Deliberately **not** an
auto-compact option on `FileSystemStateLedgerStoreOptions`: `StateHandle` prunes after every
successful append (`docs/providers/index.md:185`), so an auto-compactor on that path makes an
unrelated address's append block on `_changeFeedGate` for a whole-file rewrite, turning append
latency bimodal for a storage concern that is not urgent. And deliberately not a `Statesman.Tooling`
command: that project references only `Statesman.Abstractions`
(`src/Statesman.Tooling/Statesman.Tooling.csproj:8`) and has never seen a provider type, and — the
reason that actually decides it — the append is two file opens (`:722-775`, with the comment at
`:741-747` stating it outright), so an external compactor could rename between a writer's tail check
and its append. **Compaction must run in the writer process**, which kills the standalone-CLI option
on correctness rather than on taste.

#### 5. The `_changes.gen` generation seqlock

A sidecar file holding one integer, bumped **before** the rename. Missing reads as `0`, so every
existing store directory is compatible without migration. `RefreshChangeFeedIndexUnsafeAsync` reads
it before its refresh and again after, discarding the index and retrying on a change.

The reasoning has to be stated precisely, because the obvious version of it is wrong. Phase 11's
invalidation rule is a file-length shrink check plus a re-read of the remembered tail bytes at the
remembered offset, and its own comment (`:828`) concedes that *"a same-length rewrite of an earlier
line is undetected"*. Compaction is the first mechanism that rewrites earlier lines, so that
concession stops being hypothetical. Working the arithmetic through: for a partially-scanned
cross-process reader to *falsely* keep its index across a compaction, the bytes that land at its
remembered offset must equal its remembered tail line — and with uniform line widths that requires
**two byte-identical lines in the log**. Two byte-identical lines are constructible, by a ping-pong
import: importing `(P1, A, R)`, then `(P2, A, R)`, then `(P1, A, R)` again produces a first and a
third line that match byte for byte, because `ImportAsync` appends a line whenever the existing
revision file's `GlobalPosition` differs from the incoming one (`:259`), and the third import differs
from the second. So the compactor's duplicate-collapse rule is what removes that witness, and the
tail re-verify is sufficient **only because of it**.

That is exactly why the generation counter ships: it replaces "sufficient because of an arithmetic
argument over the current line format and the current collapse rule" with "sufficient because the
reader knows the file it indexed is the file it is reading". A future change to either the line
format or the collapse rule cannot silently reintroduce the hole. The cheaper alternative — relying
on "strictly shorter output with no duplicate lines" — is rejected in the addendum with its exact
failure witness, and the honest accounting of what that witness costs to build is recorded there
too: it needs the collapse rule disabled as well as the generation check.

Cost: two tiny file reads per feed read, on a path Phase 11 measured at 0.04 ms median at 200,000
lines. A generation bumped *after* the rename would leave a window in which a compacted log carries
an unchanged generation, which is the unsafe pairing; before the rename, a crash between the two
leaves a bumped generation over an uncompacted log, which costs one spurious index discard.

#### 6. `ManualTimeProvider.CreateTimer`

`src/Statesman.Testing/ManualTimeProvider.cs` is 41 lines: a constructor, `GetUtcNow`, `Advance` and
`SetUtcNow`. It **does not override `CreateTimer` and it does not throw** — it inherits
`TimeProvider`'s base implementation, which schedules a real `System.Threading.Timer` on the system
clock. That is worse than throwing: a caller gets a timer that silently ignores `Advance`.

Two shipped consumers route through it, both via `new PeriodicTimer(interval, timeProvider)`:
`src/Statesman.Extensions.Hosting/StatesmanHostingExtensions.cs:74` (the maintenance scan loop) and
`src/Statesman.Outbox/StatesmanOutboxHostedService.cs:98` (the outbox poll loop, alongside a
`TimeProvider`-based `Task.Delay` backoff at `:211`). There are no direct `CreateTimer` or `ITimer`
uses in `src/`.

The trap is the classic one: firing a callback while holding `_gate` (`:5`) deadlocks any callback
that calls `Advance` or `GetUtcNow`. The due set is computed under the lock and every callback is
invoked outside it. The proof is a count, not a bound — a timer scheduled at T+5s fires **exactly
once** on `Advance(6s)` and **not at all** on `Advance(4s)`; a periodic timer fires **exactly three
times** on one `Advance` spanning three periods, not once. That is the shape the Phase 8 timing
lesson asks for.

`ManualTimeProvider` is public in a shipped package referenced by six projects, so this is a public
behaviour change rather than a test-only edit.

**Lease renewal is not a timer and is not helped by this.** `StateChangeDispatcher` measures the
renew interval with `Stopwatch.GetTimestamp()` (`:213`, `:238`), not with `TimeProvider`. Making
lease-renewal cadence testable is a separate change — moving it to `TimeProvider.GetUtcNow` — and it
is a behaviour change to the Phase 10 persistent-leader mechanism that deserves its own review.
Recorded here because Phase 7's "defaults need a live probe" lesson came from exactly this area.

#### 7. The four deferred Minors

The four the Phase 11 final review deferred, in one item because each is a few lines and none
changes public API.

| # | Site | Change |
|---|---|---|
| D1 | `src/Statesman.Persistence.Redis/RedisStateLedgerStore.cs:437` | `PruneAsync` calls `ChangeFeedKey()` inline where `ImportAsync` hoists a `changesKey` local (`:283`). Pure consistency: the call happens **once**, so there is no allocation delta and no behaviour change. |
| D2 | `src/Statesman.Persistence.FileSystem/FileSystemStateLedgerStore.cs:871-873` | `stream.CopyToAsync(buffer)` then `buffer.ToArray()` copies the suffix into the `MemoryStream`'s array and then out again. Size the read from `stream.Length - _indexScannedBytes` and read into one buffer. |
| D3 | same file, `:1044-1062` | `TryReadExactlyAsync` reimplements `Stream.ReadAtLeastAsync`, which is .NET 7+ and therefore available on all three targets. `ReadAtLeastAsync(buffer, buffer.Length, throwOnEndOfStream: false)` maps exactly: it returns a short count at end of file, which the one caller (`:859`) already treats as a mismatch. Passing the default `true` would turn that deliberate path into an exception out of a feed read. |
| D4 | `src/Statesman/Runtime/InMemoryStateLedgerStore.cs:408-420` | The cursor lookup is a single O(log N) `IndexOf` and is **not** the cost. The cost is the positional indexer `snapshot[position]` at `:414`, O(log N) on a balanced tree, once per yielded record. The right shape is the set's enumerator advanced once, O(1) amortized per element. Measured at 41 ms for 200,000 records. |

D2 and D4 sit on hot paths that Phase 11's tests pin, and the Phase 11 lesson is to find those tests
*before* changing the mechanism. For D4 that is
`tests/Statesman.Tests/InMemoryChangeFeedTests.cs`, including
`ImportAsync_keeps_both_feed_entries_when_two_addresses_collide_on_position` added in `27ff731`: the
fix must not disturb the comparer's position → address → revision ordering. D4 is the only Minor
with a measurable claim, so it carries a before/after from the same kind of gated harness Phase 11
built (`tests/Statesman.FileSystem.Tests/FileSystemChangeFeedScanMeasurement.cs` is the template).

**D4's outcome, measured in Task 7:** the enumerator-walk replacement described above was
implemented and measured, and it is **slower**, not faster, on this runtime — see addendum
decision 14. It was reverted byte-identical; what ships is the gated measurement harness the
paragraph above anticipated (`tests/Statesman.Tests/InMemoryChangeFeedDrainMeasurement.cs`,
`STATESMAN_MEASURE_INMEMORY_DRAIN=1`), so the 41 ms figure remains checkable rather than fixed.

#### 8. Close-out

`CHANGELOG.md`, `docs/providers/index.md` (six locations that currently say compaction is unshipped
or that Entity Framework Core is SQLite-only: `:23`, `:86`, `:112`, `:179`, `:185`, `:193`),
`ROADMAP.md:32` and `:34`, and a `HANDOVER.md` entry in the Phase 11 entry's shape — commits,
per-task review outcomes, the mechanisms, and what was parked and why. The parked list below is
written with the phase rather than after it, which is what makes it a design decision instead of a
leftover.

#### 9. Head and history files replaced over open readers

Found while researching item 4, not planned: `AtomicWriteAsync` — the method every head write and
every history write goes through — ends with `File.Move(temporary, file, overwrite: true)`, which
fails on Windows against a destination any handle holds open, and `ReadFileAsync` opened history
files with `File.OpenRead`, which grants no `FILE_SHARE_DELETE`. A change-feed read holds each
history file open for the length of one deserialization, so a re-import of that revision could fail
with `UnauthorizedAccessException` and a concurrent `PruneAsync` with `IOException`. Both writes are
now routed through the same POSIX-semantics rename compaction uses (`ReplaceFileOverOpenReaders`),
and `ReadFileAsync` opens with `FileShare.Read | FileShare.Delete`. No storage-format change, no
behaviour change on Linux or macOS, and no widening of what this provider supports across process
boundaries: `docs/providers/index.md`'s statement that the change log is the only channel this
provider has to a reader in another process is about change *notification* and remains true; this
item does not touch it, and neither `docs/providers/index.md` nor this decision list gains a new
cross-process guarantee from it.

#### Explicitly parked, with reasons

Each of these was examined this phase and left undone on purpose.

- **Entity Framework Core migrations for the three engines** (`ROADMAP.md:34`). Item 2 proves the
  model's DDL *generates* on each engine, which is its prerequisite. Shipping versioned migrations
  for packages that deliberately ship none today — `StatesmanOutboxCursorDbContext.cs:12-14` tells
  consumers to own their migration — is a distribution decision, not a test decision. Phase 13.
- **Cross-process filesystem append locking.** Unchanged ruling from Phases 10 and 11:
  `_globalPosition` is an in-memory tick-based allocator (`:1094-1106`) never seeded from the log
  (verified in the constructor, `:75-89`), so a sidecar append lock would close the byte-interleaving
  symptom while two processes still allocated overlapping positions. It is a feature. **Item 4
  strengthens the case for leaving it out**: compaction is only safe because the provider is
  single-writer, so admitting multi-process writers would have to re-open compaction's design too.
  That coupling is new and is worth writing down.
- **`MessageId` collision from ordinal `Root` versus canonical lower-casing.** Unchanged: it needs
  address-equality surgery across the library. A 0.4 design item.
- **Redis and in-memory import residue** — the two thirds of the residue that item 4 does not reach.
  Both need the same position-match repair in two different storage shapes; that is a coherent phase
  of its own and does not belong bolted onto a filesystem task. Phase 13.
- **Converting `StateChangeDispatcher`'s renewal cadence from `Stopwatch` to `TimeProvider`.** Item 6
  makes timers virtual; it does not reach this, because the dispatcher does not use a timer. The
  conversion is a behaviour change to the Phase 10 persistent-leader mechanism and deserves its own
  review. Phase 13.

#### The public API delta

Small and worth stating exactly, because three of the four entries are on shipped surfaces.

- **`Statesman.Persistence.FileSystem` gains one public method with a dry-run overload and one public
  result type**: `FileSystemStateLedgerStore.CompactChangeLogAsync(CancellationToken)`,
  `CompactChangeLogAsync(bool dryRun, CancellationToken)`, and `ChangeLogCompactionResult`.
- **`Statesman.Testing` gains one override on a shipped public type**:
  `ManualTimeProvider.CreateTimer`. This changes the observable behaviour of existing callers, from a
  real system-clock timer to a virtual one.
- **`SnapshotDistributed`'s isolation mapping is a behaviour change on a shipped enum member**, not
  an addition. It belongs in `CHANGELOG.md` `### Changed`. *Amended 2026-09-10 at close-out:* it is
  recorded under `### Fixed` instead, because the discriminating test showed the shipped mapping did
  not merely cost latency — it restores the capability's documented meaning, since a
  `SnapshotDistributed` capture on SQL Server could return one address's pre-write revision beside
  another's post-write revision under the name of a point-in-time snapshot. It remains a behaviour
  change on a shipped enum member, and the changelog entry carries the `ALLOW_SNAPSHOT_ISOLATION ON`
  deployment requirement this bullet already names.
- **`_changes.gen` is a storage-format addition** to the filesystem provider's directory. A missing
  file reads as `0`, so no existing directory needs migrating, but the providers doc must state that
  the directory now holds a second file.

Nothing here is a new capability. The "Forwarding policy (decided before Phase 7, 2026-09-05)"
classification list is unchanged, `docs/architecture/capabilities.md` gains no row and flips no cell,
and `tests/Statesman.Capabilities.Tests/CapabilityMatrixTests.cs` is untouched. Compaction is
deliberately a method on a concrete class rather than an `IStateCapability`, for the reason recorded
as addendum decision 1.


### Phase 13 — Retrying execution strategies, import residue, and the dispatcher's clock

The phase that closes the three items Phase 12 named and parked, plus the Minors its final review
deferred and the one documentation item that keeps a 0.2 backlog bullet honest. All three big items
are defects the library currently asks its *operator* to work around, each documented as a stated
limitation for at least one phase.

**The first is that neither Entity Framework Core package supports `EnableRetryOnFailure`.** Every
Entity Framework Core deployment guide for SQL Server and PostgreSQL tells a consumer to turn it on.
Doing so breaks this store completely, and not subtly — it is the first append.
`docs/providers/index.md:201` says so in as many words: *"a consumer who calls `EnableRetryOnFailure`
gets a retrying execution strategy, and every method on this store opens its own transaction, so all
of them throw `InvalidOperationException` naming `DbContext.Database.CreateExecutionStrategy()`."*
The store is therefore unusable in its primary production shape unless the consumer reads a paragraph
deep in the providers doc.

Two numbers in the pre-Phase-12 addendum's "Named for Phase 13" paragraph are stale and are corrected
here. It says *"19 of 24 tests in `Statesman.EntityFrameworkCore.Tests` fail"* and *"The fix routes
five methods"*. Measured against `94fd0b7` with `EnableRetryOnFailure` forced into the shared test
seam: that suite is now 31 tests, of which **23 fail on PostgreSQL and 24 on SQL Server** against a
zero-failure baseline, and there are **four** transactional methods, not five —
`AppendAsync` (`EntityFrameworkStateLedgerStore.cs:167`), `AcquireAsync` (`:377`), `CaptureAsync`
(`:459`) and `ImportAsync` (`:495`) are the only `BeginTransactionAsync` call sites in either
package. `PruneAsync` (`:565-627`), `EntityFrameworkLease.RenewAsync` (`:860-901`) and
`EntityFrameworkLease.DisposeAsync` (`:903-925`) open no transaction and are already retry-safe, and
`Statesman.Outbox.EntityFrameworkCore` contains no `BeginTransaction` at all — measured at 13 of 13
green under a retrying strategy against live PostgreSQL, so that package needs no code change. The
addendum's shape of the problem was right; its two numbers were not.

**The second is that the Redis and in-memory providers leave an import residue their own code
documents and does not collect.** `docs/providers/index.md:187` names it: re-importing an existing
revision at a *different* `GlobalPosition` leaves the earlier feed entry behind, because Redis
replaces history by revision score and changes by position score
(`RedisStateLedgerStore.cs:325-328`) and the in-memory provider adds the new feed entry without
removing the old one (`InMemoryStateLedgerStore.cs:277`). Phase 12 collected the filesystem third of
this through `CompactChangeLogAsync`; these two were parked as *"a coherent phase of its own"*.
Probing them for this phase found a **second Redis residue in the opposite direction that no document
describes**: importing a different address at a position the feed already holds *evicts* the
incumbent's feed entry, because `SortedSetRemoveRangeByScoreAsync(changesKey, position, position)`
(`:327`) takes whichever member sits at that score. Measured: import `(A, r1, 100)`, then
`(B, r1, 100)`, and the feed holds only B while A's history record survives. That is a record leaving
the change feed while its history record stays — the exact violation Phase 11 exists to prevent, in
the direction Phase 11 exists to prevent it. `docs/providers/index.md:185` covers it only loosely,
as *"Redis overwrites the target's record at the colliding position"*, framed as an overwrite of the
target rather than as the eviction of a bystander. **The member-exact repair below fixes the
documented residue and the undocumented eviction in one change**, which is the strongest argument for
its shape.

**The third is that `StateChangeDispatcher` measures lease renewal with `Stopwatch`.** Three call
sites — `:213`, `:369`, `:375` — and no `TimeProvider` on the type at all. Phase 12's
`ManualTimeProvider.CreateTimer` override deliberately did not reach this, because the dispatcher
does not use a timer. The cost is paid by two tests that spend real time to reach inexact
assertions, and the worse of the two is
`OutboxLeaseTests.The_renewal_clock_survives_a_cycle_boundary` (`:328`), which pins the subtlest bug
in the Phase 10 persistent-leader mechanism — re-stamping the renewal clock once per cycle — and can
only bound it. Its own comment states why: *"`StillHeldAsync` measures the interval with `Stopwatch`,
which no `TimeProvider` can advance."*

Eight pieces of work in dependency order, the last of them the close-out. The Entity Framework Core
theme runs first, for the same reason it did in Phase 12: it is the one that can discover a blocking
design problem, and discovering that early is worth more than finishing anything else early. The four
themes are otherwise independent — nothing in the Entity Framework Core work touches Redis, the
in-memory provider or the outbox, and nothing in the outbox work touches a persistence provider.

#### 1. The four transactional methods run inside the context's execution strategy

Each of `AppendAsync`, `AcquireAsync`, `CaptureAsync` and `ImportAsync` obtains
`context.Database.CreateExecutionStrategy()` and runs its attempt body as that strategy's delegate.
The existing bounded `MaxWriteAttempts` loop stays **outside** the strategy, and the strategy runs
**inside** it: a fresh `DbContext` per outer attempt keeps the cross-attempt isolation the loop has
today, and the strategy's re-invocations are contained to one context.

`ExecuteAsync`, not `ExecuteInTransactionAsync`. Measured by reflection over
`Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore` 10.0.11: the plain `ExecuteInTransactionAsync` overloads open the
transaction at the provider's **default** isolation level and take no parameter for one. Three of the
four methods depend on a level Phase 12 measured as load-bearing — `ReadCommitted` for append and
import (addendum decision 10: `Serializable` *"fixes SQL Server and leaves PostgreSQL broken"*) and
`SnapshotIsolationLevel(context)` for capture (decision 7, a correctness fix rather than a
preference). Only the six-argument overload, which takes a `beginTransaction` factory, could preserve
them, and at that point it is `ExecuteAsync` with extra ceremony plus a `verifySucceeded` predicate
this design does not need.

**Three things every delegate invocation must establish for itself,** because the strategy reuses one
`DbContext`:

- **The change tracker**, cleared as the delegate's first statement. Without it, the
  `context.StatesmanRecords.Add(...)` of a failed attempt is still `Added` on the next one and the
  second `Add` of the same key throws — or, worse, a `SaveChangesAsync` inserts a record for a
  position that was rolled back. This is the exact hazard addendum decision 11 named when it rejected
  `IExecutionStrategy` for Phase 12.
- **The allocated position**, and everything derived from it. `TryAllocatePositionAsync`'s increment
  is rolled back with its transaction, so a retry legitimately allocates a new one; a position
  hoisted above the delegate would be reused after a rollback and break the density guarantee
  decision 10 establishes.
- **Nothing else.** `head`, `current` and the condition check are already attempt-local.

**Which layer owns which failure class.** They do not overlap and must not be merged. The outer loop
owns the sequence-row bootstrap race — `TryAllocatePositionAsync` returning `null` after a lost
`23505`, PostgreSQL-only, detected *structurally* by where it happened because `23505` is classified
transient by no provider — and, for import only, the prune-during-import
`DbUpdateConcurrencyException`. The strategy owns the genuine transient *connection* failures the
provider classifies. The outer loop is not made redundant by the strategy, because the default
`NonRetryingExecutionStrategy` retries nothing. *Amended (Task 1, 2026-09-11):* the race itself is
not PostgreSQL-only — only the `23505` SQLSTATE naming it is. Deleting the outer loop's guard fails
`Concurrent_appends_to_different_addresses_all_succeed` on SQL Server too, reproducibly in 4 of 4
runs, with the identical `InvalidOperationException`. See decision 18.

The delegate's existing transient filter therefore **rolls back and rethrows** rather than looping,
so the strategy gets first refusal and the outer loop absorbs what the strategy gives up on. Under
the default non-retrying strategy that is byte-for-byte today's behaviour: the strategy invokes the
delegate exactly once and rethrows, and `ChangeTracker.Clear()` on a freshly created context is a
no-op, so the executed statement sequence is unchanged. That has to be *demonstrated*, by the
existing suites passing unchanged with no environment variable set, not asserted.

**Idempotency when a commit succeeded but its acknowledgement was lost** is where this earns its
review. The strategy re-invokes the delegate. For `AppendAsync` the replay allocates a new position,
reads the head — which now shows the revision the lost commit wrote — fails `Matches`, rolls back
without burning a position, and returns `Conflict` carrying the record it just wrote. **That is
byte-for-byte what `docs/providers/index.md:201` already documents for the internal retry**, so the
strategy changes nothing about that paragraph. `ImportAsync` is idempotent by construction and
`CaptureAsync` is read-only.

**`AcquireAsync` is the one that changes, and it is a fix.** Today a replay after a lost
insert-acknowledgement re-reads the lease row, finds it live, and returns `null` — "another caller
won". After a lost acknowledgement *this caller's own token* is in that row, so the `null` is wrong:
it tells a caller that holds the lease that it does not. The fix is to generate the token **once per
`AcquireAsync` call, outside the strategy delegate**, so a replayed attempt can recognise its own
write, and to return a live handle when the row's token matches. The failure direction without it is
safe — a spurious `null` means "did not get the lease", and the row expires at `ttl` — but
`EnableRetryOnFailure` is what makes the window reachable at all, so it ships in the same task and is
pinned by a test that simulates the lost acknowledgement deterministically.

#### 2. The retry test surface, the CI step, and two documentation corrections

**A gated whole-suite run, because that is the honest exit criterion.** An environment variable,
`STATESMAN_TEST_EF_RETRY`, makes `EntityFrameworkTestProvider.Configure` add `EnableRetryOnFailure`,
so every existing Entity Framework Core suite runs under a retrying strategy without a second test
matrix. **The exit criterion is an observed count, not a green tick** — Phase 7's lesson:
`failed: 0` at `total: 31` for `Statesman.EntityFrameworkCore.Tests`, `64` for
`Statesman.Conformance.Tests` and `22` for `Statesman.Tooling.Tests`, against the pre-fix baseline of
23 or 24, 15 and 2 failures respectively. *Amended (final review, 2026-09-11):* those were pre-phase
predictions. Shipped and measured this session: `total: 35` for `Statesman.EntityFrameworkCore.Tests`,
`75` for `Statesman.Conformance.Tests`, `22` for `Statesman.Tooling.Tests`, all `failed: 0`. The rise
over the predicted 31 and 64 is fully explained by the phase's own work: four new Task 1 tests minus
the one deleted pinning test plus Task 2's seam test raise the first count by four, and eleven new
conformance cells raise the second by eleven; `Statesman.Tooling.Tests` is unchanged at 22.

**One extra step inside the two existing server jobs, not a fourth CI dimension.** A full
retry-on/off by three-engine matrix doubles two already-slow jobs for no additional signal;
`sqlserver-tests` and `postgres-tests` each gain one step that re-runs the three affected projects
with the variable set.

**A deterministic single-retry proof, because a gated suite proves nothing *retried*.** A
`DbTransactionInterceptor` throws a provider-transient exception exactly once, either before the
commit reaches the server (a genuine transient failure: the replay must produce exactly one record at
exactly one position) or after it (a lost acknowledgement: the replay must produce the documented
`Conflict`). The proof is a count — one context created, two transactions started — which
discriminates a strategy retry from an outer-loop retry, since the outer loop creates a fresh
context and the strategy does not. It is written for **PostgreSQL**, whose `PostgresException` has a
public constructor; SQL Server is covered by the whole-suite run, and the asymmetry is stated in the
test's own comment rather than papered over.

**The pinning test goes red first and is then deleted, not weakened.**
`EntityFrameworkServerEngineTests.A_retrying_execution_strategy_is_rejected_by_every_transactional_method_today`
(`:185-206`) exists to record the limitation and its own comment says *"When Phase 13 routes these
methods through `context.Database.CreateExecutionStrategy().ExecuteAsync(…)`, this test goes red
first."* Its failure output is recorded, then it goes.

**Documentation.** `docs/providers/index.md:201`'s last two sentences are replaced: the supported
configuration becomes `EnableRetryOnFailure` *on*, naming what the store's own bounded retry still
owns and what the strategy owns, and keeping the lost-acknowledgement `Conflict` paragraph verbatim.
And `EntityFrameworkOutboxCursorStore.cs:26-28` still says *"There is no live SQL Server test job in
this repository the way there is for Redis, so the row-lock path above is reasoned about rather than
observed"*, which Phase 12's `sqlserver-tests` job (`.github/workflows/ci.yml:95`) made false.

#### 3. Member-exact import repair on Redis and the in-memory provider

**Redis.** Before the optimistic transaction, read the existing history member for this revision;
inside the same transaction, remove that member from the change feed **by member**:

- The removal key is the stale member's own bytes, so it can only ever remove *this* record's earlier
  feed entry and never a different address's legitimate entry at the same position. That is exactly
  the reasoning `PruneAsync` already records at `:424-429` — *"the changes member and the history
  member are the SAME BYTES on every write path … Member-exact ZREM, never ZREMRANGEBYSCORE: scores
  are IEEE doubles, exact only to 2^53"* — and this is that precedent applied to the one remaining
  path that ignored it.
- The score-range removal on `changesKey` goes away with it. That single deletion is what fixes the
  bystander eviction.
- The score-range removal on `historyKey` **stays**. It is behaviour-neutral today (a history sorted
  set holds at most one member per revision score by construction) and it is belt and braces against
  the one case the member form cannot see, so the change stays confined to the key whose semantics
  are wrong.

The removals and the adds are in the one `ITransaction` (MULTI/EXEC) already guarded on the revision
key, so the repair is the same atomic step as the rewrite. The read must be *outside* the
transaction: StackExchange.Redis queues commands inside an `ITransaction` without results until
`ExecuteAsync`, so a read queued there cannot inform a removal built in the same block. The cost is
one extra round trip per imported record, on a bulk maintenance path that already makes three before
its transaction.

*Rejected: deriving the stale position from the head read, which is free.* The head is the *latest*
revision, and the record being re-imported may not be it, so it answers a different question and
misses the residue on every non-head revision — a worse defect than today's, because it would be
intermittent.

**In-memory.** Capture the previous record before the overwrite, and remove its feed entry inside the
existing `_feedLock` critical section, so a concurrent drain sees the swap or neither half of it and
never a feed missing both entries. The removal key carries the dropped record itself, exactly as
`PruneAsync` already does at `:373-376`, so `FeedEntryComparer` matches on position, canonical
address and revision and cannot take a different address's entry at the same position.

**The filesystem provider is unchanged, on purpose.** Its change log is append-only, and rewriting an
earlier line in place is precisely the hazard addendum decision 4's `_changes.gen` seqlock exists to
guard against. Its repair stays `CompactChangeLogAsync`, and item 4 says so honestly rather than
lowering an assertion to accommodate it.

#### 4. The conformance suite learns the difference between an expectation and a step

`ConformanceStore` gains one optional member, `Func<ValueTask>? Maintain` — the maintenance step a
provider requires before its change feed is fully repaired, or null when the provider repairs itself
on every write. Only the filesystem subclass sets it, to `CompactChangeLogAsync`.

*Rejected: an abstract `bool RepairsImportResidueOnWrite` that branches the assertion.* That encodes
the difference as an **expectation** rather than as a **step**, and it would let a provider regress
into the "no" column silently — the assertion would simply relax. A step cannot be satisfied by
regressing.

Two new shared tests, each recorded with its five-provider verdict:

- **`Re_importing_a_revision_at_a_new_position_leaves_no_stale_feed_entry`.** Import `(A, r1, 100)`,
  `(B, r1, 150)` as a control, then `(A, r1, 200)`; run `Maintain` if the provider has one; drain.
  Exactly one entry for A, at 200, with `Cursor.Position` **and** `Record.GlobalPosition` both 200 —
  the second half is what discriminates the filesystem case, whose pre-compaction envelope carries a
  `Cursor` of 100 beside a `Record.GlobalPosition` of 200. Today: in-memory fails, Redis fails,
  filesystem fails without the hook and passes with it (which is the point of the hook), Entity
  Framework Core passes unchanged (its feed *is* its records table), Tiered skips by construction.
- **`Importing_a_second_address_at_an_occupied_position_does_not_evict_the_first`.** Import
  `(A, r1, 100)` then `(B, r1, 100)`; drain; expect two entries. Today: in-memory passes, filesystem
  passes, Redis **fails** with only B surviving, Tiered skips.

Entity Framework Core cannot hold two records at one `GlobalPosition` —
`StatesmanLedgerDbContext.cs:40` puts a unique index on that column, which
`docs/providers/index.md:185` already documents as *"Entity Framework Core's unique index on
`GlobalPosition` rejects a collision mid-import"*. That difference is a documented provider behaviour
rather than a step, so the shared test skips there against a `RejectsCollidingPositions` flag and the
**throw is asserted by a test on the Entity Framework Core subclass itself**, where the provider's
exception type belongs. A silent skip would lose the documented behaviour; a `DbUpdateException` in
the shared base would put an Entity Framework Core type in a file five providers share.

That five-way discrimination is what proves the tests test the rule and not the suite — the same
standard `Retention_removes_a_pruned_revision_from_the_feed` (`ChangeFeedConformanceTests.cs:300`)
set in Phase 11.

`docs/providers/index.md:187` loses its "**two** providers" residue and gains the statement that
compaction is the filesystem provider's repair; `:185` gains the bystander-eviction correction.

#### 5. `StateChangeDispatcher` measures renewal with a `TimeProvider`

**`ManualTimeProvider` gains two overrides, and it needs both or neither.** `GetTimestamp()` returns
the virtual clock's ticks and `TimestampFrequency` returns `TimeSpan.TicksPerSecond`. Measured, not
assumed: a `TimeProvider` subclass reporting `TimestampFrequency => 1000` and a millisecond
`GetTimestamp` makes the base `GetElapsedTime` scale by the override, so overriding `GetTimestamp`
alone would read elapsed time at `Stopwatch.Frequency`'s scale — identical on Windows, where both are
10,000,000, and **100 times short on the Linux CI leg**, where `Stopwatch.Frequency` is
1,000,000,000. This is the single most important finding behind this item: converting the dispatcher
to `TimeProvider.GetTimestamp()` alone would have changed nothing for a test.

Like Phase 12's `CreateTimer`, this is a **behaviour change on a shipped public type** and belongs in
`CHANGELOG.md` `### Changed` with the same framing: any caller measuring an interval with
`GetElapsedTime` against a `ManualTimeProvider` moves from real to virtual time.

**The dispatcher takes a `TimeProvider` through a constructor overload, not an optional parameter.**
Adding a defaulted parameter to an existing public constructor is source-compatible but
binary-breaking: a consumer assembly compiled against 0.3.0 would throw `MissingMethodException`.
There is no public-API baseline gate in this repository yet (`ROADMAP.md:36`), which is exactly why
the conservative shape is worth taking by hand. The four-argument constructor delegates to the
five-argument one with `TimeProvider.System`; `StillHeldAsync` gains a parameter; and
`StatesmanOutboxExtensions.cs:94` passes the provider the container already resolves, so the DI graph
needs no new registration.

Behaviour-neutral in production, measured: `TimeProvider.System` and `Stopwatch` are the same counter
at the same frequency, so every existing call site computes identical values.

**The tests become counts.** The new renewal test advances a virtual clock 9 seconds (short of a
10-second interval: exactly zero renewals), then 2 seconds (11 seconds since acquisition: exactly
one), then 9 seconds again (20 seconds since acquisition but only 9 since the renewal: still exactly
one) — the last step being the exact form of what `The_renewal_clock_survives_a_cycle_boundary` could
previously only bound. `The_lease_is_renewed_periodically_under_a_nonzero_interval_not_once_per_batch`
converts from a real-time delaying sink to one that advances the virtual clock per batch, turning
`0 < RenewCalls < 25` into an exact number.

**One real-time test stays**, under `TimeProvider.System`, so the production clock path is not left
unexercised. Phase 12's own final review flagged exactly this failure mode — a conversion that cut a
real-time budget and had to be given it back (`46caf1b`) — and
`The_renewal_clock_survives_a_cycle_boundary` keeps its real delays and its inexact assertion for
that reason. Only its comment changes, because the inexactness is now a deliberate choice rather than
a structural limit.

**`_leaseRenewedAt`'s zero default is verified by a test, not by reasoning.** Zero is a `Stopwatch`
origin far in the past under `TimeProvider.System` and the year 1 under `ManualTimeProvider`; the
field is always stamped before a lease exists, so the default should be unreachable — and a test with
a renewal interval of 400 days over a clock that never moves proves it, because an implementation
that failed to stamp would measure roughly two thousand years and renew.

#### 6. The deferred Minors, batched

Three, in one task because each is a few lines and none changes public API.

| # | Site | Change |
|---|---|---|
| D(i) | `tests/Shared/EntityFrameworkTestProvider.cs:177-199` | If the `ALTER DATABASE … SET ALLOW_SNAPSHOT_ISOLATION ON` throws after `CREATE DATABASE` succeeds, `CreateSqlServer` throws before constructing the object that owns teardown, so a GUID-named database is orphaned with nothing that will ever drop it. A `try`/`catch` drops it and rethrows, with the drop itself guarded so a failing cleanup cannot replace the original failure. **Unpinned, and the commit message says so:** reproducing it needs a server that accepts `CREATE DATABASE` and rejects that `ALTER`, which is a permissions configuration rather than a code path. |
| D(ii) | same file, `:361-368` and `:370-376` | A throw from a `Dispose`-time drop **masks the test's own result** — the test may have failed for a real reason and the report shows a `SqlException` from `DROP DATABASE` instead. The leaked database is secondary; it is GUID-named, in a throwaway container. Each drop is wrapped and swallowed, with the masking named as the reason. Unpinned for the same reason as D(i). |
| D(iii) | `src/Statesman.Persistence.FileSystem/FileSystemStateLedgerStore.cs:519-523` | **Closed as a comment correction plus a pinning test — no code change, because the change would be a regression.** |

D(iii) reverses a Phase 12 review Minor, and it does so on measurement rather than on argument. The
review's finding was that `CompactChangeLogAsync`'s duplicate collapse keys on decoded lines rather
than on raw bytes. The decoded comparison is **strictly stronger**: two byte-identical lines always
decode identically, so the invariant the comment states still holds, and the only divergence is a
CRLF-terminated line against an LF-terminated one carrying the same content — which
`TryParseChangeFeedLine` parses to the same position, address and revision, so they are semantic
duplicates. Measured over a log holding one logical line three times as LF, CRLF, LF: compaction
takes 3 lines to 1 and the feed from 3 entries to 1. A raw-byte comparison would leave 2 lines and
one record still yielding twice — the exact defect the collapse exists to remove. The writer never
emits a carriage return (`AppendChangeFeedEntryUnsafeAsync` builds `string.Join('\t', …) + "\n"`), so
the divergence is unreachable from this library's own writes, and the only way to reach it is a
foreign or hand-edited log, where collapsing is the right answer anyway. What ships is a corrected
comment and a test that pins the ruling so it is not re-litigated.

#### 7. Entity Framework Core migrations: a Phase 14 sketch (2026-09-11)

`ROADMAP.md:34` has carried *"EF Core migrations for SQL Server, PostgreSQL, and SQLite"* since 0.2,
and the Phase 12 parked list promised it to Phase 13. **It does not ship in Phase 13, and this
subsection is the reason and the design.** Written now, with the phase rather than after it, so that
Phase 14 starts from a plan instead of a bullet.

**What shipping versioned migrations actually requires.**

1. **Per-engine migration assemblies, not one.** A migration is generated SQL, and the three engines
   differ in every type mapping this model uses: `nvarchar(512)` against `character varying(512)`
   against `TEXT`; `varbinary(max)` against `bytea` against `BLOB`; `datetimeoffset` against
   `timestamptz` (which loses the last tick, addendum decision 12) against `TEXT`; and the 900-byte
   clustered-key limit that is SQL Server-only (decision 9). One assembly cannot serve three, so the
   shape is **three packages per context, six in total**:
   `Statesman.Persistence.EntityFrameworkCore.SqlServer`, `.PostgreSQL` and `.Sqlite`, and the same
   three for `Statesman.Outbox.EntityFrameworkCore` — each taking the one provider package reference
   the core packages deliberately refuse.
2. **The core two packages keep their reference set**, `Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore` and
   `.Relational` and nothing else
   (`Statesman.Persistence.EntityFrameworkCore.csproj:11-12`,
   `Statesman.Outbox.EntityFrameworkCore.csproj:11-12`). `SnapshotIsolationLevel`'s own comment
   (`EntityFrameworkStateLedgerStore.cs:824-826`) states the rule and it applies verbatim here:
   *"adding a reference to either server provider to read an isolation level would push both onto
   every consumer."* A migration package may take one provider reference precisely because a consumer
   opts into exactly one.
3. **`MigrationsAssembly` and `MigrationsHistoryTable` are the seam.** `__EFMigrationsHistory` is
   shared per database, so a consumer running their own migrations alongside a shipped set in one
   database needs the history table configured or the two sets interleave. And because both contexts
   are designed to be subclassed — `StatesmanLedgerDbContext` and `StatesmanOutboxCursorDbContext`
   are non-sealed and take `DbContextOptions`, which is what every test does today
   (`EntityFrameworkChangeFeedConformanceTests.cs:39-45`) — a shipped migration must target the
   **base** context's model, and the subclass must not add to that model.
   *Amended (Phase 14 planning, 2026-09-11):* necessary and **not sufficient**. Migrations are
   discovered by exact `[DbContext]` type equality, so a subclass that adds nothing at all still gets
   nothing applied — `Database.Migrate()` returns normally having created only the history table.
   `MigrationsAssembly` alone does not fix it; a subclass-tolerant `IMigrationsAssembly` replacement
   does. See the Phase 14 section item 2 and decision 22.
4. **A baseline migration for every consumer already on `EnsureCreated`.** `EnsureCreated` writes no
   migration-history row, so a consumer adopting a shipped migration gets "table already exists" on
   the first update unless an explicit baseline is provided and documented.
   *Amended (Phase 14 planning, 2026-09-11):* the failure is confirmed, the remedy is a baseline
   **history row**, not a baseline **migration** — no extra migration is generated. Three
   `IHistoryRepository` calls write it, shipped as `BaselineAsync`. See the Phase 14 section item 3
   and decision 32.
5. **A model-drift gate in CI, as the exit criterion.** Shipping migrations without
   `dotnet ef migrations has-pending-model-changes` means the first model edit silently ships a
   schema the migration does not produce. One job per engine per context, plus
   `Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.Design` in the build and a pinned `dotnet-ef` tool.
   *Amended (Phase 14 planning, 2026-09-11):* over-specified. The check opens no connection on any
   engine, so the six jobs buy nothing; it ships as six assertions in the two existing Entity
   Framework Core test projects, which additionally assert that migrations were discovered at all —
   something the command-line form cannot. `Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.Design` is still needed,
   for authoring rather than for the gate, and the pinned tool ships as a manifest. See the Phase 14
   section item 5 and decisions 23 and 24.
6. **A versioning policy that does not exist yet.** A shipped migration is a permanent public
   contract: every model change needs a new migration, every migration needs a down, and the package
   version and the migration id are coupled forever. Nothing in this repository currently versions
   anything but the NuGet package.
7. **The consumer-owns-it contract stays valid.** Shipped migrations are **opt-in**.
   `StatesmanOutboxCursorDbContext.cs:12-14` — *"No migration ships in this package — subclass this
   context and own the migration"* — describes the supported default arrangement, not a limitation to
   be removed, and `docs/providers/index.md:201`'s *"a consumer who wants Entity Framework Core
   cursors adds one migration for one independent table"* stays true for that arrangement.

None of that is hard. All of it is a **distribution** decision — six packages, a versioning policy,
three new CI gates per context and a documented upgrade path — and together it is larger than items 1
through 6 put together. Phase 13's whole contribution to it is this subsection and an annotation on
`ROADMAP.md:34` pointing here.

#### 8. Close-out

`CHANGELOG.md` consolidated, the addendum's mid-execution decisions recorded once their evidence
exists, and a `HANDOVER.md` entry in the Phase 12 entry's shape — commits, per-task review outcomes,
the mechanisms, and what was parked and why, with **every number copied from a task report rather
than from a brief's prediction**. Plus one correction inside the Phase 12 entry itself: the sentence
at `docs/superpowers/HANDOVER.md:691` still reads *"Final whole-branch review and the CI run that
follows it have not happened yet"*, which that entry's own tail now contradicts.

#### Explicitly parked, with reasons

Each of these was examined this phase and left undone on purpose.

- **Entity Framework Core migrations as code.** Item 7 is the design; Phase 14 is the build. Six
  packages, a versioning policy and three CI gates per context is a distribution decision, and
  shipping half of it would be worse than shipping none.
- **Cross-process filesystem append locking.** Unchanged ruling from Phases 10, 11 and 12, and
  Phase 12's compaction work strengthened it: compaction is only safe *because* the provider is
  single-writer, so admitting multi-process writers would have to re-open compaction's design too.
- **The `MessageId` collision from ordinal `Root` versus canonical lower-casing**
  (`docs/providers/index.md:199`). Unchanged: it needs address-equality surgery across the library.
  A 0.4 design item.
- **The filesystem provider repairing its import residue at import time.** Rewriting an earlier log
  line in place is the hazard addendum decision 4's seqlock exists to guard against. Its repair stays
  `CompactChangeLogAsync`, and item 4's conformance test says so through the `Maintain` hook rather
  than by lowering an assertion.
- **Redis concurrent same-revision `ImportAsync` orphaning a feed member** (2026-09-11, final
  review). Two concurrent imports carrying the same revision to different positions both observe the
  same `current.Revision` and both pass `Condition.StringEqual(revisionKey, current.Revision)`
  (`RedisStateLedgerStore.cs:322-328`), so the second commits after the first. Its member-exact
  removal on the changes key targets the pre-read member and therefore misses the first import's feed
  member, leaving that feed member without a history twin. Pre-existing and unchanged by Phase 13 —
  the old score-range removal left the same orphan in the same scenario — and outside the revision
  guard's discrimination by construction, not by an oversight this phase introduced. Import is a bulk
  restore path the library never drives concurrently against itself, so this is parked as a backlog
  note rather than fixed.
- **D(iii) as a code change.** Measured to be a regression: a raw-byte comparison leaves a duplicate
  yield that the decoded comparison removes, 3 lines to 2 instead of 3 to 1. Closed as a comment
  correction plus a pinning test, which reverses a Phase 12 review Minor on evidence.
- **A retry-on/off by three-engine CI matrix.** One extra step inside the two existing server jobs
  instead. The full matrix doubles two already-slow jobs for no additional signal.
- **Re-attempting the D4 enumerator walk** (the in-memory change feed's positional indexer). Phase 12
  measured it 13 to 30 per cent slower in both configurations and left the gated harness
  (`STATESMAN_MEASURE_INMEMORY_DRAIN=1`) in place. Nothing has changed.

#### The public API delta

Small, and three of the four entries are on shipped surfaces.

- **`Statesman.Outbox` gains one public constructor overload**:
  `StateChangeDispatcher(IStateLedgerStore, IStateChangeSink, IOutboxCursorStore, OutboxOptions, TimeProvider)`.
  Additive; the four-argument constructor stays and delegates to it with `TimeProvider.System`, so it
  is binary-compatible in both directions.
- **`Statesman.Testing` gains two overrides on a shipped public type**:
  `ManualTimeProvider.GetTimestamp()` and `ManualTimeProvider.TimestampFrequency`. This changes the
  observable behaviour of existing callers, from the system counter to the virtual clock, and belongs
  in `CHANGELOG.md` `### Changed` exactly as `CreateTimer` did in Phase 12.
- **`EnableRetryOnFailure` becomes a supported configuration** for
  `EntityFrameworkStateLedgerStore<TContext>`. No signature changes, no schema change, no new package
  reference — but it moves a documented "not supported" to "supported", which is a contract change in
  the consumer's favour and belongs in `CHANGELOG.md` `### Fixed`.
- **`AcquireAsync` returns a live handle rather than `null` after a lost insert acknowledgement.** A
  behaviour change on a shipped method, reachable only under a retrying execution strategy, and a fix
  in the only direction that is safe.

No storage format changes. Nothing here is a new capability: the "Forwarding policy (decided before
Phase 7, 2026-09-05)" classification list is unchanged, `docs/architecture/capabilities.md` gains no
row and flips no cell, and `tests/Statesman.Capabilities.Tests/CapabilityMatrixTests.cs` is untouched
by this phase.


### Phase 14 — Entity Framework Core migrations as code (2026-09-11)

The phase that builds what item 7 above sketches. `ROADMAP.md:34` has carried *"EF Core migrations
for SQL Server, PostgreSQL, and SQLite"* since 0.2, Phase 12 parked it, and Phase 13 answered it with
a design rather than a package. This section is the design corrected by measurement, and Phase 14 is
the build.

**The sketch is right about shape and wrong about the one thing that decides whether any of it
works.** Entity Framework Core discovers migrations by **exact** context-type equality:
`Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.Migrations.Internal.MigrationsAssembly` filters candidate types on
`t.GetCustomAttribute<DbContextAttribute>()?.ContextType == contextType`, a reference comparison
against the *running* context's own type, never an `IsAssignableFrom`. A migration generated against
`StatesmanLedgerDbContext` is therefore **invisible** to the consumer subclass that every test and
every documentation page in this repository uses. Measured this session against Entity Framework Core
10.0.11 with `MigrationsAssembly` correctly configured: `GetPendingMigrations()` returns empty,
`Database.Migrate()` **returns normally having created only the history table**, the shipped table is
absent, and `HasPendingModelChanges()` reports `True` for a reason that has nothing to do with drift.
No exception. No log the consumer reads. The failure is discovered at the first query.

That is the worst available failure mode for a schema tool, and it is the reason this section exists
in its current form. Item 3's sentence *"a shipped migration must target the base context's model,
and the subclass must not add to that model"* is necessary and **not sufficient**: a subclass that
adds nothing at all still gets nothing applied.

Eight pieces of work in dependency order, the last of them the close-out. The two mechanisms come
first and ship no package between them, for the same reason the Entity Framework Core theme ran first
in Phases 12 and 13: they are the two things the whole phase rests on, they are provable against
SQLite alone, and if either is harder than this section claims the phase can be re-scoped before six
packages exist.

#### 1. Six packages, three per context

A migration is generated SQL, and the three engines share no type mapping this model uses. Generated
this session from one base model, three times:

| Property | SQLite | SQL Server | PostgreSQL |
|---|---|---|---|
| `Root` (`HasMaxLength(128)`) | `TEXT` | `nvarchar(128)` | `character varying(128)` |
| `Revision` (`long`) | `INTEGER` | `bigint` | `bigint` |
| `Payload` (`byte[]?`) | `BLOB` | `varbinary(max)` | `bytea` |
| `OccurredAt` (`DateTimeOffset`) | `TEXT` | `datetimeoffset` | `timestamp with time zone` |

Npgsql spells the last one `timestamp with time zone`, not `timestamptz`; the documentation matches
what is generated. Item 1 of the sketch is confirmed exactly, including all three of the pairs it
named.

So: `Statesman.Persistence.EntityFrameworkCore.{Sqlite,SqlServer,PostgreSQL}` and
`Statesman.Outbox.EntityFrameworkCore.{Sqlite,SqlServer,PostgreSQL}`. Six, never three shared ones —
a ledger-only consumer must not pull the outbox core package to get a SQLite migration, and the two
contexts have no relationship beyond both being relational.

Each of the six is `net10.0`, references exactly its own core package plus exactly one provider
package, and adds `Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.Design` with `PrivateAssets="all"` so the design-time
assembly stays out of the consumer's closure. Each holds one `IDesignTimeDbContextFactory<TBase>`, one
generated `Initial` migration with its designer partial, one model snapshot, and one extension method.

**The two core packages' reference sets do not change.**
`SnapshotIsolationLevel`'s comment (`EntityFrameworkStateLedgerStore.cs:980-982`) states the rule and
it binds here verbatim: *"adding a reference to either server provider to read an isolation level
would push both onto every consumer."* A migration package may take one provider reference precisely
because a consumer opts into exactly one. The subclass-tolerant assembly type in item 2 adds a
**type**, not a reference: it needs only `Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.Relational`, which both core
packages already have.

One base model can be snapshotted three times because there is nothing provider-conditional in it.
Neither `OnModelCreating` branches on a provider, there is no `HasColumnType`, no `HasAnnotation`, no
`HasDefaultValueSql`, no value converter and no `ValueGeneratedOnAdd` anywhere in either context;
every column type is produced by the provider's own type mapper from the CLR type plus
`HasMaxLength`. A repository-wide search for provider branching under `src/` returns exactly one
line, `EntityFrameworkStateLedgerStore.cs:989-993`, and it runs at query time rather than at
model-building time.

#### 2. The subclass seam: a subclass-tolerant `IMigrationsAssembly` in both core packages

Both core packages gain a `MigrationsAssembly` subclass whose `Migrations` and `ModelSnapshot`
overrides relax the predicate from reference equality to
`declared.IsAssignableFrom(currentContext.Context.GetType())`, registered through
`ReplaceService<IMigrationsAssembly, …>()`. Measured, with the identical code this section
prescribes, on the same subclass that gets nothing without it:

```
subclass, replacement service: migrations=1 snapshot=found pending=[20260912000734_Initial]
                               table created=True  HasPendingModelChanges=False
base context, same options:    migrations=1 snapshot=found  table created=True  HasPendingModelChanges=False
```

**A subclass that adds to the model is rejected loudly, by Entity Framework Core rather than by us.**
With the replacement in place and the running model carrying one property the snapshot does not,
`Migrate()` throws:

```
InvalidOperationException: An error was generated for warning
'Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.Migrations.PendingModelChangesWarning': The model for context
'AppDbContext' has pending changes. Add a new migration before updating the database.
```

So the sketch's "the subclass must not add to that model" is *enforced*, which is a better outcome
than documenting it, and it is worth a test. A consumer who genuinely wants shipped tables beside
their own suppresses it with
`ConfigureWarnings(w => w.Ignore(RelationalEventId.PendingModelChangesWarning))` and owns the
consequence; that escape hatch is documented, not recommended.

`MigrationsAssembly` lives in a `…Migrations.Internal` namespace, so deriving from it emits
`EF1001`. Measured on the exact shipped shape: **three** diagnostics — the class declaration, the
base constructor call, and the inherited `Assembly` property the overrides read. `TreatWarningsAsErrors`
is on repository-wide (`Directory.Build.props:7`) with only `CS1591` in `NoWarn` (`:8`), so the type
carries a local `#pragma warning disable EF1001` with a justifying comment in the
`SnapshotIsolationLevel` block's style. Never an `EF1001` entry in a project's `NoWarn`: the
suppression must be visible at the line that earns it, and it must not silently cover a second,
unrelated internal-API use added later.

**The zero-code alternative is documented rather than taken.** `AddDbContextFactory<StatesmanLedgerDbContext>(…)`
over the **base** context works with no replacement service at all — measured — because the base
context's non-generic `DbContextOptions` constructor satisfies Entity Framework Core's constructor
binding and `UseEntityFrameworkStore<TContext>` already accepts the base type. It is the right answer
for a consumer who never wanted a subclass. It is not the shipped default, because the subclass seam
is what every existing consumer, every documentation page and every test in this repository uses, and
taking it away to make migrations work would be the tail wagging the dog.

**The extension goes on the outer `DbContextOptionsBuilder`, not on the provider's inner builder.**
This is the one place this section departs from the research's recommended shape, and it departs on a
measurement. An extension on `SqliteDbContextOptionsBuilder` (or its generic base) binds a type
parameter to `Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.Sqlite.Infrastructure.Internal.SqliteOptionsExtension`,
which is itself an internal API: the ergonomic `UseSqlite(cs, o => o.UseStatesmanLedgerMigrations())`
form compiles with an `EF1001` warning **in each of the six packages**, spreading a suppression for a
second internal type across six projects. The outer form does not:

```csharp
options.UseSqlite(connectionString).UseStatesmanLedgerSqliteMigrations();
```

It reads the already-configured `RelationalOptionsExtension` off `builder.Options`, re-adds it with
`WithMigrationsAssembly` and `WithMigrationsHistoryTableName` applied, and calls `ReplaceService`.
Every one of those is public API; the only `EF1001` in the phase stays in the two core packages, on
the two assembly types. Measured: six packages, zero warnings. It throws
`InvalidOperationException` when no relational provider has been selected yet, because the
alternative is a silently ignored call, which is the failure mode this whole item exists to remove.

#### 3. The baseline for a database created by `EnsureCreated`

Item 4 of the sketch is confirmed as a real failure, with one wording correction: what a consumer
needs is a baseline **history row**, not a baseline **migration**. No extra migration is generated.

Measured: `EnsureCreated()` then `Migrate()` →

```
SqliteException: SQLite Error 1: 'table "StatesmanHeads" already exists'.
```

Both core packages gain `BaselineAsync(DbContext, CancellationToken)`, which writes the history table
and one row per shipped migration id through `IHistoryRepository` and authors no SQL of its own:
`GetCreateIfNotExistsScript()`, then `GetInsertScript(new HistoryRow(id, productVersion))` for each
id in `IMigrationsAssembly.Migrations.Keys`. The product version is read from the **shipped
snapshot** — `ModelSnapshot.Model.GetProductVersion()`, measured as `"10.0.11"` — rather than from the
consumer's runtime, because the row should record the version the migration was generated with. None
of it opens a connection to decide anything.

*Amended (Task 2, 2026-09-11):* the shipped code records `ProductInfo.GetVersion()` — the running
Entity Framework Core version, the same source `Migrator.ApplyMigration` uses — not the snapshot's
`ProductVersion` annotation this paragraph describes. See decision 33.

*Amended (Task 2, 2026-09-11):* the create-if-not-exists script and the insert loop this paragraph
describes run in one transaction, itself run inside the execution strategy
(`context.Database.CreateExecutionStrategy().ExecuteAsync(...)`), not as an unwrapped sequence of
statements. See decision 34.

Measured after it: `pending=0`, `applied=[20260912000734_Initial]`, a subsequent `Migrate()` is a
clean no-op, the pre-existing table survives its data, and `HasPendingModelChanges()` is `False`. It
works for the base context and for a subclass once item 2's replacement is in place, and it honours a
custom history table name because the repository is resolved from the same options.

It returns `bool` — `true` when it wrote rows, `false` when migrations were already applied — and is
idempotent rather than throwing on a second call, so an application that calls it unconditionally at
startup is a supported arrangement rather than a bug. It **throws** when no shipped migration was
discovered at all, because that state means the options are misconfigured and silently writing an
empty history table would bake the misconfiguration in.

#### 4. History table names, and the opt-in contract

`__EFMigrationsHistory` is shared per database, so a consumer running their own migrations beside a
shipped set in one database needs the history table configured or the two sets interleave. Each
context's shipped set therefore gets its own name:

| Context | History table |
|---|---|
| `StatesmanLedgerDbContext` | `__StatesmanLedgerMigrationsHistory` |
| `StatesmanOutboxCursorDbContext` | `__StatesmanOutboxMigrationsHistory` |

Two names rather than one, because the two contexts are independently adoptable and a consumer may
point both at one database. Measured that the seam holds: with the name configured,
`__StatesmanLedgerMigrationsHistory` is created and `__EFMigrationsHistory` is **not**.

**Shipped migrations stay opt-in, and consumer-owns-it stays the documented default.** Item 7 of the
sketch holds unchanged. Nothing in either core package applies a migration, nothing calls
`EnsureCreated`, and a consumer who never calls `UseStatesman…Migrations()` sees exactly today's
behaviour. What changes is that the sentences describing the old arrangement as the *only*
arrangement — `StatesmanOutboxCursorDbContext.cs:12-14`, `docs/providers/index.md:44` and `:201`,
`docs/guides/outbox.md:50-51` and `:109-118` — are amended in place to describe it as *a* supported
arrangement, never deleted.

#### 5. The drift gate is a test, not a continuous-integration job

Item 5 of the sketch is over-specified and this section narrows it, on measurement.

**The check needs no database on any engine.** `Database.HasPendingModelChanges()` is a design-time
diff of the context's model against `IMigrationsAssembly.ModelSnapshot`; measured against
`Data Source=Z:\nope\nope.db` it returns a value rather than throwing. The CLI form
(`dotnet ef migrations has-pending-model-changes`) behaves identically and exits 0 clean / 1 drifted
against unreachable connection strings on all three engines. So a per-engine job buys nothing a
single-process test does not already have.

**It ships as six assertions in the two existing Entity Framework Core test projects**, which
`build-test` already runs on three operating systems and which both server jobs already run by
explicit path. Each case asserts three things, and the first two are not decoration:

```csharp
var assembly = context.GetService<IMigrationsAssembly>();
Assert.NotEmpty(assembly.Migrations);                    // the gate cannot pass vacuously
Assert.NotNull(assembly.ModelSnapshot);
Assert.False(context.Database.HasPendingModelChanges());
```

Without the first two, deleting the `MigrationsAssembly` configuration turns a drift failure into a
different and misleading failure, and a missing snapshot makes `HasPendingModelChanges()` return
`True` for a reason unrelated to drift — measured, and the precise trap item 5 would have walked into.

**What the server jobs gain instead is the test a drift check cannot be**: a live `Migrate()` that
creates the throwaway database from the shipped migration rather than from `EnsureCreated`, then runs
a round trip through the store. That is what proves the generated DDL is valid on a real engine. It
is gated on the existing `EntityFrameworkTestDatabase.SelectedEngine` seam, so it runs on SQLite
locally and on SQL Server and PostgreSQL in `sqlserver-tests` and `postgres-tests` **with no workflow
edit at all** — both projects are already in both jobs' test lists (`ci.yml:134-139`, `:185-190`).

**No new test project**, for that reason: a new one would have to be added to those two lists, which
is a workflow edit, and would duplicate the fixture both existing projects already link.

**A tool manifest ships anyway.** `dotnet ef` is not in the gate, but it is how a maintainer *authors*
the next migration, and the repository has no `.config/dotnet-tools.json` at all today — the globally
installed tool on the authoring machine is `10.0.7` against a `10.0.11` runtime and warns on every
invocation. A manifest pinning `dotnet-ef` `10.0.11` makes `dotnet tool restore && dotnet ef
migrations add …` reproducible and removes the warning. Measured: with the manifest present both
`dotnet ef` and `dotnet tool run dotnet-ef` resolve to `10.0.11`.

#### 6. The versioning policy

Item 6 of the sketch is confirmed: `version.json:3` versions all packages together at
`0.4.0-alpha.{height}` through Nerdbank.GitVersioning, and nothing in this repository versions a
migration. The policy, which lives on the new documentation page and is the thing a later phase will
be measured against:

- **Append-only.** A shipped migration id is a permanent public contract. Ids are never renamed,
  never removed, and never regenerated — a consumer's `__Statesman…MigrationsHistory` table holds
  them, and changing one strands every database that applied it.
- **Every model change adds a migration to all six packages**, each with a working `Down`. Six, not
  one: the engines generate different SQL and the drift gate fails per package, so a change that lands
  in five is a broken sixth.
- **The six ids never line up.** A migration id is a UTC timestamp taken at generation; the three
  engines generated three different ids within one minute of each other in the same session. Each
  database only ever sees one package's migrations, so this is harmless — but no document may imply a
  shared id, and no code may derive one package's id from another's.
- **A `ProductVersion` move is a committed diff.** `modelBuilder.HasAnnotation("ProductVersion", …)`
  appears in both the designer and the snapshot and tracks the Entity Framework Core version at
  generation time, so an Entity Framework Core bump that changes generated output surfaces as a
  snapshot diff that must be reviewed and committed rather than regenerated silently.
- **Generated files are committed verbatim.** What `dotnet ef` writes is what ships, with the exact
  authoring command recorded, so the next maintainer regenerates rather than hand-edits. Measured
  against `eng/validate.py`: generated files end with a newline, carry no trailing whitespace, and
  contain no `TODO`/`FIXME`/`NotImplementedException`; the UTF-8 byte-order mark they carry is inert
  for every check the validator runs, and `.gitattributes:1-2` normalizes their line endings.

#### 7. Pre-Phase-12 addendum decisions 9 and 12 are **not** re-opened

Both rested partly on the premise that the affected packages ship no migrations, and Phase 14 removes
that premise. Stating the ruling here so a later reviewer finds it rather than reading the old
rationale as stale and re-litigating it.

- **Decision 9, SQL Server's 900-byte clustered key limit.** Never in the code: it is documented at
  `docs/providers/index.md:201` and boundary-tested by
  `tests/Statesman.EntityFrameworkCore.Tests/EntityFrameworkSqlServerKeyLimitTests.cs`, and the
  decision explicitly rejected a `HasMaxLength` reduction. The shipped SQL Server migration emits the
  same 1792-byte-maximum key `EnsureCreated` emits today and SQL Server emits the same warning it
  already emits. **Not re-opened**: a migration makes a schema change *possible*, not *free*, and the
  decision rests on "no realistic address reaches it", which Phase 14 does not change. One sentence on
  the new documentation page tells an operator who first sees that warning in migration output where
  its explanation lives.
- **Decision 12, PostgreSQL microsecond `timestamptz` truncation.** The decision rejected a `long`
  ticks column as *"a schema change to a package that ships no migrations"*. **Not re-opened**: the
  decision's load-bearing half is "nothing in this library compares those values below microsecond",
  which is unchanged, and a ticks column would be a breaking storage-format change to every existing
  PostgreSQL deployment for a difference nothing reads.

Neither is a Phase 14 scope item. A future phase that wants either must re-argue it on its own merits
and ship a second migration in all six packages.

#### 8. Close-out

`CHANGELOG.md` `### Added` for the six packages in the `Statesman.Outbox.EntityFrameworkCore` entry's
shape (`:28-40`) and `### Changed` for the new public types on the two shipped core packages; a
`HANDOVER.md` entry in the Phase 13 entry's shape — commits, per-task review outcomes, the mechanisms,
and what was parked and why, with **every number copied from a task report rather than from a brief's
prediction**; `ROADMAP.md:34` marked shipped; and the exit criteria below amended from predictions to
measured counts.

#### Explicitly parked, with reasons

- **Re-opening pre-Phase-12 addendum decisions 9 and 12.** Item 7 above is the ruling and the
  reasoning. Shipping a migration removes one argument for each; neither decision rested only on that
  argument.
- **A second migration of any kind.** Phase 14 ships exactly one `Initial` per package. The model
  does not change this phase, so there is nothing a second migration could carry, and inventing one
  to exercise the upgrade path would pin a migration id forever to make a test look thorough.
- **Sample projects using Entity Framework Core.** No sample references either core package today
  (`samples/Statesman.Sample.Migration` is *state* migration, `MigrateFrom`, and shares only a name),
  and adding one would put a database dependency into the sample build for documentation value the
  new providers page already delivers.
- **A public-API baseline gate.** `Directory.Build.props:12` *(corrected from :21)* sets `EnablePackageValidation`, and a
  brand-new package has no baseline to validate against on its first publish, so nothing here is
  gated. `ROADMAP.md:36` still carries the real item; six new packages do not change its shape.
- **Automating migration authoring in continuous integration.** The tool manifest makes authoring
  reproducible on a maintainer's machine; nothing generates a migration in a workflow. A generated
  migration is reviewed code, and a job that writes one would be a job that commits unreviewed schema.
- **Dependabot's grouping gap for `Npgsql.EntityFrameworkCore.PostgreSQL`.** It matches only the
  catch-all `major-updates` group at `.github/dependabot.yml:31-35`, never the `dotnet-platform`
  group's `Microsoft.*` pattern. Pre-existing, true today, and unrelated to whether `src/` references
  it. Not Phase 14's to fix.
- **Changing the model to suit a generated migration.** The generated SQL Server key is what it is
  because the model is what it is. A migration package that silently narrowed a column to make its own
  DDL nicer would be a storage-format change wearing a packaging change's clothes.

#### The public API delta

Everything here is additive. No existing signature changes, no storage format changes, no capability
changes: `docs/architecture/capabilities.md` gains no row and flips no cell, and
`tests/Statesman.Capabilities.Tests/CapabilityMatrixTests.cs` is untouched, because a migration
package contains no store.

**`Statesman.Persistence.EntityFrameworkCore` (shipped package) gains two public types:**

- `Statesman.StatesmanMigrationsAssembly` — the subclass-tolerant `IMigrationsAssembly`, registered
  by the extension below rather than by hand.
- `Statesman.StatesmanLedgerMigrations`, a static class holding
  `const string HistoryTableName`;
  `static DbContextOptionsBuilder UseStatesmanLedgerMigrations(this DbContextOptionsBuilder, Assembly)`;
  and `static Task<bool> BaselineAsync(DbContext, CancellationToken = default)`.

**`Statesman.Outbox.EntityFrameworkCore` (shipped package) gains the same two**, deliberately
duplicated rather than shared: `Statesman.Outbox.EntityFrameworkCore.StatesmanOutboxMigrationsAssembly`
and `Statesman.Outbox.EntityFrameworkCore.StatesmanOutboxMigrations` with the same three members. The
two core packages share no assembly and neither may reference the other — that separation is the
whole reason `StatesmanOutboxCursorDbContext` exists — so sixty duplicated lines are the cost of not
creating a third shared package that both would have to take.

**Six new packages**, each `net10.0`, each exposing exactly one public extension method on
`DbContextOptionsBuilder` plus one public `IDesignTimeDbContextFactory<T>`:

| Package | Extension method |
|---|---|
| `Statesman.Persistence.EntityFrameworkCore.Sqlite` | `UseStatesmanLedgerSqliteMigrations()` |
| `Statesman.Persistence.EntityFrameworkCore.SqlServer` | `UseStatesmanLedgerSqlServerMigrations()` |
| `Statesman.Persistence.EntityFrameworkCore.PostgreSQL` | `UseStatesmanLedgerPostgreSqlMigrations()` |
| `Statesman.Outbox.EntityFrameworkCore.Sqlite` | `UseStatesmanOutboxSqliteMigrations()` |
| `Statesman.Outbox.EntityFrameworkCore.SqlServer` | `UseStatesmanOutboxSqlServerMigrations()` |
| `Statesman.Outbox.EntityFrameworkCore.PostgreSQL` | `UseStatesmanOutboxPostgreSqlMigrations()` |

The generated `Initial` migration classes are public; the model snapshots are internal, and only their
`[DbContext]` stamp and their content are contractual. *Amended (final fix wave, 2026-09-11):* the
snapshots' visibility was originally stated as public too, which is wrong — each package's
`…ModelSnapshot` is declared `partial class …ModelSnapshot : ModelSnapshot` with no access modifier, by
Entity Framework Core's own generator convention, so it is internal in all six packages. Final review,
Minor 3.
