#### Explicitly parked, with reasons

- **ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 5 — filesystem verify, repair and index-rebuild tooling. This is the first
  Phase 18 item.** It was measured for this phase and does not fit the budget: items 1 to 3 plus the
  close-out already come to nine tasks of Phase 16 size, and a verify surface, a repair surface, their
  conformance coverage and an operator page is two to three more. The measured layout is written down
  here so Phase 18's planner starts from it: stream directories at `<root>/<hash[..2]>/<hash>`
  (`FileSystemStateLedgerStore.cs:950`), a `head.json` (`:955`), `history/<revision:D20>.json` files
  (`:953`, `:958`), an append-only newline-delimited `_changes.log` (`:960`), a `_changes.gen`
  generation counter (`:962`), and a change-log index that is **in-process only** and never persisted
  (`:75-118`), invalidated by file length, by a byte-compare of the last consumed line, and by the
  generation. The corruptible states a verify pass would report: an unterminated trailing fragment of
  `_changes.log` (which compaction deliberately preserves, `:544-551`), a log line that dereferences to
  no history file, a log line whose history file carries a different `GlobalPosition`, a history file
  with no head, a head whose revision has no history file, and a generation that moved under a live
  reader. `CompactChangeLogAsync` (`:455`, `:464`) plus `ChangeLogCompactionResult` (`:30-52`) is the
  shape the pair should follow: a report-only default, a `dryRun` overload, and a record of counts.
- **A store-format upgrade or restore-from-corruption runbook**, and with it the 0.2 exit criterion
  "upgrade and corruption scenarios are documented". Parked **with** bullet 5, deliberately: the
  filesystem operator page is the substantial half of that runbook, and writing a cross-provider page
  without it would document a recovery procedure for the one provider that has no recovery tooling.
- **ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 4 — serializer envelopes.** A storage-format change; its own design pass. Parked
  identically in Phases 15 and 16.
- **ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 6 — Redis Cluster coverage and the `HGETALL` to `HashScanAsync` conversion.**
  Needs a fourth infrastructure job. Parked identically in Phases 15 and 16.
- **ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 8 — analyzer code fixes.** A new surface with its own design pass. Parked
  identically in Phases 15 and 16.
- **Retention semantics (`MaxAge`/`MaxRevisions`/`MaxBytes` pruning uniformity) in the shared
  conformance suite**, bullet 1's remainder. Not measured as a one-task lift: pruning uniformity is a
  per-provider question about eviction order and byte accounting, not a contract every provider is
  already known to share, so it needs its own pre-flight probe of the kind Phase 16 ran before writing
  each suite.
- **Provider-specific corruption tests are still not lifted.** Unchanged from Phase 16 decision 63.
- **A configurable load-report bound or a configurable rate limit.** Both ship as public constants,
  matching how Phase 15 shipped the 64 maintenance-failure bound and Phase 16 the rate limit. Making
  either configurable needs a runtime options object `StatesmanDeclaration.CreateRuntime` does not have.
- **A whole-load duration histogram.** `statesman.operation.duration` with operation `refresh` already
  measures it; a second instrument over the same interval would be two names for one number.
- **`MessageId` collision from ordinal `Root` versus canonical lower-casing**, **cross-process
  filesystem append locking**, **Redis concurrent same-revision `ImportAsync` beyond the documented
  sentence**, **pipelining the Redis sink**, **an HTTP webhook sink**, and **lifting Redis's
  `MaxImportablePosition`.** All 0.4 or storage-format items, each re-ruled at least four times.
- **The `RequireAll` histogram gap.** A load that throws outright records no per-source timing on any
  channel, in the one mode (`RequireAll`) an operator chooses precisely because every source matters.
  The final review's measured, cheap shape if this is judged worth closing: `sourceReports` is already
  fully populated at each throw site, so attaching it to the thrown `AggregateException` (via `Data`, or
  a small internal exception type) would let `StateHandle.RefreshAsync`'s existing `catch` record the
  histogram measurements without threading a sink parameter through `LoadAsync`'s signature — decision
  74's amendment records the same finding.
- **Empty-`Sources` and multi-root health-check cases.** No test exercises a `seeded`/`retained` report
  (empty `Sources`) reaching `StatesmanHealthCheck`, nor a registry with more than one root contributing
  reports simultaneously. Both are straight-line code with no branch the final review's levers could not
  already reach, so this is a coverage gap rather than a suspected defect.
- **`eng/validate.py`'s `all_files` metric counts untracked local files**, so it cannot be reconciled
  against what is actually committed — two agents on different machines get different numbers for the
  same commit (measured: 13 untracked local files on the final reviewer's machine at Phase 17's close).
  Phase 18's plan should either reconcile `git ls-files` instead of a directory walk, or state plainly
  that `all_files` is expected only to move monotonically, not to match a predicted value exactly.

#### The public API delta

**Additive only. No breaking change, and no capability change.**

New in `Statesman.Abstractions`: `StateSourceLoadStatus`, `StateSourceLoadReport`, `StateLoadReport`,
`LoadDiagnostics`; two new members on `IStatesmanDiagnostics` (`ReadLoadDiagnostics`,
`ClearLoadDiagnostics`), measured free against the `0.3.0` baseline because the interface post-dates the
tag; one new constant, `StatesmanDiagnostics.MaxRetainedLoadReports`. New in `Statesman`: the meter
instrument `statesman.load.source.duration`, which is a metric name rather than a type. Nothing changes
signature, so no `CompatibilitySuppressions.xml` file is added or edited and
`docs/reference/api-compatibility.md` needs only a confirming sentence.

`StateCapabilityExtensions.TryGetCapability<TCapability>` and
`TieredStateLedgerStore.TryGetCapability(Type, out object?)` change **behaviour** without changing
shape, which the package-validation gate does not and cannot catch — which is exactly why the change is
recorded here, in `CHANGELOG.md` under `### Changed`, and in `docs/architecture/capabilities.md`'s
prose.

**No capability is added, so `docs/architecture/capabilities.md` gains no row and flips no cell, and
`tests/Statesman.Capabilities.Tests/CapabilityMatrixTests.cs` is untouched** — the Phase 10 through 16
precedent, now measured rather than assumed for a phase that changes discovery itself.

### Phase 18 — Filesystem recovery tooling and the corruption runbook (2026-09-13)

Phase 17 shipped ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 2 and parked bullet 5 — "filesystem recovery tooling" — with a
measured layout inventory written into its own parked list so this phase's planner would start from an
inventory rather than a re-measurement. This phase takes that bullet, writes the cross-provider
restore-from-corruption runbook that was deliberately parked **with** it, and sweeps the four
carry-forwards Phase 17's final review left named.

**What the pre-flight measurement changed about the plan.** Every claim in the controller's brief and
every line citation in Phase 17's parked list was treated as a hypothesis and probed at `e22dad8` on a
git-clean tree. Ten were wrong or open, and four of those changed the design rather than a number:

- **One of the six corruptible states Phase 17 recorded is not a state a verify pass can report.** "A
  generation that moved under a live reader" is an in-flight race between a compactor and a reader,
  already closed by the seqlock at `FileSystemStateLedgerStore.cs:1357-1431`. Nothing a verify pass can
  open and inspect distinguishes a store whose generation moved from one whose generation never moved:
  the durable artifact — `_changes.gen` holding a higher number — is the normal, healthy result of every
  compaction. It is dropped from the finding set. The other five survive, and four more were measured
  reachable and worse than several of them.
- **A stream with history files but no head is silent data loss on backup, not a cosmetic anomaly.**
  `ListPartitionsAsync` gates each candidate on `File.Exists(HeadFile(address))` (`:820-824`) — decision
  46's deliberate "this address was committed at least once" test — so a headless stream vanishes from
  the partition catalog, and `Statesman.Tooling` enumerates exactly that catalog when it exports. The
  stream's records are all still on disk and all still readable by address, because
  `ReadLatestUnsafeAsync` (`:850-870`) already prefers a newer history file over a stale head. An
  operator taking a backup would never know. This is why repair **writes a head** rather than only
  reporting a missing one.
- **The verify/repair surface goes on `FileSystemStateLedgerStore`, and the measurement is decisive
  rather than stylistic.** `Statesman.Tooling.csproj` references only `Statesman.Abstractions`, the
  repository holds **zero** `[InternalsVisibleTo]` attributes, and verify and repair need nine private
  members of the provider (`StreamDirectory`, `HistoryFile`, `HeadFile`, `ChangeFeedFile`,
  `TryParseChangeFeedLine`, `ReadFileAsync`, `AtomicWriteAsync`, `_changeFeedGate`,
  `ResetChangeFeedIndexUnsafe`). Putting them in `Statesman.Tooling` means either a provider dependency
  in the portable package or nine new public members that exist only to be consumed once.
- **"Index rebuild" is neither a no-op nor a new method.** The rebuild trigger already ships in two
  halves — `ResetChangeFeedIndexUnsafe()` for this process and the `_changes.gen` bump for every other
  one — and `CompactChangeLogUnsafeAsync` already performs both in the right order. What the
  measurement *did* find is a reachable gap the phase closes by construction rather than by a new
  surface: the index's length-plus-tail invalidation cannot see an out-of-band rewrite that preserves
  both the file length and the last line's bytes, which is exactly the shape of a hand-repair. Repair
  therefore rewrites the log only through the provider's own generation-bumping path.

Three further measurements settled questions the brief left open. Adding a public record and a public
method to `FileSystemStateLedgerStore` is free against the `0.3.0` baseline, proved with a positive
control after two invalid attempts (see "Testing", below). No Redis, SQL Server or PostgreSQL code is
touched by any task. And the `RequireAll` histogram gap closes through `AggregateException.Data` rather
than an exception subclass, because `StateHandle.RecordFaultAsync` (`:617-620`) stamps
`exception.GetType().FullName` into the fault snapshot's `StateError.Type` — a subclass would silently
change a persisted, operator-facing ledger field on every `RequireAll` fault.

#### 1. Verify: what a report-only pass over a store directory finds

`VerifyAsync` walks `<root>` and `<root>/_changes.log` and writes nothing at all. It returns a
`FileSystemLedgerVerificationReport`: counts (`StreamsScanned`, `RecordFilesScanned`,
`ChangeLogLinesScanned`, `FindingCount`) and an ordered `IReadOnlyList<FileSystemLedgerFinding>`, each
carrying a `FileSystemLedgerFindingKind`, the absolute path it concerns, an optional `StateAddress`,
an optional 1-based change-log line number, and a human-readable `Detail`. It follows
`ChangeLogCompactionResult`'s shape (`:30-52`) — a typed record of counts, a report-only default, no
side effects — because that is the pair an operator already knows.

The nine finding kinds, with the crash-durable shape each has on disk and what a reader does about it
today:

| Kind | On disk | Reader behaviour today |
|---|---|---|
| `TornChangeLogTail` | `_changes.log` does not end in `\n` | the final unparseable line is skipped silently; compaction copies it through verbatim (`:541-551`) |
| `DanglingChangeLogLine` | a line whose `HistoryFile(address, revision)` is absent | skipped silently, as documented |
| `ChangeLogPositionMismatch` | a line whose history file exists but carries a different `GlobalPosition` | the record yields **twice**, one envelope's `Cursor` disagreeing with its own `Record.GlobalPosition` |
| `MalformedChangeLogLine` | any **non-final** line that is not five tab-separated fields with parseable position and revision | `ReadAsync` and `ListPartitionsAsync` throw `InvalidDataException` naming the 1-based line (`:994-997`) |
| `MissingHead` | `history/*.json` present, `head.json` absent | reads by address still work; the address vanishes from `ListPartitionsAsync`, and therefore from every export |
| `MissingHistoryFile` | `head.json` at revision R, `history/<R:D20>.json` absent | the head still answers; the log line for R becomes a `DanglingChangeLogLine` |
| `UnreadableRecordFile` | a `.json` whose whole contents are not a deserializable record | an **uncapped** `ReadAsync` throws `JsonException` for the entire feed; `ReadHistoryAsync` throws for that address |
| `OrphanedTemporaryFile` | `<file>.<32 hex>.tmp` left by a crash between a temp write and its rename | invisible to every read path |
| `MisplacedStreamDirectory` | a stream directory whose name is not the SHA-256 of the address its own records carry | every read by address misses it; only a directory walk finds it |

`MissingHistoryFile` is genuinely anomalous rather than a normal retention outcome, which is what makes
restoring it safe: `PruneAsync` provably cannot delete the latest revision's history file —
`records.Count <= 1` returns early (`:372-375`), the `MaxAge` and tombstone filters both exempt
`Revision == latest` (`:381`, `:387`), `MaxRevisions` keeps the tail of a revision-ascending list
(`:391-394`), and the `MaxBytes` loop iterates `OrderByDescending(revision)` and admits its first entry
unconditionally (`:400-410`).

#### 2. Repair: what it is allowed to do to each of them

The governing rule is that **repair never destroys data verify could not prove orphaned**, and the
finding set splits cleanly on it. Only two kinds are provably orphaned, and both were already
`CompactChangeLogAsync`'s job.

| Kind | Repair action | Why |
|---|---|---|
| `DanglingChangeLogLine` | drop the line, by delegating to `CompactChangeLogAsync` | provably dereferences to nothing |
| `ChangeLogPositionMismatch` | drop the line, same delegation | provably stale; the history file is authoritative |
| `MissingHead` | **write** `head.json` from the highest-revision history file | creates a file, destroys none; it is the value `ReadLatestUnsafeAsync` already computes at read time |
| `MissingHistoryFile` | **write** `history/<R:D20>.json` from the head record | same: the head *is* the record, written back where the invariant says it belongs |
| `UnreadableRecordFile` | **quarantine** by renaming to `<file>.corrupt` (`.corrupt.<n>` on collision) | takes the file out of `*.json` enumeration, which unbricks an uncapped feed read, while the bytes stay on disk for an operator |
| `TornChangeLogTail` | **nothing** | it is the crash marker; the next append turns it into a loud, reportable malformed line, and erasing it would erase the evidence — the same reading compaction already takes |
| `MalformedChangeLogLine` | **nothing**, and the whole change-log half is skipped | a malformed line may name a record that exists; dropping it loses feed positions silently, which is the exact outcome `CorruptChangeFeedLine` exists to prevent |
| `OrphanedTemporaryFile` | **nothing** | a temp is indistinguishable from a live in-flight write without a cross-process lock this provider does not have; `FileSystemChangeLogCompactionTests.cs:225-231` already pins that compaction leaves a temp it did not create |
| `MisplacedStreamDirectory` | **nothing** | moving a directory is the destructive move that cannot be proven safe |

Two ordering rules make the table consistent. **Record-file repairs run before the change-log
compaction**, so a `DanglingChangeLogLine` whose history file this same pass just restored is healed
rather than dropped. And **a `MalformedChangeLogLine` anywhere suppresses the change-log half
entirely** while the record-file half still runs, because `CompactChangeLogAsync` throws on that line
rather than skipping it — the repair report says so in a dedicated field instead of surfacing the
exception.

`RepairAsync(CancellationToken)` is a **dry run**; `RepairAsync(bool dryRun, CancellationToken)` is the
explicit form. This is deliberately the opposite default from `CompactChangeLogAsync()`, whose
parameterless overload applies, and the doc comment says so in words.

#### 3. Index rebuild, ruled rather than built

There is no persisted index, so there is nothing to rebuild on disk — but "documented no-op" would be
the wrong answer, because a repair that rewrites `_changes.log` genuinely must invalidate three things:
this store's index, every other store instance's index in this process, and every reader's index in
every other process. All three already have a mechanism. `ResetChangeFeedIndexUnsafe()` (`:1639`)
handles the first two under `_changeFeedGate`; the `_changes.gen` bump, taken **before** the rename
(`:1078-1082`), handles the third. Repair inherits both by routing its only log rewrite through
`CompactChangeLogAsync`. No new public member, and the alternative — a public `RebuildIndexAsync` —
would be dead weight against a rule that already fires.

#### 4. The runbook

`docs/operations/recovery.md` is the cross-provider operator page that closes 0.2's "upgrade and
corruption scenarios are documented" exit criterion. The filesystem section is the substantial half —
the layout, what each finding means, the verify-then-dry-run-then-repair sequence, what repair will not
touch and what the operator must do by hand for those — and each other provider gets what it actually
offers: Entity Framework Core migrations plus the baseline history row
(`docs/providers/entity-framework-core-migrations.md`), `Statesman.Tooling` export and
fingerprint-validated exact restore (`docs/guides/backup-restore.md`), Redis's import guard and its
`MaxImportablePosition` refusal, and change-log compaction. The page links to the existing semantics
text in `docs/providers/index.md:179-204` rather than restating it. `docs/toc.yml`, `README.md` and
`docs/providers/index.md` gain links; `docs/reference/public-api.md` does not, because it is a curated
map with no provider-specific surface at all.

#### 5. The `RequireAll` histogram gap

`sourceReports` is already fully populated at all four throw sites in `RuntimeDefinition.LoadAsync`
(`:209`, `:232`, `:269`, `:279`), and `StateHandle`'s per-source histogram loop (`:322-327`) runs only
after `LoadAsync` returns, which a throw never does. The reports are attached to the thrown
`AggregateException` through `Data` under an internal const key, and `StateHandle.RefreshAsync`'s
existing catch (`:353`) records `statesman.load.source.duration` from them before writing the fault
snapshot. Nothing public changes and no signature moves.

**A throwing load still records no `StateLoadReport`** — decision 74 stands, now with the reason
measured rather than asserted. `StateLoadReport.Completeness` carries five documented values and
`StatesmanHealthCheck.IsIncomplete` (`:139-140`) classifies exactly those; a sixth value for "threw"
would need a fourth health-status ruling and would change what every existing consumer of
`Completeness` sees. Only the histogram half was ever the cheap shape the amendment named.

#### 6. The health-check and diagnostics sweep

Three coverage gaps and one ruling. No test constructs a declaration with **no** `Load` sources reaching
`StatesmanHealthCheck`, which is the shape that leaves `statesman.load.slowest.source` at
`string.Empty`; none registers **more than one root** contributing reports through the `_registry.All`
accumulation (`:61`); and `CheckHealthAsync_never_drains_the_diagnostics_surface` (`:63-86`) asserts
only `ReadMaintenanceFailures`, though the check calls `ReadLoadDiagnostics()` too (`:77`). The Degraded
condition is `retained > 0 || degraded.Count > 0 || incompleteLoads > 0` — **three operands, three
break-the-mechanism levers**, which is the Phase 17 final-review lesson applied rather than restated.

The ruling: **none of the four `IStatesmanDiagnostics` members throws after disposal**, and a test pins
it with one lever per member. Decision 69's amendment already said a change must move all four
together; the measurement that makes it more than a consistency preference is that
`StatesmanHealthCheck.CheckHealthAsync` calls two of them on every registered runtime with no disposal
check of its own, so a throwing surface would turn a health probe over a registry holding a disposed
root into an exception rather than a report.

#### 7. The deferred minors

Three `<c>` uses on `LoadDiagnostics.cs` name types or members rather than literals and become
`<see cref>`: `RequireAll` and `BestEffort` (`:47-48`), which are `StateSourceFailureMode` members
declared in the same assembly at `Policies.cs:45`, and `StateError` (`:51`). The rest are metadata
keys, instrument names and `Completeness` string values and correctly stay `<c>`. The decision-68
rationale stated twice — `LoadDiagnostics.cs:17-22` and `RuntimeDefinition.cs:402-406` — keeps the
public doc comment as canonical and turns the internal one into a pointer.

#### 8. `eng/validate.py`'s `all_files`

Phase 17's parked note said `all_files` "counts untracked local files". The count was right and the
word was wrong: this machine has **zero** untracked files and still shows a 13-file gap against
`git ls-files` (443 against 430). The thirteen are **git-ignored** local tool artefacts — `.claude/`,
`.idea/`, `Statesman.sln.DotSettings.user`, `TestResults/` — which `git status` never showed precisely
because it honours `.gitignore`, and which `_ignored` (`:371-375`) does not cover.

The validator gains a `tracked_files` metric read from `git ls-files`, which is exact and
machine-independent, and the report gains a sentence saying `all_files` is expected to move
monotonically rather than to match a predicted value. Adding a metric rather than redefining
`all_files` keeps every historical number in this spec and in eight phases of task reports meaningful.
When `git` is unavailable — a source archive with no `.git` — `tracked_files` is omitted rather than
guessed, and the validator stays offline and dependency-free in every other respect.

#### Exit criteria

1. Every test project `failed: 0` at the final commit, on SQLite and with live Redis, and the four
   Entity Framework Core-affected projects also on SQL Server and PostgreSQL, plain and under
   `STATESMAN_TEST_EF_RETRY=1`.

   *Amended (Task 9, 2026-09-13):* measured at the final commit (`eefcb86`). All fifteen test projects
   ran `failed: 0` at defaults on SQLite (`Statesman.FileSystem.Tests` `total: 80`, `Statesman.Tests`

#### Explicitly parked, with reasons

- **A cross-process filesystem append lock, and with it multi-process writers.** Unchanged from Phases
  11 through 17. The provider's writer side is single-process because `_globalPosition` lives in memory
  and is never seeded from the existing log; a lock would close a byte-interleaving symptom while the
  position allocator the feed's guarantee actually depends on stayed single-process. Verify and repair
  inherit that premise and say so rather than pretending to be safe against a concurrent writer.
- **Repairing a `MisplacedStreamDirectory` by moving it.** Verify reports it; repair will not move a
  directory it cannot prove nothing else owns. An operator with the writer stopped can move it by hand,
  and the runbook says how.
- **Deleting an `OrphanedTemporaryFile`.** Same reason, one level down: a `.tmp` is byte-identical
  whether it is crash residue or a live in-flight write, and the provider has no lock that would tell
  them apart. Reported with its full path so an operator with the writer stopped can delete it.
- **A persisted change-log index.** It would make verify able to check an index against the log, which
  is a storage-format change with its own design pass, and would buy a cross-process reader a first
  parse it currently pays once per process.
- **Rewriting a `MalformedChangeLogLine`, or recovering the record it may name.** The line's fields are
  by definition unparseable, so any recovery is a guess; the runbook documents the manual procedure
  (stop the writer, take a copy, truncate at the line, re-run verify) instead.
- **Retention semantics (`MaxAge`/`MaxRevisions`/`MaxBytes` pruning uniformity) in the shared
  conformance suite**, bullet 1's remainder. Unchanged from Phases 16 and 17: pruning uniformity is a
  per-provider question about eviction order and byte accounting, and needs its own pre-flight probe.
- **Lifting verify and repair into the shared conformance suite.** Unchanged from Phase 16 decision 63
  and Phase 17: the other four providers have no textual structure a crash can leave half-written, so a
  shared suite would have to invent a damage mechanism for them.
- **ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 4 — serializer envelopes.** A storage-format change; its own design pass. Parked
  identically in Phases 15, 16 and 17.
- **ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 6 — Redis Cluster coverage and the `HGETALL` to `HashScanAsync` conversion.**
  Needs a fourth infrastructure job. Parked identically in Phases 15, 16 and 17.
- **ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 8 — analyzer code fixes.** A new surface with its own design pass. Parked
  identically in Phases 15, 16 and 17.
- **Making `_ignored` in `eng/validate.py` cover `.idea`, `.claude`, `TestResults` and `*.user`.** The
  rejected alternative to `tracked_files`: a hand-maintained list of other people's tool directories
  that the next tool breaks, where `git ls-files` is already the exact answer and is already how the
  repository decides what a file is.
- **`MessageId` collision from ordinal `Root` versus canonical lower-casing**, **Redis concurrent
  same-revision `ImportAsync` beyond the documented sentence**, **pipelining the Redis sink**, **an HTTP
  webhook sink**, and **lifting Redis's `MaxImportablePosition`.** All 0.4 or storage-format items, each
  re-ruled at least five times.

#### The public API delta

**Additive only. No breaking change, and no capability change.**

New in `Statesman.Persistence.FileSystem`: `FileSystemLedgerFindingKind` (an enum),
`FileSystemLedgerFinding`, `FileSystemLedgerVerificationReport`, `FileSystemLedgerRepairReport`, and
three methods on `FileSystemStateLedgerStore` — `VerifyAsync(CancellationToken)`,
`RepairAsync(CancellationToken)` and `RepairAsync(bool, CancellationToken)`. Measured free against the
`0.3.0` baseline with a positive control that produced six `CP0002` errors plus
`error : API breaking changes found`, so no `CompatibilitySuppressions.xml` is added or edited and the
three existing entries in that package's file — all for the Phase 10 `ReadAsync` options break — are
untouched.

Nothing changes in `Statesman.Abstractions`, `Statesman` or `Statesman.Extensions.Hosting` except
documentation comments and internal members: the `RequireAll` histogram fix adds an `internal const
string` key and an `AggregateException.Data` entry, which the package-validation gate correctly cannot
see and which no consumer can observe, because the thrown exception's **type** is deliberately
unchanged for exactly that reason.

**No capability is added, so `docs/architecture/capabilities.md` gains no row and flips no cell, and
`tests/Statesman.Capabilities.Tests/CapabilityMatrixTests.cs` is untouched** — the Phase 10 through 17
precedent. Verify and repair are provider maintenance methods, not capabilities: they are reachable
only on the concrete `FileSystemStateLedgerStore`, exactly as `CompactChangeLogAsync` has been since
Phase 12, because no other provider has a structure a crash can leave half-written.

### Phase 19 — Retention conformance, the lease clock, and the Phase 18 sweep (2026-09-14)

Phases 16, 17 and 18 each parked ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 1's remainder — retention semantics in the shared
conformance suite — on one sentence, repeated verbatim three times: "pruning uniformity is a
per-provider question about eviction order and byte accounting, not a contract every provider is
already known to share", and so it "needs its own pre-flight probe of the kind Phase 16 ran before
writing each shared suite". Phase 19 ran that probe, and **the parked reason is measurably false.**
Eighteen scenarios against all five built-in providers, on one injected clock and against live Redis,
produced **zero disagreements**: the surviving revision set, the head, the change feed and the
partition catalog were identical in every cell of an eighteen-by-five matrix, so the matrix collapses
to a single column. The four base providers run the same six filters in the same order — validate,
early-return at one record, the age cutoff, the tombstone filter, `MaxRevisions`, then the byte walk —
and the tiered store prunes cold then hot with both agreeing. This stopped being a research problem and
became a transcription problem. This phase transcribes it, fixes the one measured flake in the lease
conformance suite, and sweeps the carry-forwards Phase 18's close-out named.

**What the pre-flight measurement changed.** Every claim in the controller's brief and every deferred
minor Phase 18 recorded was treated as a hypothesis and probed at `5a8eec1` in a scratch worktree.

- **The premise the spec parked this item on three times is refuted, and the hole is bigger than the
  premise admitted.** Not only do the providers agree; **no test anywhere in the repository asserts
  what `MaxAge` or `MaxBytes` pruning actually does.** Exhaustive grep: those two options appear in
  `tests/` only where `Validate()` throws and where a builder stores the value. Exactly two tests in
  the whole repository assert a surviving revision set after a prune, both for `MaxRevisions`
  (`ChangeFeedConformanceTests.Retention_removes_a_pruned_revision_from_the_feed` and
  `FileSystemPartitionCatalogTests.Pruning_every_older_revision_keeps_the_partition_listed`). "The
  suite is not shared" was never the real gap.
- **The Redis lease flake's true cause is the successor's TTL, not the pre-successor wait.** The brief
  offered a polling `ExpireAsync` that watches the key disappear instead of sleeping 400 ms. Measured:
  that cannot close the flake, because the failing assertion is that the **successor's** lease is still
  held two Redis round trips later — the stale holder's release script, then the renew script — and
  shortening the wait *before* the successor acquires widens nothing after it. What closes it is
  splitting the suite's single TTL into a long hold TTL and a short expiring TTL, which is the
  asymmetry `tests/Statesman.Redis.Tests/RedisLeaseProviderTests.cs` has always had and the conformance
  suite lost when it collapsed both acquisitions onto one constant. Proved with an injected stall: at
  250 ms the shipped shape fails **ten runs out of ten**, and at 1000 ms the split shape passes ten out
  of ten.
- **Two of Phase 18's seven deferred minors were already fixed by Phase 18's own final fix wave, and a
  third was never a repository defect at all.** M-e — `FileSystemLedgerRepairReport`'s "left exactly
  alone" remark being optimistic for the healed-dangling-line case — was resolved by I1 in `3efdfc0`,
  the commit the handover entry describes twelve lines further down; the remark is accurate at HEAD and
  the handover text is what is wrong. M-g, the `LoadDiagnostics.cs:52` line-wrap artefact, was fixed by
  I3 in the same commit. M-d was a defect in a *lever's* text, not in shipped code: on a real malformed
  non-final line the store returns `ChangeLogSkipped = true` **and** a fully populated
  `ChangeLogSkipReason`, and the ledger holding the faulty lever was deleted at close-out.
- **The fix wave that changed `CompactChangeLogAsync` and `RepairAsync` touched no documentation file
  at all**, so three shipped statements are now wrong or misleading. Two omit a third compaction rule
  the fix added: a change-log line whose history file exists but does not deserialize is kept
  unconditionally. The third, in the operator runbook, is measurably misleading: a quarantined
  `UnreadableRecordFile`'s own change-log line **is** dropped in the same pass whenever any unrelated
  droppable line exists, because the quarantine rename runs before the change-log half and makes that
  line genuinely dangling. Measured both ways — with another droppable line present, `quarantined=1
  dropped=2` and the log falls from four lines to two; with none, `dropped=0` and the line survives.
- **The tiered conformance fixture gives its hot tier no clock.** `ConformanceProviders` passes the
  injected `TimeProvider` to cold and constructs hot with the default constructor, so hot silently runs
  on `TimeProvider.System`. No existing suite depends on hot's clock and the asymmetry is invisible
  through `ReadHistoryAsync`, which delegates straight to cold — which is precisely why it survived
  three phases. A retention suite that left it there would be silently avoiding a real question.

#### 1. `RetentionConformanceTests`, and the fixture it needs

A new shared abstract suite in `tests/Statesman.Conformance.Tests`, run once per provider by five
subclasses, joining the nine suites that already share `ConformanceProviders`. It is a **pinning**
suite in exactly the sense `LedgerWriteConformanceTests` and `CancellationConformanceTests` are: every
assertion was measured already true on all five providers before the suite existed, so there is no RED
at baseline and the class remark says so rather than leaving a reader to infer it.

Two things make it cheap where the parked note assumed it would be expensive. `PruneAsync` is a
**required member of `IStateLedgerStore`** rather than a capability, so there is no discovery, no
per-provider reach-through and no `Assert.SkipUnless` beyond the fixture's existing "this provider's
infrastructure is not available" skip. And every provider reads the injected `TimeProvider` both to
stamp `OccurredAt` and to compute the age cutoff — **including Redis, whose server clock governs lease
expiry and never retention** — so a `ManualTimeProvider` makes `MaxAge` exact on all five rather than
approximately true on four.

The suite owns its clock (`protected ManualTimeProvider Clock { get; }`) and each subclass hands it to
its provider factory. One fixture change comes with it: `ConformanceProviders.TieredOverInMemoryAsync`
passes the injected clock to the **hot** tier as well as the cold one, so the two tiers compute the
same cutoff.

The `MaxRevisions` half pins that the newest N revisions survive and nothing older; that a prune is
per address, with a control address keeping all six; that pruning again with the same policy removes
nothing further, because each filter is computed over what is stored now; and that `StateHistoryOptions`
still pages what survived exactly as it paged what was there before.

#### 2. The `MaxAge` and `MaxBytes` halves, and six corners nothing documented

Nine more facts, and six of them settle behaviour that is uniform across all five providers and
promised nowhere:

| Corner | Ruling |
|---|---|
| The `MaxAge` cutoff is **inclusive**: `OccurredAt >= now - age`, so a record occurring exactly at the cutoff survives | pinned, one assertion |
| `MaxBytes` can leave a **non-contiguous** survivor set: the byte walk uses `continue`, not `break`, so a small old record is admitted after a large newer one was skipped | pinned as **intended**; four independent implementations agree and it retains strictly more revisions under the same budget. Decision 90 |
| `MaxBytes` counts the **serialized payload only** — no file size, no key size, no JSON envelope, no metadata; a tombstone costs zero | pinned **and documented**. Decision 91 |
| `MaxRevisions` counts what survived the age and tombstone filters, not the raw revision count: the limits narrow **sequentially** rather than intersecting independent sets | pinned through the combined-limit ordering fact |
| `KeepTombstones = false` still retains a tombstone that is the latest revision | pinned; `docs/concepts/ledger.md` already says this accurately |
| Pruning an address nobody ever wrote is a **silent no-op**, not a throw | pinned |

Prune **cancellation** is deliberately not re-asserted. It is already shared on all five providers by
`CancellationConformanceTests.An_already_cancelled_token_is_honoured_by_every_store_operation`, and the
retention suite's class remark cites it instead of growing a second copy that could drift from it.

#### 3. Feed, head and partition-catalog parity after a prune

Phase 11's rule — a record leaves the change feed exactly when its history record leaves the store — is
pinned today for `MaxRevisions` only. This phase asserts it for all three bounds on all five providers,
through the four different mechanisms that implement it: the in-memory store rebuilds its feed under
the same lock, Redis removes from the history set and the `:changes` sorted set member-exact in one
`MULTI`/`EXEC`, Entity Framework Core needs nothing because one table serves both, and the filesystem
provider leaves a dangling log line that the feed read skips and `ConformanceStore.Maintain`
(`CompactChangeLogAsync`) then compacts away. The head is pinned to survive every bound — four
independent mechanisms keep it, and the observable consequence is one assertion per bound — and the
partition catalog is pinned to keep listing a pruned address, which it does because prune never removes
the head.

#### 4. `BrokenRetentionConformanceTests`, the discrimination proof

A pinning suite's own mechanism is discrimination, so it needs the two-halves proof
`BrokenLedgerWriteConformanceTests` established: wrong doubles that must fail, and a correct double
that must pass. Five public `static Assert…Async` entry points on the shared suite are run against four
**narrow** wrong doubles — one that blanks `MaxBytes` before delegating, one that blanks `MaxAge`, one
that treats a prune as "forget this address, head included", and one whose history shrinks while its
change feed does not — plus `InMemoryStateLedgerStore` as the correct half against all five.

Narrowness is the point. A double that pruned nothing, or pruned everything, fails every assertion and
therefore proves only that the assertions are not vacuous; these four fail exactly the assertions they
should and no others, which is what proves each assertion pins its own rule rather than riding on a
neighbour's. The feed-blind double records its envelopes **at append time** rather than snapshotting on
first read: a lazily built feed does not discriminate at all, because the shared assertion's only feed
read happens after the prune, by which point the inner store has already dropped the records. The
pre-flight probe hit exactly that and had to fix its own double.

#### 5. Retention documentation, and the ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 1 call

`docs/concepts/ledger.md`'s retention section is accurate but silent on all six corners above; it gains
the `MaxAge` inclusivity, the non-contiguous survivor set, the payload-only accounting, the sequential
narrowing, and the unknown-address no-op, in the page's own register rather than as a specification.

`ROADMAP.md`'s bullet 1 says today that "retention semantics (`MaxAge`/`MaxRevisions`/`MaxBytes`
pruning) are not yet in the shared suite". After this phase they are, and the bullet's remaining
qualifier — provider-specific corruption behaviour beyond import refusal — is a **design ruling**
rather than an open gap: Phase 16 decision 63 ruled it stays per-provider because each provider's
failure shape is a property of its own storage format, and Phase 18 shipped the substantial
per-provider half of it as filesystem verify and repair plus a cross-provider runbook. **Bullet 1 is
therefore declared shipped**, with that ruling stated in the bullet rather than left as an implied
shortfall (decision 98). 0.2 is still not complete: bullets 4, 6 and 8 remain.

#### 6. The lease TTL split

`LeaseConformanceTests`'s single `LeaseTtl` becomes two members — `HoldTtl` for every acquisition whose
lease must still be alive when the assertion lands, and `ExpiringTtl` for the acquisitions `ExpireAsync`
is going to wait out. Redis overrides them 30 s and 200 ms; Entity Framework Core returns 30 s for both,
because its expiry is a `ManualTimeProvider` advance, and its `ExpireAsync` then advances past
`ExpiringTtl`. No production code changes, and
`tests/Statesman.Redis.Tests/RedisLeaseProviderTests.cs` needs no change because it never lost the
asymmetry.

This is a flake fix, not a convention fix. No repository-wide rule forbids the shipped shape:
`docs/guides/testing.md` says nothing about real-time bounds in tests, and the "no real-time bound
anywhere" rule lives in the per-phase plan briefs, aimed at `Assert.True(elapsed > …)` magnitude
assertions rather than at a TTL used to *induce* expiry. The split nonetheless **removes** one real-time
bound from the suite: five of the six acquisitions stop depending on wall-clock timing at all.

The flake is rare and the margin is large on an idle machine — 20 sequential runs, 20 more of the
per-provider suite, and 24 runs as three rounds of eight concurrent processes all came back clean, and
200 iterations of the exact shape measured the acquire-to-renew window at p50 1.13 ms and p99 2.11 ms
against a 200 ms budget, roughly 180 times the margin. That is why it reproduces on a loaded CI runner
and not here, and why the proof is an injected stall rather than a repetition count.

#### 7. The Phase 18 documentation truth pass

Three shipped statements are corrected in place — the `CompactChangeLogAsync` remarks in
`FileSystemStateLedgerStore.cs`, the matching `CHANGELOG.md` bullet, and the `UnreadableRecordFile`
paragraph plus table row in `docs/operations/recovery.md` — and `docs/superpowers/HANDOVER.md`'s Phase
18 minors paragraph is corrected where it still lists M-e as outstanding. Everything else the pass
checked is accurate and is recorded as checked rather than silently left: the dry-run-exceeds-apply
note, the torn-tail and malformed-line rows, the four remaining finding rows, the nine finding-kind doc
comments, the Phase 18 changelog entry end to end, and `docs/concepts/ledger.md`'s existing retention
prose.

The one remaining Phase 18 coverage minor — that no lever isolates "`MisplacedStreamDirectory` is never
acted on", because it shares repair's `default` switch arm with `OrphanedTemporaryFile` — is closed by
a **lever-only** step in this task rather than by shipped code: the arm is temporarily made to swallow
that kind alone, the existing fact is observed to fail, and the change is reverted. No production code
changes and no test is added, because the fact that would assert it already exists.

#### 8. The disposed-runtime health check

Phase 18's post-dispose ruling — that none of the four `IStatesmanDiagnostics` members throws after
disposal — rests on a consumer argument: `StatesmanHealthCheck.CheckHealthAsync` walks every runtime in
`IStatesmanRegistry.All` and calls two of them with no disposal check of its own, so a throwing surface
would turn a health probe over a registry still holding a disposed root into an exception rather than a
report. Phase 18's own Lever C measured that `Statesman.Hosting.Tests` stays **fully green** under a
`ThrowIfDisposed()` in `ReadLoadDiagnostics`, because no fact in that project reaches the check that
way. One fact closes that: dispose a root without disposing the container, assert it is still in the
registry, and require the check to answer Healthy. Its lever is the same `ThrowIfDisposed()`, which now
fires.

#### Explicitly parked, with reasons

- **ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 4 — serializer envelopes.** A storage-format change with its own design pass,
  and the only remaining 0.2 bullet that can break a shipped store on disk: `IStateSerializer` has no
  notion of content type or serializer identity, and adding one means either three new `StateRecord`
  members or a framing prefix on `Payload`, touching the filesystem `FileRecord` JSON, Redis's
  member-exact opaque bytes across two keys, the Entity Framework Core columns plus a migration for
  three engines, and `Statesman.Tooling`'s export/import format. Sized **L**, not measured. Parked
  identically in Phases 15 through 18.
- **ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 6 — Redis Cluster coverage.** Now sized rather than deferred: the CI job is the
  small half — `ci.yml`'s `redis-tests` job has the shape to copy, though a single-node cluster cannot
  be a plain `services:` entry because GitHub's service containers cannot run `redis-cli --cluster
  create` at startup — and the **key layout is the large half**. Two `ITransaction` sites deliberately
  span hash slots and are documented standalone-only, two Lua scripts take `KEYS` spanning the position
  counter, the partition hash, the history set and the changes set, and six key builders would have to
  be wrapped in a hash tag. That is a design change to the stored key shape and needs its own ruling
  first (decision 99). Sized **L**, not measured.

  *Amended (Phase 21, 2026-09-14):* seven key builders, three `ITransaction` sites and five Lua
  scripts, one of which spans six keys. See the Phase 21 section.
- **ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 8 — analyzer code fixes.** Parked for this phase and named as **Phase 20's
  target** (decision 99): three rules, one new `Statesman.CodeFixes` project, one new package, no
  storage format and no infrastructure. Sized **M**.

  *Amended (Phase 20 planner, 2026-09-14):* "one new package" is measurably wrong and is superseded by
  **decision 101**, which reads "one new project, packaged into the existing analyzer nupkg". A second
  **assembly** is mandatory — RS1038 forbids an analyzer assembly from referencing
  `Microsoft.CodeAnalysis.Workspaces`, and `EnforceExtendedAnalyzerRules` plus `TreatWarningsAsErrors`
  makes it a build error, one per analyzer type — but nothing forces a second **package**, and
  `Statesman.CodeFixes` ships inside `Statesman.Analyzers` under `analyzers/dotnet/cs` with
  `IsPackable=false`. The package count therefore stays at 22. Two further clauses of decision 99 are
  also superseded: "`STM003` a plausible one" is refuted by decision 103, and the phase turned out to
  touch **four** rules rather than three, because STM004 is added (decision 104) and STM001's reach is
  widened (decision 105). See the Phase 20 section above.
- **Refactoring the three near-identical `OrphanedTemporaryFile` loops in `FileSystemLedgerRecovery.cs`**
  (Phase 18 minor M-a). Measured rather than deferred again: a shared helper is eight lines replacing
  fifteen with three, a net saving of **four lines**, and the middle loop sits inside a
  `Directory.Exists` guard the other two do not have, so the three are not identical in context. The
  minor is closed, not carried (decision 96).
- **Changing `MaxBytes`'s `continue` to a `break`.** The non-contiguous survivor set is pinned as
  intended (decision 90); changing it would be a silent behaviour break with no complainant, retaining
  strictly fewer revisions under the same budget.
- **A polling `ExpireAsync` that watches the Redis key disappear.** Measured not to close the flake it
  was offered for, and it cannot even be expressed without new fixture surface, because `ExpireAsync()`
  takes no parameters and `ConformanceStore` exposes no connection. Worth about 2.4 s per run as a
  speed-up if someone wants it separately (decision 93).
- **A server-side lease lever (`PEXPIRE key 1`).** `Renew_returns_false_after_the_lease_expired_with_nobody_racing`
  exists specifically to prove the TTL bounds the hold; replacing the TTL with an explicit expire would
  prove only that a deleted key behaves like an expired one (decision 93).
- **Lifting verify and repair into the shared conformance suite.** Unchanged from Phase 16 decision 63
  and Phases 17 and 18: the other four providers have no textual structure a crash can leave
  half-written, so a shared suite would have to invent a damage mechanism for them.
- **A cross-process filesystem append lock**, and with it multi-process writers. Unchanged from Phases
  11 through 18.
- **A persisted change-log index**, **repairing a `MisplacedStreamDirectory` by moving it**, **deleting
  an `OrphanedTemporaryFile`**, and **rewriting a `MalformedChangeLogLine`.** All unchanged from Phase
  18, each with its own reason recorded there.
- **`MessageId` collision from ordinal `Root` versus canonical lower-casing**, **Redis concurrent
  same-revision `ImportAsync` beyond the documented sentence**, **pipelining the Redis sink**, **an HTTP
  webhook sink**, and **lifting Redis's `MaxImportablePosition`.** All 0.4 or storage-format items, each
  re-ruled at least six times.
- **What the pre-flight did not measure**, carried forward as caveats rather than as facts: every
  claim about the three unstarted 0.2 bullets above; whether `redis:7-alpine` can host a single-node
  cluster in a GitHub Actions job; whether the declined `AddOrphanedTemporaries` helper compiles (the
  eight-line figure is a line count, not a build); and the Entity Framework Core retention matrix on
  SQL Server and PostgreSQL, since the probe ran on SQLite only.

#### The public API delta

**None. Nothing public is added, removed or changed in any package.**

Every file this phase creates is a test file. The only edits under `src/` are XML documentation
comments in `src/Statesman.Persistence.FileSystem/FileSystemStateLedgerStore.cs`, which change no
signature, no member and no behaviour. The close-out proves it the way Phase 18 proved its additive
delta: `dotnet pack` on the touched package with the `0.3.0` baseline active and `obj/Release` and
`bin/Release` cleared first — because a single-project pack skips `RunPackageValidation` incrementally
and reports success either way — looking for the literal line `APICompat ran successfully without
finding any breaking changes`, and a `git diff --stat` over
`src/**/CompatibilitySuppressions.xml` that must be empty.

**No capability is added, so `docs/architecture/capabilities.md` gains no row and flips no cell, and
`tests/Statesman.Capabilities.Tests/CapabilityMatrixTests.cs` is untouched** — the Phase 10 through 18
precedent. `PruneAsync` is a required member of `IStateLedgerStore` rather than a capability, which is
exactly what lets a shared suite call it on all five providers with no discovery step.
`docs/reference/public-api.md` is untouched, because it is a curated map with no provider-specific
surface and nothing public moved.

### Phase 20 — Analyzer code fixes, collection-mutation analysis, and the Redis notifier dispose (2026-09-14)

Phase 19 named this phase's target in decision 99: ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 8, analyzer code fixes, chosen as the
only one of the three remaining 0.2 bullets that is both bounded and free of a storage-format or
infrastructure design decision. The pre-flight research measured every claim in that decision, and two of
them did not survive. "One new package" is wrong: a code fix **cannot** live in the analyzer assembly,
because `EnforceExtendedAnalyzerRules` plus `TreatWarningsAsErrors` turns RS1038 into a build error, one per
analyzer type — but a second *package* is not what that forces, only a second *assembly*, and it ships
inside the existing `Statesman.Analyzers` nupkg under `analyzers/dotnet/cs`. And "`STM003` a plausible one"
is wrong too: constant folding already silences every foldable argument shape, so the only mechanically
fixable row left has zero occurrences in this repository and no complainant anywhere.

The phase also carries a second, unrelated item: the Redis change-notifier dispose flake the Phase 19
close-out recorded at roughly one failure in thirteen full-suite runs. **That flake did not reproduce** —
forty runs of the named class, sixteen full-suite runs and a hundred and seventy direct dispose-race
iterations were all green. It is fixed anyway, because the research named the throwing call site by stack
trace and then made the same defect deterministic with an injected socket stall: at HEAD the stalled shape
fails **thirty-eight out of thirty-eight**.

**What the pre-flight measurement changed.** Every claim in the controller's brief and every sizing in the
research was treated as a hypothesis and probed at `017e92a` in a scratch worktree.

- **The package-validation gate is inert for the analyzer package, so adding public types to it changes
  nothing.** `EnablePackageValidation` is true and the `0.3.0` baseline is active, and the target does run —
  but the baseline nupkg has no `lib/` or `ref/` assets and neither does the new one, so there is nothing to
  compare. Proved by lever: renaming the only public analyzer type and re-packing produced **no** `CP0002`.
  No suppression file is needed and none is touched.
- **The exclusion in `src/Directory.Build.props` is mandatory, not tidiness.** A new `netstandard2.0`
  project that is not named in that condition fails with `NETSDK1005` before anything else can be measured,
  because the inherited `TargetFrameworks` makes the SDK run a cross-targeting outer build over `net8.0`,
  `net9.0` and `net10.0`.
- **The xunit-v2 flavour of the Roslyn testing packages cannot be used here at all.**
  `Microsoft.CodeAnalysis.CSharp.CodeFix.Testing.XUnit` restores and then fails to compile: it drags in
  `xunit.assert` 2.3.0 alongside the repository's `xunit.v3.assert` 4.0.0, producing `CS0433` on every
  existing `Assert` call site, and its `XUnitVerifier` is `[Obsolete]`, which `TreatWarningsAsErrors` turns
  into an error. The flavour-agnostic package with `DefaultVerifier` works. Its default reference assemblies
  are `netcoreapp3.1`, which has no `IsExternalInit`, so every fact whose fixed state uses `init` must pin
  `ReferenceAssemblies.Net.Net80` or fail with `CS0518`.
- **The brief's own instruction for the immutable-collection builders could not have worked as written.** It
  asked for the `type.ContainingType?.ContainingNamespace` clause to be dropped and the outer-namespace
  clause kept, on the reading that the two clauses separate `ImmutableList<T>` from
  `ImmutableList<T>.Builder`. They do not: Roslyn's `ContainingNamespace` for a **nested** type is the
  namespace of its containing type, so both clauses exclude the builder. The discriminator that works, and
  is measured, is `type.ContainingType is null` — exclude the top-level immutable collections and admit
  their nested builders.
- **STM004 attributes a mutation to the nearest managed owner on the receiver chain, not the outermost
  one.** `state.Child.Items.Add(1)`, where `Child`'s own type carries `[ManagedState]`, names `Child`'s
  type; `state.Plain.Items.Add(1)`, where `Plain`'s type does not, names the outer managed type. The
  research's case table recorded that both fire and did not record which type each names; measurement
  settled it, and a draft assertion that predicted the outer type failed.
- **The throwing path in the Redis notifier is not `MoveNextAsync`.** It is the
  `await subscriber.SubscribeAsync(...)` at `RedisStateLedgerStore.cs:600-603`, which at HEAD has no `catch`
  of any kind. The read loop's existing guard is *earlier* than nothing and *later* than this: it wraps
  `enumerator.MoveNextAsync()` only, and the failing await happens before `queue` exists. Cancelling the
  disposal token does nothing for a StackExchange.Redis call that takes no token, so the SUBSCRIBE round
  trip is still outstanding when `DisposeAsync` tears the socket down under it.

#### 1. `src/Statesman.CodeFixes`, one project inside the existing package

A new `netstandard2.0` project, `IsPackable=false`, `EnforceExtendedAnalyzerRules=true`, referencing
`Microsoft.CodeAnalysis.CSharp.Workspaces` — the one central pin that has had no consumer anywhere in the
solution since it was added, and that this phase finally gives one. Its DLL ships inside the existing
`Statesman.Analyzers` nupkg, so a consumer installs one package and gets both the diagnostics and their
light bulbs.

Four files are wired, each for a measured reason: `Statesman.slnx` and the
`src/Directory.Build.props` `TargetFrameworks` exclusion (without both, `NETSDK1005`);
`Statesman.Analyzers.csproj` gains a `ReferenceOutputAssembly="false"` project reference plus a second
`None` pack item (without both, the nupkg carries one DLL). `ReferenceOutputAssembly="false"` is
load-bearing rather than decorative: a compile-time reference on the code-fix assembly would pull Workspaces
back into the analyzer assembly and re-trigger RS1038, which is the error that forced the split.

The package count stays at **22** and no `PackageValidationBaselineVersion` override is added, because pack
never runs for an `IsPackable=false` project. `docs/reference/api-compatibility.md`'s "fifteen of
twenty-two" arithmetic and its seven baseline-blanking projects are therefore untouched.

#### 2. The Roslyn testing harness, and the one type it does not supply

One new central pin, `Microsoft.CodeAnalysis.CSharp.CodeFix.Testing` at **1.1.4** — the newest stable
version, measured against the NuGet flat-container index rather than assumed — plus one
`PackageReference` on `tests/Statesman.Analyzers.Tests`, which also gains a project reference to the new
code-fix project.

That package supplies `CSharpCodeFixTest<TAnalyzer, TCodeFix, TVerifier>` and **not** a C# flavour of the
analyzer-only test: `CSharpAnalyzerTest` lives in `Microsoft.CodeAnalysis.CSharp.Analyzer.Testing`, a
separate package this phase deliberately does not pin. A second pin is not needed, because a
`CSharpCodeFixTest` whose provider fixes nothing and whose fixed source equals its test source **is** an
analyzer test. The harness therefore ships a six-line `NoFixProvider` alongside the two fixture types, which
is what lets an analyzer-only fact use the framework's `{|#0:...|}` location markup and its exact message
comparison.

The repository's own hand-rolled `AnalyzerTestHost` stays, and gains one method: `CompileErrors(source)`,
returning the compiler's own errors for a source. A fixture that does not compile silences every analyzer,
so an empty analyzer-diagnostic list means nothing until that list is empty too. This is not hypothetical
tidiness — the research probe's first STM004 fixture named a property `Array`, which shadowed `System.Array`,
produced `CS0236` in all twenty cases, and read as "STM001 misses everything".

#### 3. STM002's two code fixes

`Statesman.CodeFixes.MutableShapeCodeFixProvider` fixes the two shapes STM002 reports: a publicly settable
auto-property becomes `{ get; init; }`, and a public non-readonly single-variable field gains `readonly`.
Fix-all is `WellKnownFixAllProviders.BatchFixer` — and overriding `GetFixAllProvider` at all is not
optional here, because RS1016 makes its absence a build error for any provider that registers a fix.

Four shapes are deliberately **not** fixed, each with its own guard and its own fact: a setter with a block
or expression body (rewriting `set` to `init` changes *when* that body may run, not only who may call it); a
multi-variable field declaration (`readonly` would apply to every declarator rather than the reported one); a
`const` field (already immutable, and STM002 never reports it); and converting a class to a `record`, which
the rule's own message suggests but which is a whole-type rewrite across every construction site.

Two consequences are documented rather than engineered around. `init` needs
`System.Runtime.CompilerServices.IsExternalInit`, which `netstandard2.0` does not carry, so the fixed code
fails with `CS0518` there until the consumer declares its own one-line shim; a provider cannot add a type to
a consumer's project from a single-member fix. And the `readonly` arm can trade a warning for a **compile
error**: measured, a field written from outside its declaring constructor goes from `[STM001, STM002]` to no
analyzer diagnostic at all plus a compiler `CS0191` at the write site. That is arguably louder and more
correct, and it is a build break, so the analyzer guide says so.

#### 4. STM004, collection mutation, design option (i)

A new id and a new analyzer class, `ManagedStateCollectionMutationAnalyzer`, reporting an in-place mutation
of a collection reached through a `[ManagedState]` type: a call to a known mutating member, an indexer
assignment, or an array element assignment. Design option (i) — a receiver-chain walk with **no data flow
and no alias tracking**.

It is a new id rather than a widening of STM001, for three measured reasons.
`docs/reference/diagnostics.md` is one line per id and `docs/guides/analyzers.md` is one `##` section per id,
whose slugs are the published anchors, so widening STM001's row would make the anchor
`stm001-direct-managed-state-mutation` a lie. A consumer with `<NoWarn>STM001</NoWarn>` during migration made
a decision about *assignments*, not about `List<T>.Add`, and folding in would either newly fire on them or
newly go silent for them. And the two message formats cannot be merged without going vague: STM001 names a
member and its type, the collection case names an *operation on* a member.

Three behaviours are load-bearing and each is pinned by its own fact and falsified by its own lever.
`ImmutableList<T>.Add` is silent and `ImmutableList<T>.Builder.Add` fires, separated by
`type.ContainingType is null` rather than by a namespace test or a return-type filter — a return-type filter
looks tempting and fails, because `Builder.Add` returns `void` while `ImmutableList<T>` does implement
`IList<T>` explicitly. An array element access resolves to **no symbol at all**, so the array arm needs
`GetTypeInfo` on the receiver where the indexer arm uses `GetSymbolInfo`. And the boundaries, the ignore
attribute and the object-initializer exemption are the ones STM001 already has, because both rules call the
same helper.

#### 5. STM001 gains the same receiver-chain walk

This is a **behaviour change to a shipped rule**, and the reason it belongs in this phase rather than a later
one is that shipping STM004 without it would leave the two rules disagreeing in a way a reader would read as
a bug: `state.Plain.Items.Add(1)` would report STM004 while `state.Plain.Value = 1` stayed silent, for the
same `Plain` on the same managed type.

`AnalyzeMutation` asked only the *immediately containing* type for `[ManagedState]`, and a plain class held
by managed state does not have it. Ownership now comes from the same walk STM004 uses, in the same shared
`internal static ManagedStateOwnership` helper both analyzers call — one definition, not two that can drift.
The walk stops at the nearest link owned by a managed type, so the diagnostic names that type. A plain type's
own constructor and its own methods writing its own members are reached by no managed chain and stay silent,
which is what keeps the change from reporting on every plain class in a codebase.

The `[StateMutationAnalysisIgnore]` escape hatch keeps its meaning on the *target* symbol as well as on each
link, which needs an explicit check in the caller and has its own fact and its own lever: without it,
`state.Plain.Exempt = 1` newly fires.

Measured, and this is the claim that makes the change safe to ship: the repository's own analyzer consumer,
`samples/Statesman.Sample.Migration`, builds with **zero** warnings under both the widened STM001 and the new
STM004, from a cleared `obj`, with every warning an error. Proved not to be a vacuous pass by lever: adding a
collection member to that sample's `[ManagedState]` record and mutating it produced
`error STM004: 's.Audit.Add' mutates the collection held by managed state 'SessionState' …` at
`Program.cs(28,49)`.

#### 6. Analyzer documentation, and a help link per rule

All four descriptors gain a `helpLinkUri`, which is the only discoverability surface a light bulb has here
because RS2008 (analyzer release tracking) is in the project's `NoWarn`. The anchors are not guessed: the
analyzer guide has one `##` section per rule, and a docfx 2.78.5 build of `docs/docfx.json` was read to
confirm all four ids — including the new `stm004-managed-state-collection-mutation` — appear in the generated
HTML exactly as the descriptors spell them.

`docs/guides/analyzers.md` gains a "how to fix" paragraph per rule, an STM004 section, the `IsExternalInit`
and `CS0191` consequences, a paragraph recommending read-only collection interfaces for `[ManagedState]`
members, and the STM001 ownership sentence. It also **strikes "collection mutation" from its limitations
sentence and keeps "alias"**, which is now the honest shape of what the analyzer cannot prove, stated
precisely rather than as a list.

No STM002 companion shape rule is added for a `List<T>`-typed member. Every `[ManagedState]` declaration in
this repository's samples, tests and documentation is a positional `record` whose collection members are
read-only interfaces — zero occurrences of `List<T>`, `Dictionary<,>` or `HashSet<T>` on a managed type — so
a shape rule would ship with no in-repo signal and, being enabled by default, would newly warn every consumer
who chose `List<T>` deliberately behind a boundary. STM004 catches the *consequence* at the site that
performs it, which is where the reader can act.

*Amended (Phase 21, 2026-09-14):* false at HEAD, and false when it was written.
`tests/Statesman.Analyzers.Tests/ManagedStateFixture.cs` declares all three on `Bag`, and Phase 20
shipped that file. They are deliberately adversarial string-literal fixtures for the analyzer under
test rather than a signal that a consumer wants the rule, so the ruling stands; the claim needed
"outside the analyzer's own test fixtures".

#### 7. The Redis notifier's in-flight SUBSCRIBE

Two guarded catches in `SubscribeAsync`, no change to `DisposeAsync`, and no new public surface. The first
wraps the SUBSCRIBE await itself, which at HEAD is unguarded; the second widens the read loop's existing
`OperationCanceledException`-only guard to the same set, because the Phase 19 field failure was a
`RedisConnectionException`, which would have escaped there too.

Both guards are narrowed by **which token was cancelled** — `linked.IsCancellationRequested &&
!cancellationToken.IsCancellationRequested` — never by the exception type alone. That is what keeps a genuine
connection failure propagating to the caller unchanged, and it has its own fact and its own lever: with the
token condition removed, the genuine-failure fact fails deterministically five times out of five.

*Amended (fix round, 2026-09-14):* This step's own text was wrong, and the review that found it treats the
finding as a Critical rather than a style note — see `task-7-review.md` and `progress.md`'s Task 7 ruling.
As implemented verbatim from this step, the read loop's widened guard kept only the pre-existing
`!cancellationToken.IsCancellationRequested` operand and never gained the second, `linked.IsCancellationRequested`
operand this paragraph describes. For the pre-existing `OperationCanceledException` case alone, "caller
didn't cancel" already entails "disposal did" (`linked` is that branch's only cancellation source under
`WithCancellation(linked.Token)`), so the missing operand was silently harmless there — but it is not
harmless for the four widened types (`RedisException`, `ObjectDisposedException`, `IOException`,
`SocketException`), which a genuine connection failure can throw with **neither** token cancelled, so the
guard as first shipped could swallow a non-disposal failure as a silent clean `false`. The fix round
(commit `7658903`) corrects this: the `SUBSCRIBE` guard requires `linked.IsCancellationRequested` uniformly
across all five caught types, exactly as written above, but the read loop's guard is **not** symmetric with
it — `OperationCanceledException` needs no `linked` check at all, and only the other four require it. "Both
guards are narrowed by which token was cancelled" is still true in spirit; it is no longer true that both
guards apply the same operand to every caught type. See the exit criteria's amended item 8 for the shipped
`CHANGELOG.md` wording of the corrected shape.

The catch set is a measured inventory, not a defensive net: `TaskCanceledException` (the pending message was
cancelled), `IOException` wrapping `SocketException` 995, and `RedisConnectionException`/`SocketFailure` (the
socket was aborted under it). **One half of it is honestly unpinned.** With `IOException` and
`SocketException` removed from the set, the stalled fact still passes — five times out of five here, thirty
out of thirty in the research — because the `IOException` shape appeared only once in thirty baseline
iterations. Those two types are justified by the measured inventory, not by a discriminating test, and this
section says so rather than implying a lever exists where none does.

Two facts land in `tests/Statesman.Redis.Tests`, next to the existing `OwnsConnection = false` dispose fact:
the stalled dispose, and the anti-lever in which a genuine connection failure with no disposal must still
throw. Both are driven by a `StallProxy` — a loopback TCP proxy with one switch, which once set stops
forwarding client bytes so every command after that point is on the wire and never answered while the socket
stays open. This is the Phase 19 lease-split "injected stall" pattern applied to a socket instead of a clock:
no container pause, no sleep-and-hope, and no new production seam.

**The `SocketException` 995 caveat, stated rather than hidden.** 995 is a Windows error code and every
measurement in this phase ran on Windows 11 with SDK 10.0.303. The catch is by exception **type**
(`IOException`, `SocketException`), not by code, and both types are the platform-neutral wrappers .NET raises
for an aborted socket read on every platform — only the numeric `SocketError` beneath them differs. So the
guard is expected to hold on Linux and macOS, and CI's `build-test` job on `ubuntu-latest` and `macos-latest`
is the first measurement of it. Unsubscribing before `ConnectionMultiplexer.Dispose` was rejected as an
alternative: the store keeps no registry of live subscriptions, so the only store-level option is
`ISubscriber.UnsubscribeAllAsync()`, which would make `DisposeAsync` genuinely asynchronous where it is
`ValueTask.CompletedTask` today and is itself a round trip that hangs under exactly the stalled condition
this fix has to survive.

`docs/providers/index.md`'s Redis notifier paragraph gains the dispose sentence its in-memory neighbour has
had all along.

   different, independently measured reason: neither of the two probed scenarios (the connection's actual
   owner disposing it directly, or the underlying proxy socket being destroyed) produces an exception at the
   read loop at all within an 8 s window; StackExchange.Redis's `ChannelMessageQueue` does not appear to
   tear down or complete a live subscription on a bare `Dispose` of an unrelated multiplexer, so the catch
   guard the lever targets is never reached either way. This is a second, independently confirmed coverage
   gap on the same Redis-notifier surface, not a defect in the fix.

   *Amended (fix wave, 2026-09-14):* Two of this criterion's conclusions were wrong, both proved wrong by
   measurement rather than by reading, and the fix wave adds seventeen further levers.

   **Lever 6's third case is held by syntax, not by a guard.** The correction is recorded in place above.
   A collection initializer's `Items = { 1, 2 }` form emits no `InvocationExpressionSyntax` at all, so
   `AnalyzeInvocation` never runs on it, and an element initializer's `[0] = 1` is an
   `ImplicitElementAccess` rather than the `ElementAccessExpressionSyntax` `AnalyzeElementTarget`
   requires — so neither of STM004's entry points can reach an initializer, and the `IsInitialization`
   call in its `Report` was dead code. The fix wave deletes that call and the object-initializer
   InlineData case still passes. STM001's copy of the same guard **is** live and stays: with it disabled,
   all three initializer shapes report, which is what the fix wave's three new facts now pin, one per arm
   of the `switch`.

   **Lever 12 fires after all, on a longer sample.** This criterion recorded it as not firing 5/5 and
   ascribed that to `StallProxy` being structurally unable to reproduce the `IOException`/`SocketException`
   shape, "which needs the socket itself torn down mid-round-trip". That explanation is withdrawn. The
   final review observed the `IOException` shape once in five runs of Lever 10, and the fix wave ran
   Lever 12 itself — `IOException` and `SocketException` removed from **both** guards — over **twenty**
   consecutive runs of `Disposing_the_store_ends_a_subscription_whose_SUBSCRIBE_is_still_in_flight`:
   it **failed 3 of 20** (runs 3, 11 and 15), with
   `System.IO.IOException : Unable to read data from the transport connection: The I/O operation has been
   aborted because of either a thread exit or an application request.` wrapping
   `System.Net.Sockets.SocketException`, thrown from the SUBSCRIBE await at
   `src/Statesman.Persistence.Redis/RedisStateLedgerStore.cs:623`. `StallProxy` does reproduce that shape;
   it simply does so intermittently — 3 in 20 here, 1 in 5 and 1 in 30 before. So the two types are
   discriminated by the existing stalled fact after all, and the honestly-declared coverage gap on that
   half of the catch set is closed by sample size rather than by a second stall mode. The lever was
   reverted; both types ship in both guards, unchanged.

   **Seventeen fix-wave levers, each applied alone in the working tree, observed against the whole
   `Statesman.Analyzers.Tests` suite, and reverted before the commit.** Every operand the final review
   measured as firing on nothing is now pinned, except STM004's `Report` `IsInitialization`, which was
   deleted as dead instead.
