#### Explicitly parked, with reasons

- **Option (iii), full data flow for STM004.** Unchanged and correct: option (ii), which this phase
  ships, closes the shape users actually write, and per-method-body `DataFlowAnalysis` buys aliases
  through fields and calls at a false-positive cost nothing in this repository can measure.
- **Phase 21 fix wave, 2026-09-15: four analyzer minors from the final review, added here as Phase 22
  candidates.** **M3**, `SingleInitializerOf`'s `ArrowExpressionClauseSyntax` scope fallback
  (`ManagedStateOwnership.cs:155-156`) looks unreachable: a C# local declarator always sits inside a
  `BlockSyntax`, and the shapes that do not (`foreach`, pattern and deconstruction designations) are
  rejected earlier by the `VariableDeclaratorSyntax` test. Reasoned, not measured; a harmless dead
  branch. **M4**, a local in a top-level-statement file has no enclosing block, so the alias hop
  silently answers null there; reasoned from the code, not measured, because the shared fixture cannot
  express top-level statements — a documented-false-negative candidate, not a defect. **M5**, STM001
  does not see a deconstructing assignment into a managed member: `var q = s.Plain; (q.Value, _) =
  (1, 0); q.Items.Add(1);` reports STM004 on the third statement and nothing on the second, where
  `q.Value = 1` would report STM001, because STM001's `AnalyzeMutation` reads `assignment.Left` and
  finds a `TupleExpressionSyntax` rather than the `MemberAccessExpressionSyntax` it expects.
  Pre-existing, not introduced by this phase; now pinned as a known-current-truth fact by the fix
  wave's fourth new row rather than fixed. *Amended (Phase 22, 2026-09-15):* M5 is fixed rather than
  parked. `ManagedStateMutationAnalyzer` gained `AnalyzeTarget`, which decomposes a deconstructing
  assignment before reporting, and the Phase 21 fact that pinned the gap was renamed and inverted in
  the same task. See this document's Phase 22 item 2 and addendum decision 123. **M6** was fixed outright rather than parked: the guide's
  "arrays" wording described a mechanism (the type-and-name boundary, keyed on `INamedTypeSymbol`) an
  array element write does not go through — it is caught by a separate branch in
  `AnalyzeElementTarget` keyed on `IArrayTypeSymbol` — and `docs/guides/analyzers.md` now says so in
  the same sentence.
- **A deterministic second `StallProxy` stall mode.** **Closed by measurement, not carried**, the way
  Phase 19's decision 96 was closed. Two reasons. The existing stalled fact does discriminate both
  halves of the catch set — Lever 12 re-run in the research failed **2 of 20** consecutive runs,
  confirming the Phase 20 fix wave's 3 of 20 rather than contradicting it. And determinism on that
  surface is obtainable without any stall mode at all: item 4's faulted `ChannelMessageQueue` reaches
  the read loop deterministically, so the remaining value of a second stall mode is a socket-level
  reproduction the unit-level seam supersedes for test purposes.
- **A `SymbolFinder`-gated `readonly` field fix.** Carried unchanged. Nothing measured this round
  bears on it, the arm ships with `CS0191` documented, and no complainant exists. It is the one carried
  item with no new evidence.
- **An STM002 companion shape rule for a `List<T>`-typed managed member.** Carried, with its premise
  corrected in place — see D4. The three samples and every real declaration are positional records
  whose collection members are read-only interfaces, so the ruling stands; the sentence that said
  "zero occurrences … in this repository's samples, tests and documentation" needed "outside the
  analyzer's own test fixtures".
- **An STM003 code fix.** Carried unchanged from decision 103.
- **A multi-node Redis Cluster job exercising `MOVED`/`ASK`.** The single-node configuration exercises
  `CROSSSLOT` and Lua slot validation, which is the whole of the key-layout question. Redirection needs
  at least three masters, a materially bigger CI job, and nothing in the provider reads a `MOVED`
  reply itself — StackExchange.Redis follows them. Sized **L**, not attempted.
- **A `Statesman.Outbox.Redis` key-layout option.** Measured unnecessary: both of its key builders are
  single-key on every operation and it passes 17 of 17 against a cluster unchanged. Adding an option
  there would be surface with no behaviour behind it.
- **`RedisKeyLayout` for the notification channel.** Redis 7 non-sharded pub/sub is not slot-routed, so
  there is nothing to tag. If sharded pub/sub (`SSUBSCRIBE`) is ever adopted this becomes live again.
- **A Tooling migration that renames keys in place.** Rejected above with its reason: no atomic
  primitive under a live writer, and export/import already exists.
- **Extracting the shared `GetSyntaxRootAsync` preamble from `MakeInitOnlyAsync` and
  `MakeReadOnlyAsync`**, and **a second fix-all fact for the `readonly` field arm.** Both unchanged
  from Phase 20.
- **A conformance fact for the Redis dispose race.** Unchanged from Phase 20: the defect is
  Redis-specific in its cause and `ChangeNotifierConformanceTests` already asserts the right thing for
  every provider.
- **`StallProxy`'s orphaned pump task after `Task.WhenAny`, and `DisposeAsync` not awaiting the
  per-connection `ProxyAsync` tasks.** Unchanged from Phase 20: both are in a test-only helper.
- **A separate `Statesman.CodeFixes` NuGet package** (decision 101) and **making any
  `DiagnosticDescriptor` public** (decision 109). Both unchanged.
- **ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 4 — serializer envelopes.** The last remaining 0.2 bullet, and the only one that
  can break a shipped store on disk. `IStateSerializer` is four methods over bytes with no notion of
  content type or serializer identity, so an envelope means either three new `StateRecord` members or a
  framing prefix on `Payload`, touching the eight `src` files that mention `IStateSerializer`, the
  filesystem `FileRecord` JSON, Redis's member-exact opaque bytes across two keys (where member-exact
  identity **is** the record's serialized bytes, so any framing change is also a key-content change),
  the Entity Framework Core columns plus a migration for three engines across six migration packages,
  and `Statesman.Tooling`'s export/import format. Sized **L**, inventoried but not designed. This phase
  makes it **easier**: `RedisKeyLayout` is a worked precedent for "a storage-shape change carried as an
  opt-in option with a legacy default", which is exactly the shape envelopes need.
- **Changing `MaxBytes`'s `continue` to a `break`.** Unchanged from Phase 19 decision 90.
- **Lifting verify and repair into the shared conformance suite.** Unchanged from Phase 16 decision 63.
- **A cross-process filesystem append lock**, and with it multi-process writers. Unchanged from Phases
  11 through 20.
- **A persisted change-log index**, **repairing a `MisplacedStreamDirectory` by moving it**, **deleting
  an `OrphanedTemporaryFile`**, and **rewriting a `MalformedChangeLogLine`.** All unchanged from Phase
  18.
- **A polling `ExpireAsync`** and **a server-side lease lever (`PEXPIRE key 1`)**. Unchanged from Phase
  19 decision 93.
- **Refactoring the three `OrphanedTemporaryFile` loops in `FileSystemLedgerRecovery.cs`.** Closed by
  measurement in Phase 19 decision 96 and **re-confirmed by measurement this phase**: the helper
  compiles and the saving is three lines, not four. Not carried.
- **`MessageId` collision from ordinal `Root` versus canonical lower-casing**, **Redis concurrent
  same-revision `ImportAsync` beyond the documented sentence**, **pipelining the Redis sink**, **an HTTP
  webhook sink**, and **lifting Redis's `MaxImportablePosition`.** All 0.4 or storage-format items.
- **What this phase's pre-flight did not measure**, carried forward as caveats rather than as facts:
  whether the `redis-cluster` job's `docker run` step behaves the same on a GitHub Actions runner as it
  does locally — the container and the exact shell were both measured here, the runner was not, and the
  job's first CI run is the first measurement; STM004's compile-time cost on a consumer materially
  larger than the synthetic 2,914-line, 2,000-mutation-site project the research built, which measured
  the whole analyzer package at about **27 ms of median wall time on a 1.7 s build** with the widened
  prototype adding nothing over what ships today; and the behaviour of the alias walk on a method body
  with hundreds of locals, since `SingleInitializerOf` scans the enclosing block per resolution. The
  Phase 20 caveats about `redis:7-alpine` clusters and the `AddOrphanedTemporaries` helper are **both
  closed by measurement** this phase and are not carried.

#### The public API delta

**Additive, one enum and one property, on a package whose validation gate does check it.**

- `Statesman.RedisKeyLayout`, a public enum with `Legacy = 0` and `SingleSlot = 1`, in
  `Statesman.Persistence.Redis`.
- `Statesman.RedisStateLedgerStoreOptions.KeyLayout`, a settable property of that type, defaulting to
  `Legacy`.

Nothing is removed and nothing changes signature. The gate was measured forced on that package with
`obj/Release` cleared: `APICompat ran successfully without finding any breaking changes.`, `0
Warning(s) 0 Error(s)`. **No `CompatibilitySuppressions.xml` is added or edited.**

`Statesman.Analyzers` changes behaviour without changing surface: three rules widen inside existing
method bodies, and no type, member or descriptor is added. **No capability is added**, so
`docs/architecture/capabilities.md` gains no row and flips no cell, and
`tests/Statesman.Capabilities.Tests/CapabilityMatrixTests.cs` is untouched.
`docs/reference/public-api.md` gains **one sentence** naming the new option, which is the first
provider option that page has ever listed — a deliberate, recorded departure, because a reader looking
for the switch that makes Statesman run on Redis Cluster should find it on the page called "public API
map". There is no API-listing test to update: a search for one across every `.cs`, `.py` and `.yml`
file in the repository returns nothing.


### Phase 22 — Serializer envelopes, the analyzer's last three gaps, and closing ROADMAP 0.2 (2026-09-15)

Phase 21 closed ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 6 and left the recount ending "**Bullet 4 alone remains. 0.2 is not
complete.**" Bullet 4 is serializer envelopes, parked since 0.2 was written, inventoried in Phase 21's
parked list as sized **L** and never designed. The pre-flight research for this phase
(`.superpowers/sdd/2026-09-15-phase-22-research/report.md`, 1,014 lines, every number measured at
`e2802ea`) designed it by prototyping all four options, and the measurement that collapses the bullet is
one line of C#: **`StateRecord` is not a positional record.** `src/Statesman.Abstractions/Ledger.cs:12`
is `public sealed record StateRecord` with a body of `required`/`init` properties and no primary
constructor, and `StateCommit` is the same shape. There is no constructor signature to change, so an
added member is purely additive: 45 projects compile unchanged, 22 packages pack with zero `CP`
diagnostics, and an un-enveloped Redis member is byte-identical to what 0.3.0 wrote.

The research also corrected the spec's own inventory on two counts it could measure. The parked
paragraph says "the Entity Framework Core columns plus a migration for three engines across six
migration packages"; the outbox context persists `StatesmanOutboxCursors` with `OutboxId` and `Position`
only, so it is **untouched** and this is **three** migration packages, not six. And the paragraph names
only `StateRecord`, omitting **`StateCommit`**, the write-side twin every option has to touch as well.
*Amended (Phase 22, 2026-09-15): both corrections are applied in this section's item 4 and item 6;
Phase 21's parked paragraph is superseded by them rather than by a silent rewrite.*

Alongside the envelope, this phase takes the three analyzer items Phase 21's final review left as
candidates (M3, M4 and M5), one one-sentence documentation gap with real user impact that nothing in
the repository had noticed, the Redis `ImportAsync` Lua script addendum decision 50 named as "the only
real fix" and declined to build, and the close-out that declares ROADMAP 0.2 shipped.

Every code block in this section and in this phase's plan was applied to a scratch worktree at
`df50d12`, built, run against SQLite, live standalone Redis, a live single-node Redis cluster with
`SingleSlot`, SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 16, packed with the forced package-validation gate, and
reverted, while the plan was written. Four claims the research or this phase's brief made did **not**
survive that check and are corrected in place below, each marked.

#### 1. The alias hop reaches a top-level-statements file

`SingleInitializerOf` (`src/Statesman.Analyzers/ManagedStateOwnership.cs:156-157`) computes the scope it
rescans for a rebind as `FirstAncestorOrSelf<BlockSyntax>()`, falling back to
`FirstAncestorOrSelf<ArrowExpressionClauseSyntax>()`. A local declared at the top level of a
top-level-statements file has neither: its declarator's ancestors run `VariableDeclaration`,
`LocalDeclarationStatement`, `GlobalStatement`, `CompilationUnit`. The walk therefore answered null
there and **every alias hop was silent in that file shape**, while the direct shapes on the adjacent
lines still reported.

That is not a corner case. `samples/Statesman.Sample.Migration/Program.cs`, the repository's only
analyzer consumer, is itself a top-level-statements file, and so is the shape a reader of the guide's
alias sentence would most plausibly try first.

The fix is one more fallback, to the enclosing `CompilationUnitSyntax`, and the
`ArrowExpressionClauseSyntax` fallback is **deleted in the same edit**. Phase 21's final review recorded
M3 as "looks unreachable: reasoned, not measured". It is now measured twice over. The research parsed
thirteen arrow-shaped C# constructs and found **zero** declarators with a null `BlockSyntax` ancestor
together with an `ArrowExpressionClauseSyntax` ancestor, because an expression body cannot hold a local
declaration statement and a block-bodied lambda inside one supplies its own block. This phase's compile
check confirmed it directly rather than trusting that sweep: **with the arrow fallback deleted and no
replacement, `Statesman.Analyzers.Tests` reports exactly the same `failed: 2` as with it present** —
only the two new top-level rows move, and no shipped fact does.

**The deletion therefore has no possible RED lever, and the plan says so rather than inventing one.** An
unreachable branch cannot be falsified by breaking it. The task's lever is the compilation-unit operand
alone: reverting it to the arrow fallback fires `failed: 2`, exactly the two new rows.

Two rows ship, one per rule, because both rules resolve ownership through the same walk:
`var alias = s.Items; alias.Add(1);` reports STM004 and `var plain = s.Plain; plain.Value = 1;` reports
STM001, both as top-level statements in a file compiled as `OutputKind.ConsoleApplication` with zero
compiler errors. The shared fixture cannot express top-level statements today — Phase 21's review said
so and was right — so `ManagedStateFixture` gains a `TopLevelConsumer` builder and `AnalyzerTestHost`
gains a console-application compile path. That is the whole of the test-infrastructure change.

**The consumer-reach probe, measured in both directions on the real sample.** Adding a
`List<int> Hits { get; init; }` member to the sample's `SessionState` and
`var probeAlias = probe.Hits; probeAlias.Add(1);` to its top-level statements produced
`error STM004: 'probeAlias.Add' mutates the collection held by managed state 'SessionState' …` at
`Program.cs(21,1)` with the fallback in place, and `0 Warning(s) 0 Error(s)` with it reverted. The probe
was then removed and the sample rebuilds clean from a cleared `obj`.

**The guide needs no qualification.** `docs/guides/analyzers.md:47` and its limitations paragraph at
`:80-82` describe the alias hop without reference to block structure or file shape, so both sentences
become true everywhere once the code is fixed. The task re-reads them and records that finding rather
than editing them.

#### 2. STM001 sees a deconstructing assignment into a managed member

`ManagedStateMutationAnalyzer.AnalyzeAssignment` hands `assignment.Left` straight to `AnalyzeMutation`,
whose symbol test admits only `IPropertySymbol` and `IFieldSymbol`. A deconstructing assignment's
left-hand side is a `TupleExpressionSyntax`, which is neither, so **every write a deconstruction
performs was silent**. `s.Scalar = 1` reports and `(s.Scalar, _) = (1, 0)` did not, on the same member.
That reads as a bug rather than as a scope limit, and it is the asymmetry the fix removes.

`AnalyzeTarget` decomposes the target before reporting: a tuple is analysed one element at a time,
recursing so a nested tuple is reached too; a parenthesized target is unwrapped; everything else falls
through to `AnalyzeMutation` unchanged. Five reporting rows ship, covering eight diagnostics between
them, and a ten-shape false-positive sweep ships beside them, all silent with zero compiler errors:
`var (a, b) = (1, 2)`, `(int c, int d) = (3, 4)`, `(e, f) = (5, 6)` into plain locals,
`(local.Value, _)` on an unmanaged holder, `(arr[0], _)`, `(_, _)`, `(s.Hidden.Value, _)` and
`(s.Ig.Value, _)` behind the two escape hatches, `(s.Plain.Exempt, _)` on an ignored member, and
`(p.Items[0], _)` on the unmanaged parameter. The declaration forms stay silent for the reason the rule
already had: they write locals, and an `ILocalSymbol` is neither a property nor a field.

**Corrected in place, against the research and this phase's brief.** The research's prototype carried a
parenthesis arm and this phase's brief directed that its 26-line shape ship verbatim. Measured here, the
parenthesis arm **does not change whether `((s.Child.Value), _) = (1, 0)` reports**: Roslyn answers
`GetSymbolInfo` on a `ParenthesizedExpressionSyntax` with the operand's symbol, so the diagnostic fires
either way. What the arm changes is the **reported span**, which is `(s.Child.Value)` without it and
`s.Child.Value` with it — a squiggle over punctuation the write does not happen at. The arm is therefore
kept, and it is given the only fact that can discriminate it, one that pins the span rather than the
diagnostic. Without that fact the arm would have been a branch this phase deletes elsewhere for exactly
the reason it would have been kept here.

**This overturns a ruling made the week before.** Phase 21's fix wave landed
`A_deconstructing_assignment_into_a_managed_member_reports_STM004_alone_and_never_STM001`
(`tests/Statesman.Analyzers.Tests/ManagedStateCollectionMutationAnalyzerTests.cs:239-257`), whose comment
calls M5 "explicitly parked" and which pins the gap as correct. That fact is **renamed and inverted in
the same task** — it becomes `…_reports_STM001_beside_STM004`, and its comment now says that the Phase
21 parking was overturned here and why. It is also the task's own RED lever's row: restoring
`AnalyzeMutation(context, assignment.Left)` fires `failed: 7`, the five reporting rows, the span fact,
and this one.

*Amended (Phase 22, 2026-09-15):* the Phase 21 "Explicitly parked" entry describing **M5** as pinned
rather than fixed is superseded by this item. The HANDOVER's Phase 21 M5 candidate line gets an in-place
`*Amended (Phase 22)*` pointer in the same task.

A `### Changed` entry records it, in the Phase 20 and Phase 21 style: this widens a shipped rule and can
newly warn code that has not changed.

#### 3. The analyzer guide states its minimum SDK

`global.json` pins SDK `10.0.303`, which ships the Roslyn 5.6.x compiler, and `Statesman.Analyzers`
references `Microsoft.CodeAnalysis` 5.6.0. That pin is **its consumers' compiler floor**, and below the
floor the failure is silent. Measured directly on this machine, reading the file and product versions
off all three installed 10.x SDKs:

| Installed SDK | Roslyn file version | Product version |
|---|---|---|
| 10.0.101 | 5.0.25.57005 | 5.0.0-2.25569.105 |
| 10.0.112 | 5.0.26.42308 | 5.0.0-2.26422.108 |
| **10.0.303** | **5.600.26.37803** | **5.6.0-2.26377.103** |

A consumer on the .NET 10 SDK's **1xx** feature band gets
`CSC : warning CS9057: Analyzer assembly 'Statesman.Analyzers.dll' cannot be used because it references
version '5.6.0.0' of the compiler, which is newer than the currently running version '5.0.0.0'`, then
`0 Error(s)`, a green build, and **zero STM diagnostics**. A quiet build or a `NoWarn` hides the only
signal. `docs/guides/analyzers.md` mentions no minimum SDK, no Roslyn floor and no `CS9057`; the floor is
written down only in `.github/dependabot.yml`, this spec, the handover and `CHANGELOG.md`, none of which
a package consumer reads.

One sentence in the guide names the feature band and the symptom. Note for anyone writing a check: Phase
21's research recorded the analyzer-not-loading failure as `CS8034`; the **version-too-new** case is
`CS9057`, a different diagnostic, so a grep for `CS8034` alone would not catch it.

Whether to *lower* the Roslyn pin to widen the floor is a separate, larger question needing its own
measurement of which 5.6-only API the analyzers use. It is recorded in this phase's parked list, not
attempted.

#### 4. Serializer envelopes: the abstraction

**What the gap is, in the library's own words.** `StateChangeMessage`
(`src/Statesman.Outbox/StateChangeMessage.cs:28-96`) already declares `Format`, `ContentType` ("the
media type of `Payload`") and `Fingerprint` ("the declaration fingerprint the payload was serialized
under"), and its own doc comment states the problem: **"No store persists it, so it comes from
`OutboxOptions.Fingerprint`."** The outbox asserts three envelope facts it cannot verify, because the
ledger does not record them. `ValueType` names the declared .NET type and `SchemaVersion` names the
declaration's version of that type; neither says anything about the encoding, so two serializers
producing different bytes for the same type and version are indistinguishable on the record.

**One public record, one nullable member on each of two types.** `StateEnvelope` ships in
`Statesman.Abstractions`, non-positional with `required`/`init` members, matching `StateRecord`'s own
style rather than the positional shape the research prototyped: it is a public type whose member set may
grow, and a positional record's constructor is a signature the package-validation gate would then hold
forever. It carries `ContentType` and `SerializerId` (both `required`), `Fingerprint` (nullable) and
`FormatVersion` (defaulting to `StateEnvelope.CurrentFormatVersion`, which is 1). `StateRecord` and
`StateCommit` each gain one nullable `StateEnvelope? Envelope`.

**Nullability is the compatibility story, and it is the whole of it.** A record written before this
release reads back with `Envelope = null`; no stored byte moves and nothing migrates. Making the members
`required` instead was measured and rejected: the research's first wave produced **5 distinct `CS9035`
errors across 3 `src` files**, fixing those exposed **30 more across 19 files**, and the build had not
yet reached the Redis, Entity Framework Core and conformance test projects — at least 35 construction
sites in at least 22 files, plus a source break for every consumer who constructs either type.

**`IStateSerializer` gains identity additively.** `SerializerId` and `ContentType` become **default
interface members**, defaulting to the implementing type's full name and to
`application/octet-stream`. Measured: the whole solution builds `45 projects, 0 errors, 0 warnings`, the
forced pack gate on `Statesman.Abstractions` prints `APICompat ran successfully without finding any
breaking changes.` with zero `CP`, and a hand-rolled serializer that implements only the four methods
the interface has always declared still compiles and still reports a non-empty identity. The package
targets net8.0, net9.0 and net10.0, all of which support default interface members, and the two
`netstandard2.0` projects do not reference it.

`JsonStateSerializer` overrides both with stable string constants, `JsonStateSerializer.Id =
"statesman.json/v1"` and `JsonStateSerializer.MediaType = "application/json"`. **These are a persisted
contract from this release on**: records in a consumer's store carry those exact strings, so neither is
renamed, and a future incompatible change to what that type emits ships as a *new* id rather than as a
redefinition of this one. The documentation says so in those words.

**New writes stamp the envelope by default.** One private helper on `StateHandle` builds it, so the
three write points (`TryCommitValueAsync`, `CommitMarkerAsync`, `RecordFaultAsync`) cannot disagree, and
it answers null for a commit with no payload: an envelope describes bytes, and a cleared record has
none. A null-on-write default would make the feature inert and need its own opt-in switch, which the
`RedisKeyLayout` precedent does not argue for — that option exists because the key layout *moves
existing data*, and this does not.

**Nothing validates an envelope on read this phase.** `Statesman.Tooling` carries it verbatim through an
export and a restore. Validation is a second behaviour change on a path whose header fingerprint check
already refuses a mismatched declaration, and it would need its own rejection facts.

**Migration provenance, and why there is no fifth member.** The ROADMAP bullet asks for "content type,
serializer id, and migration provenance". The first two are members; provenance is the `Fingerprint`
plus `FormatVersion` beside the record's existing `SchemaVersion`. This phase's brief asked whether the
`Migrate` path could additionally stamp the schema version it migrated **from**, at S cost. Measured: it
cannot, and not for cost reasons. Migration happens on **read**, in
`RuntimeDefinition.CreateTypedSnapshot` (`src/Statesman/Runtime/RuntimeDefinition.cs:95`), which produces
a `StateSnapshot<T>` — a public record in `Statesman.Abstractions` with no member that could carry
provenance, so carrying it would be a public API addition of its own. Worse, it would be false: the main
write path, `TryCommitValueAsync`, serializes a value the **caller** supplies, which was not necessarily
derived from the migrated one, so stamping "migrated from schema N" on it would assert something the
runtime does not know. The item is recorded here as measured-and-declined rather than left as an open
question.

#### 5. Filesystem and Tooling carry it

`FileRecord` (`src/Statesman.Persistence.FileSystem/FileSystemStateLedgerStore.cs`) gains one property
and two mapping lines, plus one line carrying `commit.Envelope` into the record it builds. A file
written before this release simply has no `envelope` key and reads back null. No migration.

`StateLedgerExportRecord` (`src/Statesman.Tooling/StateLedgerExportFormat.cs`) gains the same. The export
header's `Format` (`statesman.ledger-export/v1`) does **not** bump: restore compares the header string
only, and an added optional record field reads back as null on an old file. Two facts ship, one per
direction of that claim — an enveloped record survives an export and a restore verbatim, and a record
without one writes no `"envelope":` key at all.

*Measured, and worth stating because the compile check caught it:* a test asserting the absence of the
bare word `envelope` in an export is vacuous when the fixture's own state path contains it. The fact
asserts the absence of the property name `"envelope":` instead.

#### 6. Entity Framework Core carries it

`StatesmanLedgerHead` **and** `StatesmanLedgerRecord` (`StatesmanLedgerDbContext.cs`) each gain one
nullable `EnvelopeJson` column — **one JSON column per entity, not four typed ones**. That is the choice
that makes this bullet cheap twice over: two columns instead of eight now, and because the column holds
a JSON document, a fourth or fifth envelope member later needs **no further migration**. One nullable
member also has two states, enveloped or not, where four independently nullable columns would have
sixteen, fifteen of them meaningless.

A generated migration ships in the **three ledger** packages
(`Statesman.Persistence.EntityFrameworkCore.{Sqlite,SqlServer,PostgreSQL}`), produced with the pinned
`dotnet-ef` exactly as `docs/providers/entity-framework-core-migrations.md` describes, one per engine.
The `Initial` migrations are never edited. **The outbox context is untouched, measured**: with both
ledger entities changed, `Statesman.Outbox.EntityFrameworkCore.Tests` is `total: 28 failed: 0` on every
engine.

Migration ids are UTC timestamps taken at generation, so the plan cannot pin them; the implementer
records the three it gets and the close-out carries them into the docs page.

**`EntityFrameworkMigrationDriftTests.cs:62`'s failure message is corrected in the same task.** It tells
a contributor to add a migration to "ALL SIX packages — three engines times two contexts". Following it
literally here would produce three empty outbox migrations whose ids are, by the same message's own next
sentence, "a permanent public contract". It now says "the THREE packages for the context whose model
changed", and names this phase's measurement as the reason.

**Corrected in place, against the research.** The research predicted that omitting one engine's
migration would fire "that engine's drift fact". Measured: it does not. Deleting the SQLite migration's
two files while leaving its model snapshot in place fires **`failed: 1`, and it is
`EntityFrameworkShippedMigrationTests.The_shipped_migration_creates_a_schema_the_store_can_round_trip`,
not the drift fact.** The drift facts compare the model against the committed **snapshot**, which the
generation step also writes; the round-trip fact is what needs the migration itself. Generating no
migration at all, which leaves all three snapshots stale, is the `failed: 4` the research measured (three
drift facts plus the round-trip). Both are real levers and the plan names both, with what each one
proves.

#### 7. The shared conformance suite, and the legacy-read facts

`SerializerEnvelopeConformanceTests` is one shared abstract suite with a subclass per provider —
in-memory, filesystem, Redis, Entity Framework Core and tiered — in the shape
`RecordMetadataConformanceTests` already uses. Four facts: an envelope survives an append and a head
read, a history read, and an exact import, and **a record written without one reads back with none**. The
fourth is as load-bearing as the first three: a provider that materialised an empty envelope, or
round-tripped null through a default instance, would break every record written before this release
without failing anything else.

`BrokenSerializerEnvelopeConformanceTests` is the discrimination proof, in the repository's existing
shape: the shared assertion is run against a wrong double that drops the envelope on write, which must
fail, and against a correct one, which must pass.

`SerializerEnvelopeLegacyReadTests` is deliberately **not** a shared suite, and the plan says why: there
is no shared contract to express, because each provider's pre-envelope shape is a property of its own
storage format and the point of each fact is the literal bytes. Six facts: one per provider, plus one
pinning that the seeds are addressed at the stream hash both the filesystem and Redis derive from
`StateAddress.Canonical`. **Every seed was captured by running the pre-envelope tree at `df50d12` and
dumping what it wrote**, not hand-written from the model — a hand-written seed proves only that the test
author and the reader agree. The filesystem seed is the captured `head.json` and history file byte for
byte; the Redis seed is the captured member text, written into the head string and the history sorted
set; the Entity Framework Core seed is a raw insert of the eighteen columns that release knew about,
leaving the nineteenth to the NULL the migration gives existing rows; tiered is seeded through its
filesystem cold store, which is authoritative for reads and is the only honest version of the fact for a
provider with no storage format of its own; and in-memory is the degenerate case, stated rather than
skipped, because that store holds the `StateRecord` object itself.

**The discrimination proof for all six is one edit**, and the plan states it exactly: add an
`"envelope":{…}` key to the seeded bytes and a non-null `EnvelopeJson` to the seeded row. Measured: all
five provider facts then fail their `Assert.Null`. A seed that still passed would be asserting nothing.

*Measured, and corrected against the plan draft:* the Entity Framework Core seed's raw SQL must
double-quote every identifier. PostgreSQL folds an unquoted identifier to lower case and answers
`42P01: relation "statesmanheads" does not exist`; SQL Server and SQLite accept the quoted form
unchanged. The defect was found by running the suite against live PostgreSQL, not by reading the code.

#### 8. Redis carries it, and the ordering trap is a named step

`RedisRecord` (`src/Statesman.Persistence.Redis/RedisStateLedgerStore.cs`) gains one property, two
mapping lines and one line carrying `commit.Envelope` into the record the append builds — **and the
property is declared before `GlobalPosition`, which is load-bearing rather than tidiness.**

`GlobalPosition` is declared last on purpose: `PositionPrefix` trims the trailing `0}` off
`"globalPosition":0}` so `AppendScript` can supply the position it allocates, and throws loudly if that
property is no longer last. `System.Text.Json` emits properties in declaration order and
`DefaultIgnoreCondition.WhenWritingNull` omits a null one, so **a nullable property declared after
`GlobalPosition` is invisible while it is null and becomes the document's last property the moment it is
populated.** Measured with the member deliberately misplaced: every Redis fact that does not write a
populated envelope still passes, and an append that carries one throws

```
InvalidOperationException: The Redis provider serializes 'globalPosition' as the last JSON property so
that the append script can supply the position it allocates. That property is no longer last; the
document ends ':"fingerprint-redis","formatVersion":1}}'.
```

The guard therefore needs a fact that **writes a populated envelope**, and this phase ships one that
asserts both halves: the stored member contains `"envelope":{` **and** still ends `"globalPosition":1}`.
A second fact pins byte identity for the un-enveloped case across both keys member identity spans, which
is what makes this change free of any data migration on Redis.

*Corrected in place, against this phase's brief.* The brief states that a round-trip test alone does not
catch the trap. That is true of an **import**-based round trip, which does not go through
`PositionPrefix`, and it is how the research measured it. It is **not** true of this phase's shared
conformance suite: with the member misplaced, `RedisSerializerEnvelopeConformanceTests` goes `failed: 2`
on its two appending rows, while its import row still passes. The dedicated Redis fact earns its place
anyway, because it pins the *mechanism* — `globalPosition` last in the stored bytes — rather than only
the round trip, and because it names the failure a reader would otherwise have to diagnose.

#### 9. The Redis `ImportAsync` Lua script

Addendum decision 50 documented a hazard on the condition-guarded transaction at
`RedisStateLedgerStore.cs:350-405` and took option (i), one documented sentence, naming option (ii), one
Lua script, as "the only real fix". Two concurrent imports of the same revision each read the history
member they are about to replace, each pass the revision-key condition, and the second never learns
about the member the first added — so history ends with one member and the change feed ends with
**two**, a record in the feed whose history entry is gone.

Two things changed since that ruling. The script is now feasible **on a cluster**: `SingleSlot`, shipped
in Phase 21, puts every key of one store in one hash slot, which is exactly what made `AppendScript`
legal there. And `PruneAsync`'s own comment already records the member-identity reasoning the script has
to preserve, so the invariant is written down rather than needing rediscovery.

`ImportScript` performs every read and every write of one import in a single server-side step, replacing
an `AdvanceGlobalPosition` `EVAL`, three read round trips and the condition-guarded retry loop. The
member-identity reasoning is preserved exactly: the change feed is cleaned **member-exact**, by the stale
member's own bytes, never by score, because a score range over that key cannot tell one address's member
from another's; the score-range removal stays on the history key, where one member per revision score
holds by construction. No arithmetic happens on positions — the global-position high-water comparison is
the same length-then-lexicographic decimal-string test `AdvanceGlobalPositionScript` already uses,
because Lua 5.1 numbers are IEEE doubles. The two places a number is parsed, the revision guard and the
partition high-water mark, are bounded well inside exact double range, the second by
`MaxImportablePosition` (2^52) which the method enforces before calling.

**The fact, and how its interleaving is forced without timing.** A `DispatchProxy` over `IDatabase`
pauses the first import immediately after its stale-member read returns, on a
`TaskCompletionSource` rather than a delay; a second import then runs to completion through an
unproxied store against the same server; only then is the first released. There is no sleep and no
timing assumption. The pause point is precisely the window a condition-guarded `MULTI`/`EXEC` import
has between its read and its write — and once the import is one server-side script **that window does
not exist**: `ImportAsync` issues no sorted-set read at all, the gate never trips, and the two imports
serialize on the server. That absence is the fix, and the fact is written with a `Task.WhenAny` so it
proceeds whether the gate trips or the first import simply finishes.

**Measured, both directions, ten consecutive runs each.** With `ImportAsync` reverted to the
condition-guarded transaction the fact fails `Assert.Equal(1, feedMembers)` with `Actual: 2` on
**10 of 10** runs; with the script it passes on **10 of 10**. Every pre-existing Redis fact, including
every import-exactness row of the shared conformance suite, is `failed: 0` with the script, against
standalone Redis and against the single-node cluster with `SingleSlot` alike.

#### 11. The outbox message reads the record's envelope

*Added 2026-09-15, after Tasks 1 through 4 had landed. It is numbered **11** because items 1 to 10 were
already written and their task numbers already dispatched, and it is placed **here**, before the
close-out, because that is where it ships: as **Task 9b**, between the Redis import script and Task 10.*

**The finding.** Task 4's reviewer noticed, and the controller verified against
`src/Statesman.Outbox/StateChangeMessage.cs`, that `StateChangeMessage.FromRecord` never reads
`record.Envelope`. Its `ContentType` came from `OutboxOptions.PayloadContentType` and its `Fingerprint`
from `OutboxOptions.Fingerprint`. So from the moment item 4 landed, a record could carry an envelope
saying one thing while the message published from it asserted another, and **nothing in the repository
would have failed**. That is the same class of defect as the payload-frame option this phase rejected on
measurement: a change the test suite cannot distinguish from a no-op.

It is worth stating how it was missed, because the omission was not subtle. This phase's own research
named the outbox as **the clearest consumer of bullet 4** — quoting the type's own doc comment, *"No
store persists it, so it comes from `OutboxOptions.Fingerprint`"*, as the plainest statement of what the
bullet buys — and then the outbox appeared in neither the plan's production-file list nor this section's
parked list. The spec's item 4 fixed the ledger's half of the gap the outbox documents, and left the
outbox's half untouched.

**The design, additive throughout.** When `record.Envelope` is non-null the message takes its content
type and its fingerprint from the envelope; when it is null both fall back to the options exactly as
before, so every record a consumer's store already holds produces byte-identically the message previous
releases produced. Two finer points are decisions rather than consequences:

- **A null `Fingerprint` *inside* a populated envelope still falls back to the option.** A null there
  means the writer had no declaration to name, not that the outbox's configured fingerprint is wrong,
  and losing the option's value would discard information for nothing. It has its own fact.
- **`ContentType` keeps its documented invariant, "null exactly when there is no payload", and the new
  `SerializerId` joins it.** Both describe bytes, and a record with no payload has none. The invariant
  is a wire contract, and the fact that has guarded it since Phase 7 still does: the lever that removes
  the payload gate fires that fact as well as the new one.

**`StateChangeMessage` gains one nullable `SerializerId`, and the brief left that open.** It is added.
`StateChangeMessageFormat.Json` sets `DefaultIgnoreCondition.WhenWritingNull`, so a null property is
absent from the document — measured by a fact that serializes both a legacy and an enveloped message —
which makes the addition additive on the wire and leaves `StateChangeMessageFormat.Version` at
`statesman.state-change/v1`. It earns its place because `ContentType` alone does not identify an
encoding: the type's own remarks say the payload "is not necessarily JSON", and two serializers can both
answer `application/json` while producing different bytes. That is exactly what the envelope exists to
disambiguate, and the outbox is where a consumer outside this repository finally sees it. It has **no**
`OutboxOptions` fallback on purpose — a serializer id the writer did not record is not something the
outbox can honestly assert, and adding an option for it would recreate the defect this item fixes.

**`OutboxOptions` is not changed**, neither its two members nor its validation. They remain the fallback
and keep their defaults; `StateChangeDispatcher`'s call site is untouched.

**Measured**, in a scratch worktree at `d1fd032`: `Statesman.Outbox.Tests` rises from **83** to **90**
with seven new facts, `failed: 0`; `Statesman.Outbox.Redis.Tests` is `17` / `failed: 0` against live
Redis, which is the regression check that the dispatcher-to-sink path still produces what the sink
expects; the forced package-validation gate on `Statesman.Outbox` prints `APICompat ran successfully
without finding any breaking changes.` with zero `CP` and `0 Warning(s)  0 Error(s)`. Each of the four
levers fires on the exact facts named for it, and the payload-gate lever additionally fires the
pre-existing Phase 7 row.

The doc comment sentence that said the content type "is supplied from
`OutboxOptions.PayloadContentType`" is **rewritten rather than annotated**: it asserts a mechanism that
no longer holds, and leaving a false statement in a consumer-facing doc comment beside an amendment
pointer would be worse than either alternative. `docs/guides/outbox.md`'s message table is corrected in
the same task, and a `### Changed` entry records it.

#### 10. Close-out

`ROADMAP.md`'s 0.2 bullet 4 declares serializer envelopes shipped and the recount declares the **0.2
milestone complete**, with the date. Two exit-criterion clauses are amended in the same pass, and both
are rulings rather than code:

- **Criterion 1**, "all built-in providers pass one conformance suite", is declared **met**. Addendum
  decision 98 parks general corruption conformance beyond import refusal *by design*, on the reasoning
  that each provider's failure shape is a property of its own storage format, and Phase 16 decision 63
  keeps filesystem verify and repair per-provider for the same reason. Per-provider corruption coverage
  therefore **satisfies** the criterion, and the wording says so explicitly rather than leaving the
  ruling implied. The alternative, a shared corruption suite, contradicts a standing decision and is
  sized **L**.
- **Criterion 3**, "CI builds every supported target framework", is narrowed to the measured truth. CI
  builds net8.0, net9.0 and net10.0 for the thirteen cross-targeting packages on three operating
  systems, and **tests net10.0 only**, because `tests/Directory.Build.props:6` is the only place any
  test project declares a framework. Literally the sentence is true; in substance no behaviour is ever
  tested on net8.0 or net9.0 on any operating system. The honest phrasing is that CI builds every
  supported target framework and tests one of them. A net8.0/net9.0 **test-run** job is recorded as a
  Phase 23 candidate, not attempted here.

Both Phase 21 caveats are **retired with numbers rather than carried a third time**. STM004's
compile-time cost on a materially larger consumer: a generated 23,819-line, 350-type, 14,000-site
consumer costs **53 ms of median wall time on a 2.2 s build, about 2.4%**, against Phase 21's 27 ms on a
1.7 s build at one-eighth the size, so wall-time cost is strongly sub-linear; per-analyzer, STM004 is
1.519 s of a 1.828 s total and scales linearly with mutation sites, and the 1,400 alias hops cost about
0.29 s, roughly 0.2 ms each. The alias walk on a method body with hundreds of locals: **flat**, a
500-local body costs about **10 ms** more than a zero-local one, and the adversarial N-independent-alias
shape is roughly 0.8 to 1.2 ms per alias including compilation, every alias correctly reported.

The `MessageId` casing item's premise is **corrected in place** in the parked list. The spec's recorded
reason, that "it cannot be scoped small", is about **data migration and not code size**, and that
distinction was never written down. Making `StateAddress.Canonical` ordinal is a one-expression change:
the whole solution builds and exactly **four** facts fail, in two projects, because no test in the
repository uses two differently-cased roots. The real cost is that filesystem per-address directory
names and Redis stream keys are both a SHA-256 of `address.Canonical`, so flipping it renames every key
and directory for any consumer whose root is not already lower-case, with no in-place migration. Sized
**S** for code and **L** for the migration story, and recorded as a 0.4 storage-format item.

#### Explicitly parked, with reasons

Rebuilt from Phase 21's list. Everything Phase 22 ships or closes is dropped from it rather than
restated.

- **A shared corruption-conformance suite**, beyond the import-refusal half the shared suite already
  covers. Unchanged from addendum decision 98, and now load-bearing for a ruling: this phase declares
  ROADMAP 0.2's first exit criterion **met** on the basis that per-provider corruption coverage
  satisfies it by design. Sized **L**. **Lifting verify and repair into the shared suite** stays parked
  with it, unchanged from Phase 16 decision 63.
- **A net8.0 and net9.0 test-run job. Phase 23 candidate, recorded here with its reasoning.** CI builds
  all three target frameworks for the thirteen cross-targeting packages and tests **net10.0 only**,
  because `tests/Directory.Build.props:6` is the only place a test project declares a framework. A
  net8.0-only regression, a conditional-compilation branch or a BCL behaviour difference would compile
  green and ship. Making the test projects cross-target is not a workflow edit; it is a change to every
  test project's framework set, with whatever follows for the packages that are net10.0-only.
- **Lowering the `Microsoft.CodeAnalysis` pin to widen the consumer compiler floor.** This phase
  documents the floor (item 3) rather than moving it. Moving it needs its own measurement of which
  5.6-only API `Statesman.Analyzers` and `Statesman.CodeFixes` actually use, and it is a consumer-facing
  decision about which SDK feature bands are supported, not a dependency bump.
- **`MessageId` collision from ordinal `Root` versus canonical lower-casing. A 0.4 storage-format item,
  with its premise corrected.** The blocker is **data migration, not code size**: the code is one
  expression and exactly four pinning facts in two projects, measured, and the whole solution builds;
  filesystem per-address directory names and Redis stream keys are both a SHA-256 of
  `address.Canonical`, so flipping it renames every key and directory for any consumer whose root is not
  already lower-case, with no in-place migration. The alternative fix, normalizing `Root` at
  construction, instead changes Entity Framework Core row keys, which key off the raw columns. Sized
  **S** for code and **L** for the migration story. It is a strictly larger break than the envelope,
  which takes none, so joining them would have made a non-breaking release breaking.
- **Lifting Redis's `MaxImportablePosition`. A 0.4 storage-format item.** `1L << 52`, enforced because
  the change feed is a sorted set scored by position and Redis scores are IEEE doubles, exact only to
  2^53; the same reasoning is recorded again for `PruneAsync`. Lifting it means the feed can no longer
  be ordered by a numeric score, which is a redesign of the Redis change feed's storage. Six tests and
  one conformance comment reference the constant. Sized **L**. The documented workaround, restoring a
  filesystem export into in-memory, filesystem or Entity Framework Core instead, is present in the
  exception message itself.
- **Validating an envelope on read, and `Statesman.Tooling` restore validating one.** This phase carries
  the envelope; it does not check it. Validation is a second behaviour change on a path whose header
  fingerprint check already refuses a mismatched declaration, and it needs its own rejection facts and
  its own ruling about what a mismatched serializer id should do to a read.
- **A payload framing prefix**, the envelope design this phase rejected on measurement rather than
  taste. Recorded because the measurement is worth keeping: a `STM1`-magic frame around every payload
  fails **zero** facts across ten test projects while changing the on-disk byte shape of every payload,
  shifting `PayloadLength` and therefore `MaxBytes` retention admission by 40 bytes per record, making a
  downgrade impossible, and breaking every 0.3.0 reader with `JsonException: 'S' is an invalid start of
  a value.` at byte position 0. A design the test suite cannot distinguish from a no-op is the one to be
  most suspicious of.
- **Option (iii), full data flow for STM004.** Unchanged: option (ii) closes the shape users actually
  write, and per-method-body `DataFlowAnalysis` buys aliases through fields and calls at a
  false-positive cost nothing in this repository can measure.
- **The `event` add/remove asymmetry**, documented and pinned rather than fixed. Unchanged from Phase 21
  decision 116.
- **A `SymbolFinder`-gated `readonly` field fix**, **an STM003 code fix**, **an STM002 companion shape
  rule for a `List<T>`-typed managed member**, **a separate `Statesman.CodeFixes` NuGet package**
  (decision 101), and **making any `DiagnosticDescriptor` public** (decision 109). All unchanged from
  Phases 20 and 21.
- **A multi-node Redis Cluster job exercising `MOVED`/`ASK`**, **a `Statesman.Outbox.Redis` key-layout
  option**, **`RedisKeyLayout` for the notification channel**, and **a Tooling migration that renames
  keys in place.** All unchanged from Phase 21, each with its measured reason.
- **A deterministic second `StallProxy` stall mode.** Closed by measurement in Phase 21 decision 117 and
  not carried.
- **Changing `MaxBytes`'s `continue` to a `break`** (Phase 19 decision 90), **a cross-process filesystem
  append lock** (Phases 11 through 21), **a persisted change-log index**, **repairing a
  `MisplacedStreamDirectory` by moving it**, **deleting an `OrphanedTemporaryFile`**, **rewriting a
  `MalformedChangeLogLine`** (all Phase 18), **a polling `ExpireAsync`** and **a server-side lease lever**
  (Phase 19 decision 93), **extracting the shared `GetSyntaxRootAsync` preamble**, **a second fix-all
  fact for the `readonly` field arm**, **a conformance fact for the Redis dispose race**, and
  **`StallProxy`'s orphaned pump task**. All unchanged.
- **The Phase 21 caveats are not carried.** STM004's compile-time cost on a materially larger consumer
  and the alias walk's cost on a method body with hundreds of locals are both **closed by measurement**
  in item 10 and retired. The third, whether the `redis-cluster` job behaves on a GitHub Actions runner
  as it does locally, was answered by Phase 21's own first CI run and is not a Phase 22 item.
- **What this phase's pre-flight did not measure**, carried forward as caveats rather than as facts:
  whether the current majors of the nine GitHub Actions in use are current, which the research had no
  verified network source for; and whether newer container images (Redis 8, PostgreSQL 17 or 18) would
  change any measured behaviour, which would want its own cluster run because the single-node
  slot-assignment shape is what Phase 21 verified.

#### The public API delta

**Additive throughout: one type, two properties, and two default interface members.**

- `Statesman.StateEnvelope`, a public sealed record in `Statesman.Abstractions`, with `ContentType` and
  `SerializerId` (`required string`), `Fingerprint` (`string?`), `FormatVersion` (`int`, defaulting to
  `StateEnvelope.CurrentFormatVersion`), and the public constant `CurrentFormatVersion = 1`.
- `Statesman.StateRecord.Envelope` and `Statesman.StateCommit.Envelope`, settable-on-init properties of
  type `StateEnvelope?`, both defaulting to null.
- `Statesman.IStateSerializer.SerializerId` and `Statesman.IStateSerializer.ContentType`, **default
  interface members**, so no existing implementation breaks. `JsonStateSerializer` overrides both and
  exposes their values as the public constants `JsonStateSerializer.Id` (`statesman.json/v1`) and
  `JsonStateSerializer.MediaType` (`application/json`), which are a **persisted contract** from this
  release onward.
- `Statesman.Persistence.EntityFrameworkCore.StatesmanLedgerHead.EnvelopeJson` and
  `StatesmanLedgerRecord.EnvelopeJson`, `string?` properties on the two public entity types.
- `Statesman.Tooling.StateLedgerExportRecord.Envelope`, a `StateEnvelope?` property.
- `Statesman.Outbox.StateChangeMessage.SerializerId`, a `string?` property. *Added 2026-09-15 with
  item 11.* It is additive on the wire as well as in the API: `StateChangeMessageFormat.Json` omits a
  null property, so a message built from a record with no envelope is byte identical to what previous
  releases wrote, and `StateChangeMessageFormat.Version` does not bump. `OutboxOptions` is unchanged.

Nothing is removed and nothing changes signature. The forced gate was measured on all seven affected
packages with `obj/Release` and `bin/Release` cleared: `APICompat ran successfully without finding any
breaking changes.`, `0 Warning(s)  0 Error(s)`, zero `CP` diagnostics. **No `CompatibilitySuppressions.xml`
is added or edited.**

`Statesman.Analyzers` changes behaviour without changing surface: two rules widen inside existing method
bodies, and no type, member or descriptor is added. **No capability is added**, so
`docs/architecture/capabilities.md` gains no row and flips no cell, and
`tests/Statesman.Capabilities.Tests/CapabilityMatrixTests.cs` is untouched. `docs/reference/public-api.md`
gains a short paragraph naming `StateEnvelope` and the two serializer members.


## Testing

ROADMAP 0.2's shared provider conformance suite doesn't exist yet; this plan builds the minimal
version needed for 0.3:

- One conformance test base class per capability interface (e.g. `LeaseProviderConformanceTests`),
  inherited by every provider test project that implements that capability.
- A capability-matrix test asserting `docs/architecture/capabilities.md` matches actual
  `TryGetCapability` results across all shipped providers, so the doc can never silently drift from
  the code. This must include a completeness check (reflection over all `IStateCapability`
  implementers and all `IStateLedgerStore` implementations in the referenced assemblies) — a
  per-cell check alone passes silently when a new capability or provider ships without a matching
  row/column, which is the actual drift risk each later phase introduces.
- Each phase's PR(s) must leave `main` green (full CI: build, test, pack, analyzers, docs) before
  the next phase begins.

## Non-goals (unchanged)

This design does not turn Statesman into a distributed transaction coordinator, message broker, or
event-sourcing framework, per `ROADMAP.md` § "Deliberate non-goals." `StatesmanRemoteClient`
remains an operations client, not a transparent distributed `IState<T>` proxy
(`docs/guides/hosting.md:36`) — nothing in this design changes that boundary.

## Open questions for implementation planning

- Exact `StateChangeEnvelope` / `StateChangeCursor` / `StatePartitionDescriptor` /
  `StateReplicationLag` field shapes — deferred to the per-phase implementation plan so they can be
  test-driven rather than speculated here.
- Naming of the import/export and outbox packages.
- Whether EF Core's serializable-transaction capture (Phase 4) needs per-relational-provider
  (SQL Server / PostgreSQL / SQLite) isolation-level caveats documented separately, given
  `docs/providers/index.md:47`'s existing note that "lock and isolation behavior differs by
  relational database."

## Addendum (post-Phase-0, 2026-09-03)

Phase 0's final whole-branch review surfaced a real gap this design had not addressed: capability
discovery through wrapping stores (`TieredStateLedgerStore`) and through the universal-but-not-
directly-implemented `IStateChangeFeed`. Both are now resolved above (`IStateCapabilityProvider`
for wrapping stores; a resolution-time decorator for the universal feed) — recorded here rather
than silently rewritten into the original sections, so the history of why they exist is visible.
`[NotNullWhen(true)]` was added to `TryGetCapability`'s signature for the same reason: it was a
correctness gap the design's original code sample didn't have, caught by the same review.

## Addendum (post-Phase-1, 2026-09-03)

Phase 1's mechanism-placement correction (leasing lives in `StatesmanRuntime.MaintainAsync`, not
`StatesmanHostedService`) and the never-renewed-lease and forwarding-policy gaps are recorded above
in the Phase 1 section rather than here, since they're corrections to that section specifically.
Two process notes worth keeping: (1) a plan-authoring bug (the lease's `await using` scope ending
before the work it protected) was caught by a per-task review that read the code rather than the
plan, and neither the original test nor the plan's own text would have caught it — a durable
lesson for writing lifetime-sensitive tests in later phases: assert what happens *during* the
protected window, not just a terminal count. (2) The EF Core lease's uncaught
`DbUpdateConcurrencyException`/insert-PK-violation race (parked, not fixed, in Phase 1) needs to be
resolved before Phase 4's EF Core distributed-capture work, which will hit the same class of
concurrent-transaction-failure handling gap.

## Addendum (pre-Phase-2, 2026-09-03)

Phase 2 planning research found the original Phase 2 design above (a generic decorator over
`ReadHistoryAsync`) was not feasible as specced — `ReadHistoryAsync` is single-stream, and a change
feed is a cross-stream query only EF Core's schema already supports. Presented to the user as a
genuine scope decision (full per-provider native cross-stream indexing vs. a cheaper but
structurally-incomplete runtime-layer merge over already-observed addresses vs. reordering Phase 3
ahead of Phase 2): **full per-provider native implementation was chosen**, recorded in the revised
Phase 2 section above. This turned out to also simplify the design: no resolution-time decorator
means no need for that decorator to separately implement `IStateCapabilityProvider`, which removes
the interaction risk this document's Phase 1 addendum had flagged.

## Addendum (post-Phase-2, 2026-09-03)

Phase 2's final whole-branch review found two Critical, cross-provider issues that no single
per-task review could see (each only looked at one provider). Both were real. One was fixed before
merge; the other is a genuine design gap, documented here rather than papered over, because the
still-unplanned `IStateChangeNotifier` half of this phase and any future outbox adapter (Phase 7)
depend on knowing exactly what guarantee this feed does and doesn't make.

**Fixed before merge, verified directly (not just claimed):** `TieredStateLedgerStore`'s default
`ReadMode` (`ValidateCold`) calls `ImportAsync` on the hot replica on every read that hits cold, and
`ImportAsync` was unconditionally appending to the change feed on InMemory and FileSystem — turning
ordinary reads through the default tiered configuration into an unbounded feed-growth leak.
Confirmed empirically, not just by reading the diff: temporarily reverting the `isNewPosition` guard
and re-running the regression test showed a triple `ImportAsync` of the same record producing three
change-feed entries instead of one. Both providers now only append when the import genuinely
introduces a new `GlobalPosition`, and both gained a regression test pinning this.

(The controlling session briefly and incorrectly concluded, mid-session, that this finding was a
"false positive": it lost track of an already-running fix-wave agent across a context-compaction
event, read that agent's uncommitted in-progress edit off the shared working-tree disk, and mistook
it for pre-existing history — then wrote that incorrect conclusion into the spec and dispatched a
second, different fix-wave agent for a narrower scope (a real `ReadAsync` file-sharing fix and a
real `CancellationToken.None` fix, both independently verified against the code and genuinely
needed — see below) without realizing the first agent was still active on the same file. Both
agents' edits were real, not fabricated, and turned out to be compatible; the second agent's edits
were deliberately reverted, not because they were wrong, but to let the first agent land its
already-further-along, independently-verified fix cleanly first — the second agent's fixes were
redone against that clean baseline afterward. Recorded here as a process note: losing track of
a long-running background agent across compaction, then re-deriving conclusions from a shared
working tree without checking who else might be mid-edit on it, produced a confidently-wrong
"already correct" claim — verify empirically and account for concurrent agents before writing a
correction into permanent documentation.)

**Not fixed, tracked as an open design question — resolve before building anything on top of this
feed:** `GlobalPosition` is allocated before the record is durably visible on four of five
providers (Redis: `INCR` then a transaction round-trip later; FileSystem: an in-memory counter then
two fsync'd writes later; InMemory: `Interlocked.Increment` then an enqueue moments later — only EF
Core allocates and commits the position inside the same transaction). A consumer reading while
position *N*'s append is still in flight but *N+1*'s has already landed sees only *N+1*, persists
that as its cursor, and can never see *N* — it will land below the consumer's watermark on the next
read. This is the standard change-data-capture "low water mark" problem, and as shipped, none of
`IStateChangeFeed`'s doc comment, the provider docs, or the tests say the feed can do this.

A real fix is either a stability window (only yield positions older than some settle interval, or
below the lowest currently-in-flight position — needs each provider to track in-flight allocations)
or moving position allocation to commit time everywhere (which EF Core already does, but doing the
same for Redis/FileSystem/InMemory means giving up the per-address write concurrency those
providers currently have — a real trade-off, not a small patch). Until one of these ships:
`IStateChangeFeed` consumers must treat the feed as **at-least-once with possible tail loss under
concurrent writes to different addresses**, not lossless — the spec's "push accelerates, feed is
authoritative" framing for `IStateChangeNotifier` (below) needs to be revisited against this before
that follow-on plan is written.

**Resolved in Phase 8 (2026-09-07).** Commit-time allocation shipped on every affected provider; see
the "Phase 8 — Change-feed hardening (lossless feed)" section above for the mechanism and the
"Addendum (pre-Phase-8, 2026-09-07)" below for the precise meaning "authoritative" now carries. The
paragraph above stands as the record of the gap, not as current behaviour.

**Also flagged by the same review, lower priority, tracked but not blocking:**
- **Retention/prune interaction is inconsistent and mostly undefined.** Pruning a stream deletes
  it from `ReadHistoryAsync` but each provider's change feed reacts differently: EF Core's feed
  silently loses the pruned records (it queries the same table `PruneAsync` deletes from);
  FileSystem's index file keeps a now-dangling line forever (silently skipped on read, never
  cleaned up); Redis's and InMemory's change-feed structures aren't trimmed at all and retain
  everything forever regardless of the store's own retention policy — for InMemory this is a real
  regression, since `MaxRevisions`/`MaxBytes` existed specifically to bound memory and the change
  feed now defeats that bound. Needs a decided, documented, and provider-consistent semantic (own
  follow-up).
- **`TieredStateLedgerStore` advertises `IStateChangeFeed` even in a hypothetical composition where
  its cold store couldn't back it** — `TryGetCapability<T>`'s direct-cast-first behavior means
  capability negotiation says yes unconditionally, and only a `NotSupportedException` on the first
  actual read would reveal a real gap. Not reachable with any of today's five providers (all
  implement the capability), but the same shape will recur for `IPartitionCatalog` (Phase 3) and
  needs an architectural answer then: either let `IStateCapabilityProvider` veto a direct cast, or
  have `TryGetCapability<T>` consult the provider before the cast when a store implements both.
- **No paging/batch limit on `IStateChangeFeed.ReadAsync`.** Only EF Core streams the underlying
  query; Redis and InMemory materialize the full since-cursor result before yielding. FileSystem
  already avoids the worse version of this — it sorts lightweight `(position, address, revision)`
  tuples first and re-reads each full record lazily inside the yield loop
  (`FileSystemStateLedgerStore.cs:294-338`), so it only holds the small tuples in memory, not full
  deserialized records — but it still reads the entire index file per call regardless of cursor. A
  consumer resuming after being offline a long time — the primary use case for a durable feed —
  pulls its entire backlog into memory in one call today. Worth deciding
  before any external consumer exists, since adding a batch parameter to a shipped capability
  interface later is a breaking change.
- `StateChangeCursor` carries no store identity, so a cursor obtained from one store can be passed
  to a different store's `ReadAsync` with no error, silently returning the wrong slice. Consider a
  store fingerprint if this proves to be a real footgun in practice.

**Genuinely fixed, empirically verified (commit `e37b4a7`):** `FileSystemStateLedgerStore.ReadAsync`
was not taking `_changeFeedGate` before reading the index file — the same gate
`AppendChangeFeedEntryAsync` already holds while writing to it — a real Windows file-sharing race
(broader than the Task 5 per-task review's "narrow Import-repair" framing: any concurrent read plus
any write). `ReadAsync` now acquires the gate around the file-exists check, read, and line-parsing
loop, releasing it before the second loop that does the actual yielding. A concurrent
`AppendAsync`/`ReadAsync` stress test reliably reproduced the exact pre-fix `IOException` (5/5 manual
runs against the unpatched code) and now passes consistently. `AppendAsync`/`ImportAsync` were also
passing the caller's `CancellationToken` into the change-feed append after the primary record write
had already durably committed — a cancellation in that narrow window made an already-successful
write look like it failed to the caller — both call sites now use `CancellationToken.None` for that
specific, already-committed-work append.

These caveats belong in `docs/providers/index.md`'s change-feed section (added as part of this
phase's final fix wave) so they're discoverable by users, not just by whoever reads this spec.
## Addendum (pre-Phase-8, 2026-09-07)

The open design question the post-Phase-2 addendum parked — `GlobalPosition` allocated before the
record is durably visible on four of five providers — is being resolved by the Phase 8 section
above, by commit-time allocation on every affected provider. The addendum's own text stays as
written; read it as history, and read Phase 8 for what ships.

The consequence for `IStateChangeNotifier`, which that addendum said needed revisiting first: the
spec's "push accelerates, feed is authoritative" framing now has a precise meaning, and it is this.

> The feed is **lossless within retention**: a consumer that resumes from the cursor of the last
> record it accepted eventually sees every record the store still retains at a higher position.
> A notification is a **latency hint only** — it is a signal to poll the feed now. It carries no
> delivery guarantee and may arrive for a record the feed will not yet yield, because a lower
> position is still in flight. A consumer that polls on a notification and sees nothing must treat
> that as normal and poll again; it must never treat the notification's own payload as delivery, and
> it must never advance its cursor from one.

That is compatible with ADR 0004 as written: notification is still not delivery, and the feed is
still what makes delivery durable. What changes is that "authoritative" is now a property a test can
assert rather than a word, so the `IStateChangeNotifier` follow-on plan is no longer blocked on
defining it.

## Addendum (pre-Phase-10, 2026-09-08)

Decisions taken while writing the Phase 10 section and its implementation plan. **As in Phases 8 and
9, they were taken without `AskUserQuestion` because the session ran under an autonomous `/goal`.**
Four are worth revisiting if that ruling was wrong, and each is listed with what reversing it would
cost.

1. **`RenewAsync` never resurrects an expired lease, even when nobody else took it.** Redis's
   behaviour became the contract and Entity Framework Core changed to match, rather than the reverse.
   The argument is that `AcquireAsync` already treats an expired row as free, so a lease that renews
   across expiry produces two live handles for one lease id. The cost of this ruling is that a slow
   holder whose renewal arrives one millisecond after its TTL loses a lease nobody was contending
   for, and must re-acquire — which the outbox's `EnsureLeaseAsync` now does automatically, so the
   cost lands on a direct `IStateLease` caller rather than on the shipped consumer. Reversing it means
   changing Redis, which would mean re-implementing expiry in a Lua script against the store's own
   clock instead of the server's; that is why this direction was chosen and not merely preferred.

2. **`IStateChangeFeed.ReadAsync` takes a required `StateChangeReadOptions` — a breaking change.** An
   additive second method was rejected because it leaves the unbounded read as the path of least
   resistance, and because a defaulted parameter cannot precede `CancellationToken` without breaking
   positional callers anyway. The window for this is `0.4.0-alpha` before a 0.4 consumer exists, and
   it closes on its own. If the ruling is wrong the cost is real: every external implementation of
   `IStateChangeFeed` and every external call site breaks at compile time, with no runtime surprise
   and no silent behaviour change, and thirty-five in-repo call sites move in one commit. The related
   sub-ruling worth naming separately is that **`Take` bounds records yielded rather than entries
   parsed**, which is what stops a filesystem page of nothing but pruned entries from reading as "the
   feed is exhausted" and stalling an outbox cursor permanently.

3. **The filesystem change-log append fsyncs whenever `FlushToDisk` is on, with the throughput
   measurement as a hard gate.** No second option was added, because the docs tell durability as one
   lever and the existing caveat is written as a caveat *of* `FlushToDisk`. The measurement is not
   advisory: the implementer records 200-append cost at both option values before and after, and
   **reports BLOCKED rather than proceeding if `FlushToDisk = true` append cost more than triples**,
   at which point reversing to an opt-in `FlushChangeLogToDisk` is a decision with data behind it.
   The related admission is that the fsync itself is not black-box provable — nothing in this
   repository proves `AtomicWriteAsync` fsyncs either — and the plan says so rather than shipping a
   timing assertion dressed as a mechanism proof.

4. **The unbounded in-memory and Redis feed growth stays parked, and is named the top Phase 11
   candidate.** It is a genuine defect — `MaxRevisions` and `MaxBytes` exist to bound memory and do
   not bound it on two providers — and it was left undone specifically because the coherent fix
   ("feed retention follows stream retention", making all four providers uniform) *changes the
   published feed semantics of the two providers that currently retain everything*. Today
   `docs/providers/index.md` tells operators that Redis and the in-memory provider have no prune loss;
   the fix would make that false. Trading a documented no-loss property for a documented memory bound
   is a product decision, so it is flagged here rather than taken quietly inside a close-out phase.

One thing this phase deliberately did **not** decide: nothing in Phase 10 is a new capability, so the
"Forwarding policy (decided before Phase 7, 2026-09-05)" classification list is unchanged,
`docs/architecture/capabilities.md` gains no row, and no cell flips.

## Addendum (pre-Phase-11, 2026-09-09)

Decisions taken while writing the Phase 11 section and its implementation plan. **As in Phases 8, 9
and 10, they were taken without `AskUserQuestion` because the session ran under an autonomous
`/goal`.** Seven are worth revisiting if that ruling was wrong, and each is listed with what
reversing it would cost.

1. **Feed retention follows stream retention, unconditionally.** The rule is "a record leaves the
   change feed exactly when its history record leaves the store", and it is taken as a behaviour
   change rather than hidden behind a knob. This is the decision Phase 10 explicitly deferred to the
   maintainer, and it is taken here because the alternatives are not honestly implementable: an opt-in
   `TrimChangeFeed` flag would be **silently ignored on three of five providers** — Entity Framework
   Core's feed *is* its history table and the filesystem provider's pruned history file is simply
   gone — which is what the capability honesty rule and Phase 10's "implemented exactly or not at all"
   ruling forbid; and a feed bound independent of stream retention invents a second retention
   vocabulary and admits a new loss shape, a record dropped from the feed while its history survives.
   The mitigation is real and not rhetorical: `StateRetentionPolicy.KeepAll` is the default with every
   bound null, so a default-configured store loses nothing, and the change is observable only to a
   deployment that configured a memory bound and is not getting it. The window is `0.4.0-alpha` with
   no 0.4 consumer, and Phase 10 already took a breaking change to this interface in it. **Reversal
   cost:** two statements of production code and one in-memory structure, plus re-flipping six
   documentation locations. The structure change is the part that would not simply revert, because
   the read now takes a lock it did not take before.

2. **The filesystem page-scan regression is fixed in Phase 11, not deferred again.** It is a
   regression Phase 10 introduced and documented rather than a pre-existing limitation, and the
   standing instruction for this effort is that incomplete work ships rather than waits. Keeping it
   parked would leave a documented "raise `BatchSize`" workaround standing for a cost the library
   creates. **Reversal cost:** none in code — deferring it again would simply have meant a smaller
   phase — but the documentation would keep telling operators to tune around a defect.

3. **The incremental read is an in-process memo inside `FileSystemStateLedgerStore`, not a
   cursor-carried byte offset.** `StateChangeCursor` stays a bare `long`, and no cursor store, file
   format, Redis value or Entity Framework Core column changes. The cursor form is more general and
   survives a process restart, but it breaks four `IOutboxCursorStore` implementations and one
   persisted JSON shape, pushes a store-specific field into a type documented as portable, and cannot
   help the case that motivates it — a cursor read back after a restart carries a previous process's
   view of the file. The memo also shortens the window `_changeFeedGate` is held, which the cursor
   form would not. **Reversal cost:** the memo is private to one class and can be deleted, restoring
   the previous read verbatim; the tests written for it would be deleted with it.

4. **Redis prune becomes atomic between history and feed through one `ITransaction`**, rather than two
   sequential `SortedSetRemoveAsync` calls. Prune is already a non-atomic read-then-remove against a
   concurrent append, so the transaction is not closing that window; it is stopping a reader from
   seeing a feed that still serves a record the history no longer holds. The two keys span hash slots,
   so prune becomes standalone-only — the shape `ImportAsync` and `CaptureAsync` on this provider
   already document, so no new Redis Cluster constraint is added. The removal is member-exact `ZREM`
   and never `ZREMRANGEBYSCORE`, because sorted-set scores are IEEE doubles exact only to 2^53, which
   is the Phase 6 lesson `MaxImportablePosition` already encodes. **Reversal cost:** two lines — the
   transaction becomes two awaited calls again, at the price of a visible half-pruned window.

5. **The in-memory feed becomes an `ImmutableSortedSet<FeedEntry>`, published under `_feedLock` and
   read lock-free.** Two smaller designs were rejected, one on paper and one by running it. A
   `ConcurrentDictionary` keyed by position is the smallest diff and is wrong on correctness: its
   enumeration is not a snapshot, so a reader could observe position P+1 without P and a paging
   consumer would advance its cursor past P forever — a lossless-within-retention violation
   introduced by a change meant to bound memory. A mutable `SortedSet` read under `_feedLock` was the
   plan's first design; the Task 1 implementer found that it turns the read into a wait on the
   writer's critical section, and that two existing in-memory tests which pause a writer inside that
   lock and drain meanwhile deadlock rather than fail — they pin a documented, in-memory-specific
