   guarantee stronger than the shared one (a drain during an in-flight append sees a prefix,
   immediately). The immutable set keeps that guarantee for the same reason the queue had it — a
   reader works on a structure that is never mutated — and buys O(log N) insert and remove.
   `System.Collections.Immutable` ships in the shared framework on every target, so the core
   package's zero-dependency rule holds. **Reversal cost:** returning to a lock-free *mutable*
   structure means returning to a queue, which cannot be trimmed — so reversing this means reversing
   decision 1 as well; returning to the locked mutable set means rewriting the two tests to accept a
   blocking read, which is a weaker guarantee than the one they document.

6. **The scan-cost figures get a committed, env-gated measurement harness before the fix lands.** The
   2.3 / 18.5 / 83.7 ms figures in two documents have no artifact behind them, and Phase 10's fsync
   task already established that a performance claim carries a before and an after. Figures published
   in documentation are labelled as taken on the development machine rather than as a contract, and
   the harness asserts nothing on wall-clock time — a threshold on a shared CI runner is a flake, not
   a gate. **Reversal cost:** deleting one test class; the cost of not having it is that "the fix
   worked" would again be unprovable, which is the pattern this phase exists to stop repeating.

7. **Six items are parked with named homes rather than left implicit**, and the list is written with
   the phase rather than after it: the Entity Framework Core test-provider seam bundled with its SQL
   Server / PostgreSQL CI job and the cross-address append race (one deliverable, Phase 12);
   `_changes.log` compaction, which is the actual fix for filesystem feed *storage* growth that
   decision 1 does not address (Phase 12, and decision 3's memo is built to survive it);
   cross-process filesystem append locking (unchanged Phase 10 ruling); `MessageId` casing (0.4
   design); `IsolationLevel.Snapshot` (needs a server engine, so it pairs with the seam);
   and `ManualTimeProvider.CreateTimer` (orthogonal, small). **Also parked and newly documented
   rather than fixed:** re-importing an existing revision at a *different* position leaves feed
   residue on three providers — Redis keeps a changes member with no history twin, the in-memory
   provider keeps the old entry, and the filesystem provider's old log line now dereferences to the
   rewritten history file so the record yields twice. It is the colliding-lineage restore
   `docs/providers/index.md:167` already describes; Phase 11 adds a comment at each import site and
   one sentence in the providers doc. **Reversal cost of the parking, collectively:** each item is a
   later phase's work rather than a decision that has to be undone; the one that would cost something
   to have got wrong is the import residue, because documenting a defect rather than fixing it is only
   defensible while the scenario stays the unsupported one the docs say it is.

Nothing here is a new capability: the "Forwarding policy (decided before Phase 7, 2026-09-05)"
classification list is unchanged, `docs/architecture/capabilities.md` gains no row and flips no cell,
and `CapabilityMatrixTests.cs` is untouched.

## Addendum (pre-Phase-12, 2026-09-10)

Decisions taken while writing the Phase 12 section and its implementation plan. **As in Phases 8, 9,
10 and 11, they were taken without `AskUserQuestion` because the session ran under an autonomous
`/goal`.** These fourteen are the ones to revisit if that ruling was wrong, and each is listed with
the alternative rejected and what reversing it would cost. (The first nine were written before
Phase 12 began; decisions 10 onward were added by Task 8 as the phase's own record of decisions
taken mid-execution, once their evidence existed — see each decision's dated note.)

1. **Compaction is a method on `FileSystemStateLedgerStore` — not a capability, not an option, and
   not a `Statesman.Tooling` command.** *Rejected: a capability interface.*
   `CapabilityMatrixTests.cs` reflects over every `IStateCapability` in the abstractions assembly
   (`Capability_and_provider_lists_are_complete`, `:69`), so a one-provider maintenance capability
   forces a row in `docs/architecture/capabilities.md` with four honest "No" cells — which is exactly
   what the capability-honesty rule exists to prevent. *Rejected: an auto-compact option*, because
   `StateHandle` prunes after every successful append (`docs/providers/index.md:185`) and an
   auto-compactor on that path would make an unrelated address's append wait out a whole-file rewrite
   under `_changeFeedGate`, turning append latency bimodal for a storage concern that is not urgent.
   *Rejected: a Tooling command*, because `Statesman.Tooling` references only
   `Statesman.Abstractions` (`Statesman.Tooling.csproj:8`) and — the decisive reason — because the
   append is two file opens (`FileSystemStateLedgerStore.cs:722-775`), so an external compactor could
   rename between a writer's tail check and its append. **Reversal cost:** the method is additive on
   a concrete class; moving it to a capability later is a new interface plus a matrix row, with this
   method kept as its implementation. Nothing has to be undone.

2. **Compaction inherits the single-writer rule rather than weakening it.** The provider is already
   documented single-writer (`docs/providers/index.md:23`: *"only one process may append to a given
   store's directory"*). *Rejected:* making compaction safe for multi-process writers, which would
   require the cross-process position allocator that has been parked since Phase 10. **Reversal
   cost:** none now — but it means cross-process writers can never be taken without re-opening
   compaction's design, which is a coupling that did not exist before this phase and is worth writing
   down.

3. **The three change-log read opens gain `FileShare.Delete`** (`FileSystemStateLedgerStore.cs:838`,
   `:790`, `:1076`), as do the two new opens compaction and the generation sidecar add. *Rejected:*
   leaving them and retrying the rename with backoff, which makes compaction fail on Windows for as
   long as any cross-process reader is active — that is, permanently, for the deployment shape the
   change log exists to serve. **Reversal cost:** three flags; but the pre-fix failing evidence would
   have to be deleted with them and the Windows failure comes back. The CI matrix runs Windows
   (`ci.yml:188`), which is what would catch a regression. *Amended (Task 4, 2026-09-10):*
   `FileShare.Delete` on these opens is still necessary — a destination replace needs it from every
   live handle — but it is not sufficient by itself: `File.Move(overwrite: true)` still fails with
   `ERROR_ACCESS_DENIED` against an open handle regardless of the flag. See decision 13 for the
   POSIX-semantics rename that actually closes this.

4. **Index invalidation across compaction becomes correct by construction, via a `_changes.gen`
   seqlock.** *Rejected:* relying on "strictly shorter output with no duplicate lines". The witness
   that kills that argument is constructible: two byte-identical log lines, which a ping-pong import
   produces — import `(P1, A, R)`, then `(P2, A, R)`, then `(P1, A, R)` again, and the first and
   third lines match byte for byte, because `ImportAsync` appends a line whenever the existing
   revision file's `GlobalPosition` differs from the incoming one (`:259`). With two identical lines
   a compaction can shift one into the other's offset and the tail re-verify at `:857-862` passes on
   stale data. **The honest accounting, which the plan's proof reflects:** the compactor's
   duplicate-collapse rule already removes that witness, so with both rules shipped the failure
   cannot be demonstrated — the demonstration needs the collapse rule disabled *as well as* the
   generation check. The seqlock is therefore defence in depth, and what it actually buys is that the
   property no longer depends on an arithmetic argument over the current line format and the current
   collapse rule. A future change to either cannot silently reintroduce the hole. The comment at
   `:828` already concedes that a same-length rewrite of an earlier line is undetected; this makes
   that concession unnecessary rather than louder. **Reversal cost:** one sidecar file and two small
   reads per feed read; deleting it restores the previous read verbatim and reinstates the
   dependency on duplicate collapse. **This is the decision in this addendum most worth a
   maintainer's second look**, because it is the only one whose benefit is not directly observable.

5. **Compaction collects the filesystem import residue by dropping any line whose history file
   carries a different `GlobalPosition`.** *Rejected:* an existence-only predicate, which is cheaper
   (one `File.Exists` per line, no deserialization) but leaves the duplicate yield
   `docs/providers/index.md:179` documents — and leaves it in the one place where the two envelopes
   disagree with themselves, the `Cursor` carrying the stale position while `Record.GlobalPosition`
   carries the rewritten one. **Reversal cost:** one clause; but the providers doc goes back to
   describing a defect on three providers instead of two, and the memoized history-file read that the
   clause requires would be deleted with it.

6. **The Entity Framework Core test seam is a linked shared file under `tests/Shared/`, not a
   `Statesman.Testing` type.** *Rejected:* shipping it in `Statesman.Testing`, which would put a SQL
   Server package dependency into a published package for a test-only concern — the same reasoning
   `tests/Shared/PausingTimeProvider.cs:21-25` already records. **Reversal cost:** moving one file
   and adding a package reference, if the seam later turns out to be something consumers want.

7. **`SnapshotDistributed` maps per provider: `IsolationLevel.Snapshot` on SQL Server,
   `RepeatableRead` on PostgreSQL, `Serializable` on SQLite and anything else.** *Rejected:* keeping
   `Serializable` everywhere on the grounds that it is strictly sufficient — true, but it is the
   reason the SQLite write stall exists and the reason the capability's name does not match its
   behaviour. *Also rejected:* `Snapshot` unconditionally, which PostgreSQL does not have at all.
   This introduces the codebase's **first provider-conditional branch**, and it ships only if the
   discriminating test can show the difference: a reader inside a `SnapshotDistributed` capture
   observing a pre-transaction value while a concurrent writer commits, where the same test under
   `Serializable` blocks or aborts. If that cannot be shown, the mapping does not change.
   **Reversal cost:** one switch expression at `EntityFrameworkStateLedgerStore.cs:300-306`, and the
   fixture's `ALLOW_SNAPSHOT_ISOLATION` DDL deleted with it. *Amended (Task 3, 2026-09-10):* the
   discriminating test showed more than a latency difference: under `Serializable` on SQL Server, a
   key-range lock taken for one address does not cover another, so a `SnapshotDistributed` capture
   could return one address's pre-write revision beside another's post-write revision — a torn
   multi-address view under the name of a point-in-time snapshot. This makes the mapping change a
   correctness fix rather than a preference; see the corrected Phase 12 item 3 paragraph above.

8. **`ManualTimeProvider.CreateTimer` ships, but lease-renewal cadence stays untestable.**
   `StateChangeDispatcher` measures renewal with `Stopwatch.GetTimestamp()` (`:213`, `:238`), not with
   `TimeProvider`, so a virtual timer does not reach it. *Rejected:* converting the dispatcher in the
   same task, which is a behaviour change to the Phase 10 persistent-leader mechanism and deserves its
   own review. Two smaller rulings inside the override, both documented on the member: callbacks are
   invoked **outside** `_gate`, because a callback that calls `Advance` while `Advance` holds the
   monitor deadlocks; and a `period` of `TimeSpan.Zero` is treated as **one-shot** rather than as a
   fire-as-fast-as-possible loop, because an unbounded fire loop inside `Advance` is a hang rather
   than a test result. `PeriodicTimer` validates `period > 0` and never reaches that branch.
   **Reversal cost:** none for the parking, which is a named Phase 13 candidate rather than a
   decision; the `TimeSpan.Zero` ruling is one line and one doc sentence.

9. **SQL Server's 900-byte clustered index key limit is documented and boundary-tested, not designed
   around.** Probed against `mcr.microsoft.com/mssql/server:2022-latest` while this section was
   written: the limit is a **`CREATE TABLE` warning, not a DDL failure** — *"The index
   'PK_StatesmanHeads' has maximum length of 1792 bytes. For some combination of large values, the
   insert/update operation will fail"* — and the failure is at INSERT, `Msg 1946`, when an actual key
   exceeds 900 bytes. An 888-byte key inserts; a 908-byte key does not. *Rejected:* reducing
   `HasMaxLength` on `Root`/`Path`/`Partition`, or replacing the composite key with a computed hash.
   Both are schema changes to packages that ship no migrations at all, taken on the strength of a
   warning that no realistic address reaches. What ships is the limit stated in
   `docs/providers/index.md` and `CHANGELOG.md`, plus one gated SQL Server test pinning the boundary
   in both directions. **Reversal cost:** if a consumer does hit it, the fix is a migration they own
   plus a `HasMaxLength` change here — and the boundary test is what would tell them which column to
   shorten. **The reason this is a decision rather than a finding:** the research report predicted a
   hard DDL failure on the first SQL Server run, and that prediction was wrong; the plan is sized for
   the real behaviour.

10. **The Entity Framework Core append AND import allocate their global position first, with an
    atomic increment, and both transactions run at `ReadCommitted`.** The `global-position` row's
    exclusive lock is taken as the append transaction's first statement and held to commit, which
    makes it a global mutex with a single lock order; a single lock order cannot deadlock. *Rejected:
    the Phase 12 item 3 fix as originally planned* — a bounded retry on `DbUpdateConcurrencyException`
    inside the existing `catch (DbUpdateException)` — for two independent reasons, both measured.
    First, the diagnosis was wrong: SQL Server does not raise `DbUpdateConcurrencyException` here at
    all, it deadlocks on a **key-range lock over `PK_StatesmanHeads`** that two different addresses
    share when their keys fall in the same index gap, and the deadlock graph
    (`DBCC TRACEON(1222)`) names that index and shows both processes converting `RangeS-S` to
    `RangeI-N`. Second, the retry could not have fired even if the diagnosis had been right: EF Core's
    provider execution strategy classifies the failure as transient and rethrows it wrapped in
    `InvalidOperationException`, which `catch (DbUpdateException)` does not match — so that branch is
    dead code on this path today. *Also rejected: allocating first but keeping
    `IsolationLevel.Serializable`*, which fixes SQL Server and leaves PostgreSQL broken: a blocked
    `UPDATE` under `REPEATABLE READ` or `SERIALIZABLE` aborts with `40001` when it unblocks, because
    the holder committed after the blocked transaction's snapshot, so *N* concurrent appenders cost
    *O(N)* retries — instrumentation counted twelve retries in one run of a suite with four
    concurrent writers, and it still failed at five attempts. *Also rejected:
    `SELECT … FOR UPDATE` / `WITH (UPDLOCK)`*, which has no provider-neutral LINQ form and would need
    raw SQL branching on `ProviderName` or a provider package reference, and this package references
    `Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore` and `.Relational` only. **What this costs:** one extra round trip
    per append (the read-back after the increment), and appends serialize from their first statement
    rather than from their commit — a small addition, because the row's write lock already serialized
    every append at commit time. **What it buys beyond the fix:** position order becomes exactly
    commit order, which is strictly stronger than the Phase 8 mechanism it replaces, and positions
    stay dense because a rejected append rolls its increment back. **Reversal cost:** one method and
    four private helpers in one file, no schema, model or signature change; reverting restores the
    previous `AppendAsync` verbatim and reinstates both engine failures, which the three acceptance
    tests would show immediately. Reverting only the `ReadCommitted` half lands in the measured
    middle position — green on SQL Server, starving on PostgreSQL. *Extended in the same phase (fix
    round 1).* Allocating first gave `AppendAsync` the order `sequence → head/record`, the reverse of
    `ImportAsync`'s `record → head → sequence` — a lock-order inversion this phase introduced, not a
    pre-existing one, reproduced deterministically on both server engines (SQL Server deadlock graph:
    `PK_StatesmanSequences` and `PK_StatesmanHeads`; on PostgreSQL the append's retry absorbed the
    deadlock and the only symptom was a silently burned position). `ImportAsync` now takes the
    position row first too, by an atomic conditional max-advance (`Value = MAX(Value, position)` via
    `ExecuteUpdateAsync`, run as the transaction's first statement), drops to `ReadCommitted` for the
    same measured reason as the append, and gets the same bounded retry (the shared constant is now
    `MaxWriteAttempts`) widened by one case: `DbUpdateConcurrencyException`, because at read-committed
    a concurrent `PruneAsync` can delete the record an import is replacing between its read and its
    write, and re-running the idempotent import inserts it. Measured: `Serializable` never protected
    that case — it failed the import outright on PostgreSQL (`40001`) and blocked the prune for the
    full command timeout on SQL Server. `PruneAsync` (no explicit transaction, one table) and
    `AcquireAsync` (disjoint `StatesmanLeases`, stays `Serializable`) were checked and need no
    reorder. *Reversal cost:* one method and one private helper; reverting reinstates the SQL Server
    deadlock and the PostgreSQL position burn, which three gated tests show.

11. **The bounded retry stays, at three attempts, for one measured case; `DbException.IsTransient` is
    one of its triggers and not its mechanism.** The only race that reaches the retry in normal
    operation is the one-time creation of the sequence row in an empty store, and it is
    PostgreSQL-only: SQL Server's locking `ReadCommitted` blocks the second appender's scan on the
    uncommitted insert, while PostgreSQL's MVCC does not, so several appenders insert and all but one
    lose the unique index with `23505`. One retry always settles it, for any number of concurrent
    bootstrappers, because the second attempt finds the row — which is what makes three a bound
    rather than a guess. Detection there is **structural**, by where the failure happened, because
    `23505` is not classified as transient by any provider. *Rejected: `DbException.IsTransient` as
    the transience test*, measured and found half-broken: Npgsql reports `IsTransient = true` and
    `SqlState = "40001"`, but `Microsoft.Data.SqlClient` reports `IsTransient = **false**` and a
    **null** `SqlState` for error 1205, the deadlock victim — even though EF Core's own
    `SqlServerExecutionStrategy` classifies that same exception as transient. It is kept as one
    trigger because it costs four lines, needs no provider reference and correctly absorbs a
    PostgreSQL `40001`/`40P01`, but the design deliberately does not depend on it. *Rejected:
    `IExecutionStrategy`*, because the default strategy does not retry and so fixes nothing for a
    consumer who has not opted in, and because a retry inside one strategy invocation reuses the same
    `DbContext` and would need the change tracker cleared between attempts. **Reversal cost:** four
    lines for the transience test; deleting the retry itself reinstates a deterministic PostgreSQL
    failure on the first two concurrent appends into any new store.

12. **PostgreSQL's microsecond timestamp resolution is pinned by its own gated test, and the two
    exact round-trip tests are seeded from a microsecond-aligned clock rather than skipped.**
    `timestamptz` is a microsecond count and a .NET tick is 100 ns, so `OccurredAt`, `FreshUntil`,
    `ServeUntil` and lease `ExpiresAt` lose their last digit on that engine. *Rejected:
    `Assert.Skip` on PostgreSQL for the two `ProviderRoundTripTests` cases*, which is what the plan
    anticipated: it retires the whole export/restore round trip on that engine — three records, both
    directions, history, heads, positions and payloads — to work around one field's last digit, and
    the tests' own stores already accept a `TimeProvider`, so generating values every shipped
    provider can hold costs six lines and loses nothing. *Rejected: truncating on write inside the
    provider*, which degrades SQLite and SQL Server, both of which store the full tick. *Rejected: a
    `long` ticks column*, which is a schema change to a package that ships no migrations — the same
    reasoning that kept the 900-byte key shape in decision 9. Checked rather than assumed: nothing in
    the library compares these values at finer than microsecond resolution, and `AcquireAsync`,
    `EntityFrameworkLease.RenewAsync` and `EntityFrameworkLease.DisposeAsync` each re-read the lease
    row before writing, so the truncated `ExpiresAt` concurrency token is never sent back stale; the
    outbox cursor store compares no timestamps at all. **Reversal cost:** one test-local
    `TimeProvider` and one gated test; deleting them makes two Tooling tests fail on PostgreSQL again
    for a documented reason.

**Named for Phase 13, not decided here.** Neither Entity Framework Core package supports
`EnableRetryOnFailure`: every method opens its own transaction, and a retrying execution strategy
rejects that. Measured — 19 of 24 tests in `Statesman.EntityFrameworkCore.Tests` fail with *"The
configured execution strategy 'SqlServerRetryingExecutionStrategy' does not support user-initiated
transactions."* The fix routes five methods through
`context.Database.CreateExecutionStrategy().ExecuteAsync(…)` and has real semantics to get right, so
it is a second production change with its own test surface. Phase 12 documents the limitation
instead.

13. **The change log and the generation sidecar are replaced by a POSIX-semantics rename on Windows,
    through this package's first P/Invoke.** Measured during Task 4:
    `File.Move(…, overwrite: true)` — `MoveFileEx(MOVEFILE_REPLACE_EXISTING)` — fails with
    `ERROR_ACCESS_DENIED` against a destination **any** process holds open, whether or not that handle
    granted `FILE_SHARE_DELETE`. Decision 3 was written on the opposite assumption. The classic
    replace has to remove the destination's name from the directory, and while a handle is open the
    file system can only mark the file delete-pending and leave the name in place.
    `SetFileInformationByHandle(FileRenameInfoEx, REPLACE_IF_EXISTS | POSIX_SEMANTICS)` is the
    primitive that detaches the name immediately; it is what `rename(2)` has always done and what this
    provider's readers already assume. *Rejected: `File.Delete` then `File.Move`, and its two-rename
    variant.* Both work, both need `FILE_SHARE_DELETE` just the same, and — decisively — both have the
    **same** platform floor, because `File.Delete` only frees a held name where POSIX delete is
    implemented. What they add is a window in which `_changes.log` does not exist, during which a
    cross-process `ReadAsync` returns an empty page and `ListPartitionsAsync` returns an empty list
    with no retry signal; the two-rename variant closes the crash hole but not the reader hole, and
    pays for it with a second reserved file name and a repair step the compactor's own design
    disclaims. *Rejected: `ReplaceFileW`*, which works but is a compound operation specified for
    attribute preservation, not crash atomicity. *Rejected: retrying the rename with backoff*, which
    against a permanently-attached reader is a hang rather than a failure.
    **Reversal cost:** delete two `DllImport`s and one helper and call `File.Move(overwrite: true)`
    directly — which restores the Windows failure this decision exists to remove, so the reversal is
    only available together with dropping cross-process readers.

14. **D4 (the in-memory change feed's positional-indexer read) is measured, not fixed — the
    enumerator walk it was researched to replace was reverted.** The Phase 11 final review's argument
    was that `snapshot[position]`'s O(log N) positional lookup, paid once per yielded record, is worse
    than the set's own O(1)-amortized enumerator; measured at 41 ms for 200,000 records. *Rejected:
    the enumerator-walk fix as researched*, because it measures **slower**, not faster, on this
    runtime: a controlled 5-run comparison in both Debug (34.22–35.68 ms → 39.03–41.07 ms) and Release
    (32.59–36.82 ms → 40.08–44.33 ms) configurations at 200,000 records shows the enumerator
    consistently 13–30% slower than the indexer it would replace, with the deep-page case flat in
    both. The indexer's log factor is not where the drain's time goes on this .NET runtime — plausibly
    `ImmutableSortedSet<T>.Enumerator`'s constant-factor overhead, unverified. **What ships instead:**
    a gated measurement harness, `tests/Statesman.Tests/InMemoryChangeFeedDrainMeasurement.cs`
    (`STATESMAN_MEASURE_INMEMORY_DRAIN=1`), so the claim is checkable rather than assumed, and the
    indexer read is kept byte-identical to before. **Reversal cost:** none — nothing shipped was
    reverted; re-attempting the enumerator walk starts from the harness this decision leaves in place.

## Addendum (pre-Phase-13, 2026-09-11)

Decisions taken while writing the Phase 13 section and its implementation plan. **As in Phases 8
through 12, they were taken without `AskUserQuestion` because the session ran under an autonomous
`/goal`.** These are the ones to revisit if that ruling was wrong, and each is listed with the
alternative rejected and what reversing it would cost. Decisions 1 through 16 were written before
Phase 13 began; anything numbered above that was added by the close-out task as the phase's own
record of decisions taken mid-execution, once their evidence existed.

1. **The four transactional methods run through `ExecuteAsync`, not `ExecuteInTransactionAsync`.**
   *Rejected: the `ExecuteInTransactionAsync` family.* Measured by reflection over
   `Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore` 10.0.11, it has exactly three overloads: one taking an operation
   and a `verifySucceeded` predicate; one adding a state argument; and one adding a
   `beginTransaction` factory on top of that. The first two open the transaction at the provider's
   **default** isolation level with no parameter for one, and three of the four methods depend on a
   specific level Phase 12 measured as load-bearing:
   `ReadCommitted` for append and import (decision 10) and `SnapshotIsolationLevel(context)` for
   capture (decision 7, a correctness fix). Only the six-argument overload could preserve them, and
   at that point it is `ExecuteAsync` plus a `verifySucceeded` predicate this design does not need —
   the lost-acknowledgement case already resolves to the documented `Conflict`. **Reversal cost:**
   the delegate bodies are unchanged either way; swapping the wrapper is one line per method, but the
   isolation levels would have to move into a `beginTransaction` factory and both engine behaviours
   decision 10 measured would be back in play.

2. **The bounded `MaxWriteAttempts` loop stays outside; the execution strategy goes inside; the
   change tracker is cleared as the delegate's first statement.** The two layers own disjoint failure
   classes and must not be merged: the outer loop owns the sequence-row bootstrap race
   (`TryAllocatePositionAsync` returning `null` after a lost `23505`, PostgreSQL-only, detected
   structurally by where it happened because `23505` is classified transient by no provider) and, for
   import, the prune-during-import `DbUpdateConcurrencyException`; the strategy owns provider-
   classified transient connection failures. *Rejected: deleting the outer loop on the grounds that
   the strategy subsumes it* — the default `NonRetryingExecutionStrategy` retries nothing, so a
   consumer who has not opted in would lose the bootstrap retry entirely, which is a deterministic
   PostgreSQL failure on the first two concurrent appends into any new store. *Rejected: strategy
   outside, loop inside*, which would put the strategy's re-invocations around a loop that creates a
   fresh `DbContext` per iteration and leave the outer-most layer unable to retry the context
   creation. The change-tracker clear is what makes one reused context safe, and it is the exact
   hazard decision 11 named when it rejected `IExecutionStrategy` for Phase 12. **Reversal cost:**
   removing the clear reinstates the duplicate-insert hazard, which the plan's break-the-mechanism
   lever demonstrates; removing the loop is a deterministic PostgreSQL regression. *Amended (Task 1,
   2026-09-11):* the loop's necessity is not PostgreSQL-only either — measured, deleting it is a
   deterministic regression on SQL Server too (4 of 4 runs), not only PostgreSQL. See decision 18.

3. **The delegate's transient filter rolls back and rethrows rather than looping, and keeps its
   `attempt < MaxWriteAttempts` guard.** *Rejected: swallowing the transient exception inside the
   delegate and returning a retry signal*, which would give the outer loop first refusal and leave
   the execution strategy with nothing to retry — the feature would be theatre. *Rejected: dropping
   the attempt guard from the delegate's filter*, which changes behaviour under the default strategy:
   today, on the final attempt a transient `DbUpdateException` falls through to
   `catch (DbUpdateException)` and can return `Conflict`, and an unguarded filter would throw instead.
   **Reversal cost:** one filter clause; the cost of getting it wrong is a caller-visible change from
   `Conflict` to an exception on the third attempt, which no existing test covers because the case
   needs a live engine under load.

4. **`AcquireAsync` returns a live handle when the lease row carries this caller's own token, and the
   token is generated once per call rather than once per attempt.** Without the hoist the fix cannot
   work: a replayed delegate would generate a fresh token and fail to recognise its own write.
   *Rejected: leaving it*, on the grounds that the failure direction is safe — a spurious `null` means
   "did not get the lease" and the row expires at `ttl`. It is safe, but it is also wrong, it becomes
   reachable only because this phase makes retrying strategies work, and the fix is four lines inside
   a branch the method already has. *Rejected: putting the comparison only in the
   `catch (DbUpdateException)` re-read*, which is where the research placed it: traced against the
   code, a replay after a lost acknowledgement re-reads the row inside a **new** transaction and
   takes the live-lease branch at the top of the method, never reaching that catch. The comparison
   goes in both places, and the plan states which one the test exercises and why the other is
   symmetric. **Reversal cost:** delete two comparisons and the hoist; `AcquireAsync` returns `null`
   for a lease this caller holds, which one gated test would show.

5. **The retry test surface is one environment variable on the existing seam, plus one extra step
   inside the two existing server CI jobs.** *Rejected: a retry-on/off by three-engine matrix*, which
   doubles two already-slow jobs for no additional signal; the same three projects run twice inside
   the jobs that already have the containers up. *Rejected: a second test project*, which would
   duplicate every fixture. **Reversal cost:** one YAML block and one branch in
   `EntityFrameworkTestProvider.Configure`.

6. **The deterministic single-retry proof is PostgreSQL-only, and says so in its own comment.**
   Npgsql's `PostgresException` has a public four-argument constructor
   `(messageText, severity, invariantSeverity, sqlState)`, verified by reflection, so a transient
   `40001` can be constructed by a test; building a `SqlException` needs reflection over a private
   constructor. *Rejected: writing the test for both engines by reflecting a `SqlException` into
   existence*, which pins the test to `Microsoft.Data.SqlClient` internals that no contract covers.
   *Rejected: claiming symmetric coverage.* SQL Server's coverage is the whole-suite run under
   `STATESMAN_TEST_EF_RETRY`, which is weaker — it proves nothing failed, not that anything retried —
   and the test comment says that rather than implying parity. **Reversal cost:** none; adding the
   SQL Server half later is additive.

7. **`OptionsWithRetryOnFailure<TContext>` is kept and gains a `configure` parameter rather than
   being deleted.** The research's sizing said to delete it once `Configure` learns the environment
   variable. *Rejected: deleting it*, because the deterministic retry tests must force a retrying
   strategy **regardless** of the environment variable — a test whose whole point is observing a
   retry cannot be silently skipped by an unset variable. Its doc comment changes from "pins a
   limitation" to "forces the strategy on for a test that must observe a retry". **Reversal cost:**
   one method; the deterministic tests would have to set a process-wide environment variable from
   inside a test, which is exactly the shared-state hazard the seam exists to avoid.

8. **The Redis repair is member-exact on `changesKey`, and the score-range removal on `historyKey`
   stays.** The member-exact removal fixes the documented residue and the undocumented bystander
   eviction in one change, because the removal key is the stale member's own bytes. *Rejected:
   deriving the stale position from the head read at `RedisStateLedgerStore.cs:295`*, which is free
   but answers a different question — the head is the *latest* revision, so it misses the residue on
   every non-head revision and would make the defect intermittent rather than deterministic, which is
   worse than today. *Rejected: making `historyKey` member-exact too*, because a history sorted set
   holds at most one member per revision score by construction and the range form is belt and braces
   against the case the member form cannot see; keeping it confines the change to the one key whose
   semantics are wrong. **Reversal cost:** one statement each way; reverting the `changesKey` half
   reinstates both residues, which two conformance tests show on Redis.

9. **The in-memory removal happens inside `lock (_feedLock)`, alongside the add.** *Rejected: removing
   before taking the lock*, which is cheaper and lets a concurrent drain observe a feed holding
   neither entry — a record briefly absent from the change feed while its history record exists, the
   violation Phase 11 exists to prevent. **Reversal cost:** moving one statement; the Phase 8
   in-flight conformance test is the one positioned to catch it.

10. **The conformance suite gets a `Maintain` step, not a per-provider expectation.**
    `ConformanceStore.Maintain` is the maintenance call a provider needs before its feed is fully
    repaired; only the filesystem provider sets it, to `CompactChangeLogAsync`. *Rejected: an
    abstract `bool RepairsImportResidueOnWrite` that branches the assertion*, which encodes the
    difference as an expectation rather than as a step and would let a provider regress into the "no"
    column silently — the assertion would simply relax. A step cannot be satisfied by regressing.
    **Reversal cost:** one property; the test would then need a per-provider expected count, which is
    the thing a conformance suite exists to avoid.

11. **The Entity Framework Core cell of the colliding-position test asserts the documented throw, in
    a test on the Entity Framework Core subclass rather than in the shared base.**
    `StatesmanLedgerDbContext.cs:40` puts a unique index on `GlobalPosition`, and
    `docs/providers/index.md:185` documents the rejection, so the outcome there is a documented
    provider difference rather than a step. *Rejected: a silent skip*, which loses the documented
    behaviour. *Rejected: asserting `DbUpdateException` in the shared base*, which puts an Entity
    Framework Core exception type in a file five providers share. The shared test skips against a
    `RejectsCollidingPositions` flag whose default is the strict behaviour, so a provider that
    regresses into the rejecting column has to say so out loud. The exception type is **measured in
    the task**, not predicted here. **Reversal cost:** one virtual property and one subclass test.

12. **`ManualTimeProvider` gains `GetTimestamp` and `TimestampFrequency` together, as a behaviour
    change on a shipped public type.** Measured: a `TimeProvider` reporting `TimestampFrequency`
    of 1000 and a millisecond `GetTimestamp` makes the base `GetElapsedTime` scale by the override,
    so `GetTimestamp` alone would read elapsed time at `Stopwatch.Frequency`'s scale — identical on
    Windows, where both are 10,000,000, and 100 times short on the Linux CI leg, where
    `Stopwatch.Frequency` is 1,000,000,000. *Rejected: a separate `VirtualStopwatchTimeProvider`*,
    which splits the clock in two and reintroduces exactly the confusion Phase 12's `CreateTimer`
    override removed. *Rejected: overriding `GetTimestamp` only*, measured above. This belongs in
    `CHANGELOG.md` `### Changed` with the same framing decision 8 of the pre-Phase-12 addendum gave
    `CreateTimer`. **Reversal cost:** deleting two members; the dispatcher's virtual-clock tests go
    red immediately, which is the right direction.

13. **`StateChangeDispatcher` takes its `TimeProvider` through a constructor overload, not an
    optional parameter.** *Rejected: a defaulted parameter on the existing four-argument
    constructor*, which is source-compatible but binary-breaking — a consumer assembly compiled
    against 0.3.0 would throw `MissingMethodException`. There is no public-API baseline gate in this
    repository yet (`ROADMAP.md:36`), which is precisely why the conservative shape is taken by hand.
    **Reversal cost:** collapsing the two constructors into one is a later, deliberate break.

14. **The periodic-renewal test converts to an exact count, and one real-time test stays under
    `TimeProvider.System`.** `The_lease_is_renewed_periodically_under_a_nonzero_interval_not_once_per_batch`
    swaps its delaying sink for one that advances the virtual clock per batch, turning
    `0 < RenewCalls < 25` into an exact number. `The_renewal_clock_survives_a_cycle_boundary` keeps
    its real delays and its inexact assertion, and only its comment changes. *Rejected: converting
    both*, which would leave the production clock path unexercised — the failure mode Phase 12's own
    final review caught when a conversion cut a real-time budget that had to be given back
    (`46caf1b`). *Rejected: converting neither*, which leaves the subtlest bug in the Phase 10
    persistent-leader mechanism pinned only by a margin. **Reversal cost:** one sink class and one
    test body.

15. **`_leaseRenewedAt`'s zero default is verified by a test rather than reasoned about.** The field
    is stamped at acquisition before any lease exists, so zero should be unreachable. *Rejected:
    arguing it from the code*, which is the Phase 7 "defaults need a live probe" failure mode exactly:
    a default nobody exercised is a default nobody has checked. The test sets a 400-day renewal
    interval over a clock that never moves and asserts zero renewals across three cycles; an
    implementation that failed to stamp would measure roughly two thousand years and renew. **Reversal
    cost:** one test; deleting it restores an unchecked assumption about an unreachable default.

16. **D(iii) is closed as "measured, decoded comparison retained" — no code change.** This **reverses
    a Phase 12 review Minor**, and the evidence is a measurement rather than an argument, which is the
    handover's "read before trusting any review finding" rule applied in the other direction. The
    review held that `CompactChangeLogAsync`'s duplicate collapse should key on raw bytes rather than
    on decoded lines. Measured over a log holding one logical line three times as LF, CRLF, LF:
    compaction takes 3 lines to 1 and the feed from 3 entries to 1; under a raw-byte comparison the
    CRLF variant survives, the log compacts 3 to 2, and the feed keeps yielding one record twice —
    the exact defect the collapse exists to remove. The decoded comparison is strictly stronger, not
    weaker: two byte-identical lines always decode identically. *Rejected: making the change anyway
    for symmetry with the stated invariant*, which would ship a regression to satisfy a comment; what
    ships instead is a corrected comment and a pinning test, so the ruling is evidence rather than
    argument the next time someone reads that line. **Reversal cost:** none — nothing was changed;
    re-opening it means re-running the probe the test now encodes.

The decisions below were taken mid-execution, once Tasks 1–7 turned up evidence the plan above did
not anticipate; each is recorded here for the same reason the sixteen above are — so a later phase
finds the ruling and its cost rather than re-deriving it.

17. **The rival-refusal check in
    `A_lost_commit_acknowledgement_on_acquire_returns_this_callers_own_lease` moves above
    `lease.DisposeAsync()`.** The plan's own test disposed the caller's own lease and then asserted a
    rival was refused for that already-released lease — a released lease must be re-acquirable by
    anyone, so the assertion as ordered would have pinned a false contract. *Rejected: leaving the
    plan's ordering as written*, which asserts something the production code (correctly) does not do.
    **Reversal cost:** one test asserts less than the plan intended (no assertion that a rival is
    refused a lease this same test has already released); the released-lease-reacquire contract is
    exercised elsewhere. Task 1, 2026-09-11.

18. **Decision 2's "PostgreSQL-only" characterization of the outer loop's sequence-row bootstrap-race
    retry is corrected: the retry is load-bearing on SQL Server too.** Task 1's break-the-mechanism
    lever deleted the outer loop's `attempt < MaxWriteAttempts` guard and re-ran
    `Concurrent_appends_to_different_addresses_all_succeed` against a fresh database on both engines:
    PostgreSQL failed as decision 2 and the plan's step 1.14 predicted, and SQL Server also failed,
    with the identical `InvalidOperationException: The global-position sequence row could not be
    created after several attempts.`, reproducibly across 4 of 4 runs — not "still passes" as step
    1.14 stated. *Rejected: trusting the plan's prediction that SQL Server's read-committed locking
    serializes the bootstrap race well enough to make the outer loop unnecessary there* — measurement
    contradicts it at 8-way concurrency. **Reversal cost:** none; no code changed, since the outer
    loop already ships unconditionally on every engine. This decision only corrects the
    design-rationale prose in decision 2 and the Phase 13 spec section above, so a later task does not
    conclude the outer loop is droppable on SQL Server. Task 1, 2026-09-11.

19. **Task 3's four RED-step numeric and pass/fail divergences from the plan's predictions are the
    same known import-residue defect surfacing one call earlier than the plan's author counted, not a
    second, undiscovered defect.** In all four new tests (two in-memory, two Redis), the seed
    `AppendAsync` is itself immediately followed by a first `ImportAsync` that moves the revision,
    which leaks the residue the tests exist to demonstrate a step before the plan's prose accounted
    for. *Rejected: stopping to investigate an unknown in-memory/Redis defect*, which the plan's own
    instructions call for on an unexpected second failure. **Reversal cost:** if wrong, a distinct
    eviction defect would be hidden behind the known residue — mitigated because the in-memory
    lock-placement lever (3.6) and Task 4's conformance suite independently exercise the same code
    path and would expose it. Task 3, 2026-09-11.

20. **The Redis test `ImportAsync_does_not_evict_a_different_addresss_member_at_the_same_position` is
    kept as committed, and a third Redis test is added rather than editing it, to pin the
    bystander-eviction defect against the true pre-Task-3 baseline.** Task 3's review found the
    existing test passes on both the original code and the fixed code, for two different reasons — the
    seed's own residue coincidentally satisfies its assertions at the true baseline — so it gave no RED
    evidence for the defect it is named for. *Rejected: modifying the existing test in place to drop
    its seed `AppendAsync`*, which would lose its separate value as the regression check for the
    score-range reimplementation exercised by lever 3.11. **Reversal cost:** one extra test method and
    one `Record(...)` helper in `RedisChangeFeedTests.cs`; low. Task 3 review / fix round 2,
    2026-09-11.

21. **`DelayingStateChangeSink` is deleted in Task 6's batch, reversing the plan's "delete it only if
    the compiler reports it unused" condition.** Task 5's conversion of the periodic-renewal test left
    the class with zero remaining references, but this repository's warning set emits no diagnostic
    for an unreferenced private nested class, so the plan's stated deletion condition can never fire.
    *Rejected: leaving it in place as harmless, plan-authorized dead code* — a maintainer skimming the
    file later would have no record of why an apparently-dead type survived. **Reversal cost:** one
    test helper restored from git, if a future task needs the delay-based sink shape again. Task 5
    review / Task 6, 2026-09-11.

## Addendum (pre-Phase-14, 2026-09-11)

Decisions taken while writing the Phase 14 section and its implementation plan. **As in Phases 8
through 13, they were taken without `AskUserQuestion` because the session ran under an autonomous
`/goal`.** These are the ones to revisit if that ruling was wrong, and each is listed with the
alternative rejected and what reversing it would cost.

**Numbering continues the pre-Phase-13 addendum rather than restarting at 1.** Every addendum before
this one restarted, which is why an unqualified "decision 9" in the Phase 13 section means the
pre-Phase-12 addendum's ninth decision and an unqualified "decision 18" means the pre-Phase-13
addendum's eighteenth. Continuing from 21 makes every number from here on unambiguous on its own.
References below to decisions 1 through 21 name the addendum they belong to explicitly.

22. **The subclass-tolerant `IMigrationsAssembly` ships in both core packages, behind an options
    extension, rather than the subclass seam being taken away.** Measured: Entity Framework Core
    filters migrations on `DbContextAttribute.ContextType == contextType`, an exact reference
    comparison, so `Database.Migrate()` on a consumer subclass **returns normally and applies
    nothing**, creating only the history table — no exception, no log the consumer reads. *Rejected:
    documenting "use `StatesmanLedgerDbContext` directly, do not subclass"*, which also works
    (measured) and costs no code, but takes away the arrangement every test, every documentation page
    and every existing consumer in this repository uses, to work around a filter we can replace in
    forty lines. *Rejected: shipping the migrations and documenting the no-op as a limitation*, which
    is the one option that is worse than shipping nothing. **Reversal cost:** two types deleted and
    two documentation paragraphs rewritten to "do not subclass"; the risk carried in the meantime is
    a dependency on an Entity Framework Core internal API (`EF1001`) that a major bump could change,
    which the seam test makes loud rather than silent.

23. **The model-drift gate is an xUnit test in the ordinary test run, not six `dotnet ef` jobs.**
    Spec item 5 of the Phase 13 sketch called for "one job per engine per context". Measured: both the
    runtime `HasPendingModelChanges()` and the CLI `has-pending-model-changes` open **no connection**
    and answer correctly against unreachable connection strings on all three engines, so an engine job
    adds no signal. *Rejected: the six CLI jobs*, which need `Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.Design`
    resolved in continuous integration, a pinned tool in the workflow, and six steps whose failure
    output is a sentence on standard output rather than a test name. The test form additionally
    asserts what the CLI cannot — that migrations were **discovered at all** — which is the
    difference between a gate and a gate that passes vacuously. **Reversal cost:** none; adding CLI
    steps later is additive and runs the same check.

24. **A `.config/dotnet-tools.json` pinning `dotnet-ef` 10.0.11 ships even though the gate does not
    use the tool.** The repository has no tool manifest at all today and the authoring machine's
    global `dotnet-ef` is `10.0.7` against a `10.0.11` runtime, warning on every invocation. *Rejected:
    no manifest*, which leaves the next maintainer regenerating migrations with whatever tool happens
    to be installed — and a migration is generated output that must be reproducible, not merely
    correct once. *Rejected: installing it globally in a workflow*, the pattern `nbgv`/`docfx` use,
    which does not help the human who authors the migration locally. **Reversal cost:** delete one
    small file.

25. **Each context's shipped set uses its own history table name**,
    `__StatesmanLedgerMigrationsHistory` and `__StatesmanOutboxMigrationsHistory`. *Rejected: the
    default `__EFMigrationsHistory`*, which is shared per database, so a consumer running their own
    migrations beside a shipped set gets the two interleaved. *Rejected: one shared Statesman name for
    both contexts*, which fails the case the separate `StatesmanOutboxCursorDbContext` exists to
    serve — a consumer adopting ledger migrations but not outbox ones, or the reverse, in one
    database. **Reversal cost:** a rename before the first release; afterwards it strands every
    database that applied the old name.

26. **Six packages, three per context, never three shared ones.** *Rejected: three packages each
    carrying both contexts' migrations*, which makes a ledger-only consumer take a reference on the
    outbox core package to get a SQLite migration. The two contexts are independently adoptable by
    construction. **Reversal cost:** merging later is a package rename and a breaking change for
    anyone who took the narrower dependency.

27. **Pre-Phase-12 addendum decisions 9 (SQL Server's 900-byte key) and 12 (PostgreSQL microsecond
    `timestamptz`) are not re-opened, and the spec section says so out loud.** Both rested partly on
    "a schema change to a package that ships no migrations", and Phase 14 removes that premise.
    *Rejected: re-opening either*, because a migration makes a schema change *possible*, not *free*,
    and each decision's load-bearing half — "no realistic address reaches 900 bytes", "nothing
    compares below microsecond" — is untouched by Phase 14. *Rejected: saying nothing*, which leaves a
    later reviewer reading a rationale whose first clause has lapsed and re-litigating it from
    scratch. **Reversal cost:** none; re-opening either means a second migration in all six packages
    and, for decision 12, a breaking storage-format change.

28. **Shipped migrations stay opt-in; consumer-owns-it stays the documented default.** Nothing in
    either core package applies a migration or calls `EnsureCreated`, and a consumer who never calls
    `UseStatesman…Migrations()` sees byte-for-byte today's behaviour. *Rejected: making the shipped
    migration the default arrangement*, which would change the meaning of an existing registration.
    The sentences describing the old arrangement as the only one are amended **in place**, never
    deleted, per the Phase 13 lesson on superseded statements. **Reversal cost:** none technically;
    flipping the default later is a breaking change to a documented contract.

29. **The options extension goes on the outer `DbContextOptionsBuilder`, not on the provider's inner
    `RelationalDbContextOptionsBuilder`.** This departs from the research's recommended shape, on a
    measurement. *Rejected: `UseSqlite(cs, o => o.UseStatesmanLedgerMigrations())`*, the Entity
    Framework Core idiom and the shape the research proposed: an extension over
    `RelationalDbContextOptionsBuilder<TBuilder, TExtension>` binds `TExtension` to the provider's own
    internal options extension (`SqliteOptionsExtension` and its two siblings), so every call site
    emits `EF1001` — measured — and the suppression would have to be repeated in all six packages for
    a *second* internal type. The outer form uses only public API
    (`RelationalOptionsExtension.WithMigrationsAssembly` / `.WithMigrationsHistoryTableName`,
    `IDbContextOptionsBuilderInfrastructure.AddOrUpdateExtension`, `ReplaceService`) and builds the six
    packages with zero warnings, measured, keeping the phase's entire `EF1001` surface on two types in
    two core packages. **Reversal cost:** six one-line signature changes plus six pragmas; the
    consumer-visible call moves from inside the `UseSqlite` lambda to after it.

30. **No new test project; the tests go into the two existing Entity Framework Core test projects.**
    Both are already in `sqlserver-tests` and `postgres-tests` by explicit path (`ci.yml:134-139`,
    `:185-190`) and in `dotnet test Statesman.slnx`, so the live `Migrate()` tests reach all three
    engines with **no workflow edit**. *Rejected: a dedicated `…Migrations.Tests` project*, which
    would have to be added to both jobs' lists — a workflow edit the brief set out to avoid — and
    would duplicate the `EntityFrameworkTestDatabase` fixture both existing projects already link.
    **Reversal cost:** one csproj, two `ci.yml` list entries, and a duplicated fixture link.

31. **The six migration projects each declare `<TargetFramework>net10.0</TargetFramework>`, and
    `src/Directory.Build.props:6`'s exclusion list gains their six names anyway.** Measured with
    `dotnet msbuild -getProperty`: an explicit `<TargetFramework>` in the csproj wins over an
    inherited `TargetFrameworks`, so the exclusion is defensive rather than load-bearing and a project
    absent from it still builds `net10.0`-only. *Rejected: leaving the condition alone*, which would
    leave a line whose stated intent — "these projects do not multi-target" — is silently false for
    six of the eight projects it describes. *Rejected: inverting the pattern* so each Entity Framework
    Core csproj carries its own target framework and the condition disappears, which is the better
    end state and is a refactor of eight projects that does not belong inside a phase that ships six
    new ones. **Reversal cost:** one line either way; nothing builds differently.

    *Amended (Phase 15, 2026-09-12):* wrong, for the case this measurement never exercised. This
    decision's `dotnet msbuild -getProperty` check only ever ran with the condition active, where
    `TargetFrameworks` is therefore never actually set for the nine projects it names — so nothing was
    ever contending with a project's own `<TargetFramework>` for precedence, and "wins" was true only
    vacuously. Phase 15 Task 7 measured the other case, by actually inverting the condition: once
    `TargetFrameworks` is set unconditionally, the .NET SDK's cross-targeting outer build activates for
    that project regardless of its own `<TargetFramework>`, and all nine excluded projects fail restore
    with `NU1202` (their EF Core/Npgsql package references only support net10.0). **The exclusion list
    is load-bearing, not defensive**, and Task 7 did not invert it. See pre-Phase-15 addendum decision
    51's own amendment.

32. **`BaselineAsync` returns `bool` and is idempotent, and throws only when no migration was
    discovered.** It returns `false` rather than throwing when the history table already holds rows,
    so an application that calls it unconditionally at startup is supported. *Rejected: throwing on a
    second call*, which makes the safe usage pattern the one that crashes on restart. *Rejected:
    returning `false` when no migration was discovered too*, which would quietly write nothing and
    leave a misconfigured consumer believing they were baselined — the same silent-success failure
    mode decision 22 exists to remove. **Reversal cost:** one return type and one throw; callers
    ignoring the `bool` are unaffected either way.

33. **`BaselineAsync` records `ProductInfo.GetVersion()` — the running Entity Framework Core
    version — not the shipped snapshot's `ProductVersion` annotation.** Taken during Task 2's fix
    round, superseding item 3's own text below (see the in-place amendment there). `ProductInfo` is
    public API (no `EF1001`), and this is the same source `Migrator.ApplyMigration` uses when it
    writes a history row for a migration applied through `Migrate()`, so a baselined row is
    indistinguishable from one `Migrate()` would have written itself. *Rejected: the snapshot's
    `ProductVersion` annotation* (item 3's original text, and the plan's own snippet) — it records the
    Entity Framework Core version the migration was *generated* with, which for a database baselined
    long after generation is a different number than any real `Migrate()` call on that same database
    would write, a metadata inconsistency with no documented reader but no honest justification either.
    **Reversal cost:** one field read; `ProductVersion` is stored but never read back to gate
    behaviour, so no consumer-visible effect either way.

34. **The baseline's create-if-not-exists script and its per-migration inserts run in one
    transaction, itself run inside `context.Database.CreateExecutionStrategy().ExecuteAsync(...)`,
    with the already-baselined check inside that delegate.** Taken during Task 2's fix round, on two
    independent grounds: **atomicity** — without it, a crash between two inserts leaves a history
    table the idempotency check reads as fully baselined when it is only partially baselined, and a
    later `Migrate()` either silently skips the still-unrecorded migration's schema or fails the same
    "already exists" class of error this helper exists to prevent; and **retry participation** —
    `BaselineAttemptAsync` issues every statement through `ExecuteSqlRawAsync`, which bypasses Entity
    Framework Core's per-operation retry entirely, so a bare, unwrapped transaction around it gets
    zero benefit from a caller's `EnableRetryOnFailure`, while the execution-strategy wrapper is what
    makes the whole attempt replay as a unit on a transient failure. **The "does not support
    user-initiated transactions" exception that a bare transaction throws under a retrying strategy
    does *not* fire for this method** — measured directly, with the wrapper temporarily removed, on
    both live server engines under `STATESMAN_TEST_EF_RETRY=1`: the exception is raised by
    `SaveChangesAsync`/LINQ paths, not by `BeginTransactionAsync` or `ExecuteSqlRawAsync` on their own,
    so `BaselineAttemptAsync`'s all-raw-SQL body never trips it either way. Record this so nobody
    re-documents the exception as the reason for the wrapper; the wrapper's real justification is the
    two grounds above. *Rejected: no transaction* (the plan's own snippet, item 3's original text) —
    self-healing only for the single-migration case every test in this phase exercises, and silently
    unsafe as soon as a context ships a second migration. **Reversal cost:** one extra round trip per
    baseline call.

35. **Every migration package csproj sets
    `<CentralPackageTransitivePinningEnabled>false</CentralPackageTransitivePinningEnabled>`, scoped to
    the six packages.** Taken during Task 3, discovered by measurement rather than anticipated by this
    section: `Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.Design` transitively pins `Microsoft.CodeAnalysis.Workspaces.Common`
    exactly (`= 5.0.0`, via `Microsoft.CodeAnalysis.Workspaces.MSBuild`), and the repository's central
    `Microsoft.CodeAnalysis.CSharp.Workspaces 5.6.0` breaks it under transitive pinning (`NU1608` as an
    error, because warnings are errors repository-wide). `Design` carries `PrivateAssets="all"`, so
    nothing in this dependency subtree — Roslyn included — reaches a consumer of the shipped package;
    the controller verified this against all six nuspecs directly. *Rejected: a throwaway startup
    project for `dotnet ef`*, which keeps `Design` out of the six csprojs but makes authoring
    unreproducible from the package alone — the tool would need a project the package itself does not
    ship. **Reversal cost:** six csproj lines.

36. **The replacement migrations-assembly types catch `ReflectionTypeLoadException` on
    `Assembly.DefinedTypes` and degrade to the loadable types, silently.** Taken during Task 1's fix
    round, closing an Important review finding: item 2's `StatesmanMigrationsAssembly` and its outbox
    twin originally called `Assembly.DefinedTypes` directly, so a consumer migrations assembly with one
    unloadable type got an unhandled exception where the stock, un-replaced `MigrationsAssembly` would
    have kept discovering every other migration. Fetched against Entity Framework Core 10.0.11's actual
    source rather than memory: `MigrationsAssembly.Migrations`/`ModelSnapshot` call
    `Assembly.GetConstructibleTypes()` with `logger: null`, and the one public logging extension for
    this event is keyed to a different diagnostics category than a `MigrationsAssembly` is constructed
    with — so Entity Framework Core's own path degrades without logging here either, and matching it
    means degrading silently rather than adding a second `EF1001` site or a wrong-category logger for a
    diagnostic upstream itself never surfaces at this call site. Covered by one deterministic test per
    package, using a fake `Assembly` subclass overriding only `DefinedTypes`. **Reversal cost:** the two
    try/catch blocks and their two tests; no public surface changes either way.

37. **The six migration packages drop the packaged `runtimeconfig.json` by narrowing
    `DefaultAllowedOutputExtensionsInPackageBuildOutputFolder` to exclude `.json`, rather than turning
    `GenerateRuntimeConfigurationFiles` off.** Taken during the final fix wave, closing a Minor from the
    final review: `Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.Design`'s build props turn on
    `GenerateRuntimeConfigurationFiles` so `dotnet ef` can load the built assembly at authoring time, and
    that stays on. Measured: setting `AllowedOutputExtensionsInPackageBuildOutputFolder` directly in the
    csproj does not remove the file, because `NuGet.Build.Tasks.Pack.targets` always redefines that
    property as the SDK default *plus* whatever the csproj set — the default's own `.json` entry
    survives the concatenation regardless. `DefaultAllowedOutputExtensionsInPackageBuildOutputFolder` is
    the value that default is drawn from, guarded by an empty-check the six csprojs' own setting
    satisfies, so setting it (rather than the derived property) actually narrows the list. Verified: the
    packed `lib/net10.0/` folder for one ledger and one outbox package now holds only the `.dll` and
    `.xml`, and `dotnet ef migrations has-pending-model-changes` against the SQLite ledger package still
    exits 0 afterward, so authoring-time loading is unaffected. *Rejected: accept the inert file* — it is
    harmless to a consumer (NuGet copies assemblies out of `lib`, not loose JSON) but is exactly the kind
    of unexplained artifact a future maintainer has to re-investigate from scratch the first time they
    open one of these nupkgs. *Rejected: turn `GenerateRuntimeConfigurationFiles` off* — `dotnet ef`
    needs it at authoring time, and turning it off entirely would break every future migration-authoring
    session on these six packages for one packaging nicety. **Reversal cost:** six lines.

## Addendum (pre-Phase-15, 2026-09-12)

Decisions taken while writing the Phase 15 section and its implementation plan. **As in Phases 8
through 14, they were taken without `AskUserQuestion` because the session ran under an autonomous
`/goal`.** These are the ones to revisit if that ruling was wrong, and each is listed with the
alternative rejected and what reversing it would cost.

**The two most worth revisiting are decision 39 — the committed suppression files, which make a
deliberate break a permanent artifact of this repository — and decision 43, which takes a breaking
change to a published package.** Everything else here is either a measurement or a small ruling.

**Numbering continues the pre-Phase-14 addendum, which ended at 37.**

38. **Phase 15 is a sweep, and its scope is "every deferred item that is still open, still small and
    still decided", plus the last open ROADMAP 0.2 bullet.** Nine tasks. *Rejected: one more
    feature phase*, which would leave a thirteen-times-carried list to be carried a fourteenth time.
    *Rejected: sweeping everything, including the three large parked items* — address-equality
    surgery, cross-process filesystem locking and Redis's position representation are each
    storage-format or library-wide changes that have been examined and re-ruled at least three times,
    and folding one into a sweep is how a sweep becomes a phase that does not finish. *Rejected:
    splitting the sweep from the baseline gate into two phases*, which would put the gate after a
    phase that changes a public surface, so the gate's first act would be to suppress a break it could
    have caught. **Reversal cost:** none; the tasks are independent and any subset can be dropped.

39. **The public-API baseline is `0.3.0` for the fifteen packages `v0.3.0` published, and the
    `CompatibilitySuppressions.xml` files recording the deliberate breaks are generated once and
    committed.** Option (i) of the three the inventory named. This is the shape the tooling is designed
    for, it starts protecting consumers on the next release rather than the one after, and a
    suppression entry is exactly how a deliberate pre-1.0 break is meant to be recorded. *Rejected:
    cutting `0.4.0` first and baselining against that*, which keeps the alpha window free of
    suppression entries but leaves `ROADMAP.md:36` unshipped for another release and makes the gate's
    arrival contingent on a release rather than on work. *Rejected: enabling the gate in the
    non-breaking direction only*, which is not a mode the tooling has. **What it costs if wrong:** six
    committed XML files, nineteen entries, all of them recording one change
    (`IStateChangeFeed.ReadAsync`), become a durable artifact that a reader encounters before they
    encounter the reason for it — which is why decision 41 puts the reason in a documentation page.
    **Reversal cost:** delete the files and one property; nothing in the shipped code changes either
    way.

40. **`PackageValidationBaselineVersion` is set once in `src/Directory.Build.props`, and the seven
    packages with no `0.3.0` baseline blank it in their own csproj.** Measured, and this is not a
    style preference: the baseline nupkg is fetched by a **restore-time** download, so passing the
    property on the `pack` command line fails with
    `MSB4018: System.IO.FileNotFoundException: Package '…statesman.outbox.0.3.0.nupkg' not found`,
    while the same property in the props file makes `dotnet restore Statesman.slnx` download all
    fifteen and the CI-shaped `dotnet pack --no-build` validate against them. *Rejected: naming the
    fifteen in a condition* — an inclusion list of fifteen names beside the nine-name exclusion list
    decision 31 already calls "explicitly parked" doubles the thing item 7 exists to reduce.
    *Rejected: an exclusion list of the seven in the props file*, which gives a newly added package the
    wrong default: it would inherit a `0.3.0` baseline that does not exist and fail its first pack.
    The blank-in-csproj form puts the fact next to the package it is true of, and the end state when
    `0.4.0` ships is one property bumped and seven overrides deleted. **Reversal cost:** eight lines.

41. **Every suppression entry is explained on a documentation page, never in an XML comment inside
    `CompatibilitySuppressions.xml`.** Measured: a hand-added comment in that file **does not survive
    regeneration** — `ApiCompatGenerateSuppressionFile` rewrites the file whole. *Rejected: XML
    comments*, which would be a note that silently vanishes the next time a deliberate break is taken,
    which is exactly the moment someone needs to read it. *Rejected: no explanation at all*, leaving
    nineteen `CP0002`/`CP0006` entries naming method signatures with no record of which release broke
    them on purpose. **Reversal cost:** one page.

42. **The property that generates the suppression files is `ApiCompatGenerateSuppressionFile`, not
    `GenerateCompatibilitySuppressionFile`.** Recorded as a decision rather than a footnote because the
    older name appears in the brief this phase was planned from and in much of the .NET 6-era
    documentation, and it fails silently — the pack simply errors as though the flag were absent. The
    SDK's own failure message names the correct one:
    `'/p:ApiCompatGenerateSuppressionFile=true'`. **Reversal cost:** none; it is a fact, not a choice.

43. **`Statesman.Outbox.OutboxCursorFile` becomes `internal` — a breaking change to a package that
    shipped in `v0.3.0`, taken deliberately in the `0.4.0-alpha` window.** It is the on-disk shape of a
    cursor file, it has no consumer anywhere in `src` or `tests`, and the same window already carries
    the `IStateChangeFeed.ReadAsync` break. *Rejected: leaving it public*, which freezes a serialization
    detail into the public surface for the life of 1.0 — and decision 39 makes that concrete rather
    than theoretical, because the gate would then require a suppression entry to ever remove it.
    *Rejected: deferring it to a later phase*, which is strictly worse under decision 39: a break not
    taken in the same phase as the gate needs a second suppression file written later, against a
    baseline that by then records the type as still public. **Reversal cost:** one access modifier and
    one suppression entry; no consumer is known to be affected, but "known" is doing real work in that
    sentence, and reversing it after `0.4.0` ships would itself be an additive change.

44. **Phase 14's deferred Minor (c) — the `MigrationAttributeMissingWarning` diagnostic — ships as
    code, and Minor (e), the test harness's logger sink, ships with it.** Conditional on a
    measurement, which passed. `RelationalLoggerExtensions` and its
    `MigrationAttributeMissingWarning(IDiagnosticsLogger<DbLoggerCategory.Migrations>, TypeInfo)`
    overload are both `public`; neither they, nor `IDiagnosticsLogger<>`, nor
    `RelationalEventId.MigrationAttributeMissingWarning` carry `[EntityFrameworkInternal]`; the logger
    the extension wants is **exactly** the one the derived constructor already receives; and a call
    site in the shipped shape compiles with zero warnings under `TreatWarningsAsErrors=true`, proven
    non-vacuous by a control `MigrationsAssembly` subclass in the same project that failed with two
    `EF1001` errors. So the item adds **no** second internal-API site. *Rejected: a comment recording
    the lost diagnostic*, which was the correct outcome only if the extension had been internal.
    *Rejected: shipping the diagnostic without the sink* — a diagnostic nothing in the harness can
    observe is not a testable claim, which is what changes Phase 14's "not worth closing for one
    warning" ruling on the sink. The sink is scoped to the migrations category at the one test that
    needs it, matching `EntityFrameworkChangeFeedTests.cs:136`, not wired globally. **Reversal cost:**
    one field, one call, one `LogTo`, two tests.

45. **Phase 14's deferred Minor (a) is deleted rather than tested, and says so in the brief.** The two
    `ArgumentNullException.ThrowIfNull(currentContext)` calls run after the base constructor has
    already dereferenced the same argument, so they are unreachable. *Rejected: writing a test*, which
    is impossible — the behaviour cannot be pinned, and an implementer who tries will either invent a
    test that passes for the wrong reason or report BLOCKED. Each deleted line is replaced by a
    one-line comment recording why no guard can run there. **Reversal cost:** two lines.

46. **The filesystem partition catalog filters on the head file existing, never on the history
    file.** Verified: `PruneAsync` deletes history revisions and leaves `head.json`, and the only
    `File.Delete` calls in the provider are prune's and two temp-file cleanups, so no path removes a
    head. *Rejected: filtering on the history file*, which is the obvious fix and is a **regression**:
    it would silently drop a legitimate partition every one of whose revisions had been pruned. The
    fully-pruned-address test is mandatory precisely because it is the one that discriminates the two
    mechanisms; the torn-line test passes under either. **Reversal cost:** one predicate; getting it
    wrong costs a silently missing partition, which is worse than the phantom it replaces.

    *Amended (Phase 15, 2026-09-12):* the head-file mechanism and both tests stand, but "discriminates
    the two mechanisms" overclaimed what the fully-pruned-address test can show. The implementer found,
    and the controller independently re-verified against `PruneAsync` (`:346-430`), that **no lever
    reproduces a history-file filter failing this test**: `MaxAge` and `KeepTombstones` both exempt
    `Revision == latest`, `MaxRevisions` keeps the last N, and `MaxBytes` always seats the newest
    record — every retention path keeps at least the newest revision, so no shipped sequence can ever
    empty a history directory while a head file remains, and a history-file filter is observably
    equivalent to the head-file filter under today's code. The test is relabeled a **pinning** test
    (`Pruning_every_older_revision_keeps_the_partition_listed`) that the catalog and retention agree,
    not a rejected-mechanism proof; the source comment and `docs/providers/index.md:149` were corrected
    to match. The head-file mechanism itself is unaffected — it is justified by what it *is* (the one
    write-time artifact nothing deletes), not by the lever this decision predicted.

47. **The maintenance-failure queue is bounded and stays `internal`.** *Rejected: making it public in
    the same task*, which is a design question with three shapes, one of which conflicts with the core
    package's zero-external-dependency invariant, and therefore belongs in front of a maintainer.
    *Rejected: correcting only the documentation and leaving the queue unbounded*, which leaves a real
    unbounded collection on a path `StateHandle` runs after every successful append. The two
    documentation sentences that promise a drain are corrected in the same task as the bound, because a
    bound without the correction still leaves the documentation wrong. **Reversal cost:** one field and
    two sentences.

48. **Two items in the outbox sweep may correctly ship as a comment rather than as code, and the plan
    permits either while requiring the reason.** `RedisOutboxCursorStore`'s token, because
    StackExchange.Redis's `StringGetAsync` and `ScriptEvaluateAsync` take no `CancellationToken`, so
    the choice is a `.WaitAsync` wrapper that abandons rather than cancels, or a documented entry-only
    contract; and `RedisStreamStateChangeSink.Deduplicated`, because `StateChangeDispatcher` publishes
    serially and there is no live race, so a documented single-writer contract is probably better than
    `Interlocked`. *Rejected: mandating code*, which would have an implementer add a wrapper whose
    semantics the plan had not measured. *Rejected: mandating a comment*, which pre-empts a measurement
    the implementer is better placed to make. **Reversal cost:** whichever one was not chosen.

49. **The `FileSystemOutboxCursorStore` triple flush is measured before it is changed, under a new
    `STATESMAN_MEASURE_OUTBOX_CURSOR_WRITE=1` gate, in Debug and Release, over 200 writes — and stays
    unchanged unless the collapse measures faster.** This is a durability path, `fsync` is not
    black-box provable in this repository, and Phase 12's D4 was reverted byte-identical for exactly
    this reason. *Rejected: collapsing the three calls on the complexity argument alone*, which is the
    shape of change D4 was. *Rejected: not measuring and leaving a one-line comment*, which is the
    cheaper honest outcome but throws away the harness a future maintainer would have to build from
    scratch to re-ask the question. The existing round-trip tests are left unedited, so the evidence is
    the measurement plus unchanged behaviour. **Reversal cost:** one measurement file and one
    environment variable.

50. **Redis's concurrent same-revision `ImportAsync` hazard is documented in one sentence rather than
    fixed, and the sentence goes where the Redis provider's import caveats already live.** Option (i)
    of the three the Phase 13 parked note lists. *Rejected: option (ii), one Lua script*, which is the
    only real fix and is a new script on the one write path Phase 8 deliberately left alone — not a
    sweep item. *Rejected: option (iii), a per-address import lock*, which bounds the symptom on one
    provider and adds a lock to a bulk restore path. *Rejected: leaving it undocumented*, which is what
    made it a Phase 15 item in the first place. Import is a bulk restore path the library never drives
    concurrently against itself, which is what makes documenting it honest rather than evasive.
    **Reversal cost:** one sentence deleted when the script lands.

51. **`src/Directory.Build.props`'s exclusion list is resolved by output, not by reading.** The
    condition may be entirely inert — decision 31 measured that every project it names declares its own
    `<TargetFramework>`, which wins — but "may be" is the whole risk, so the exit check is a CI-shaped
    `dotnet pack` over `src` producing 22 nupkgs with unchanged `lib/` folders per package, plus
    `eng/validate.py` still reporting `projects: 41`. *Rejected: reading the nine csproj files and
    declaring the condition inert*, which is the reasoning decision 31 already did and which cannot
