    catch a tenth project added since. **Reversal cost:** one line either way; nothing builds
    differently if the measurement holds.

    *Amended (Phase 15, 2026-09-12):* the measurement held, and it disproved the premise it was
    checking. The condition is **not** inert — it is load-bearing. Task 7 built the CI-shaped `dotnet
    pack` this decision names, and before that, measured the case decision 31 never exercised: with the
    condition actually removed and `TargetFrameworks` set unconditionally, `dotnet build --configuration
    Release` failed with 56 `NU1202` errors across the nine previously-excluded projects, because the
    .NET SDK's cross-targeting outer build activates whenever `TargetFrameworks` is non-empty,
    independent of a project's own `<TargetFramework>`. The exclusion list was **not inverted** and
    stays exactly as it was; only the comment above it was rewritten to state this measured mechanism,
    and pre-Phase-14 addendum decision 31 carries the same amendment. The 22-nupkg,
    unchanged-`lib/`-folders exit check this decision specifies still passed, because nothing in the
    shipped file changed.

52. **No capability is added, so `docs/architecture/capabilities.md` and
    `tests/Statesman.Capabilities.Tests/CapabilityMatrixTests.cs` are untouched.** Stated as a decision
    because this phase touches five providers and two packages' public surface, which is the shape of
    change that tempts a capability-matrix edit. The Phase 10 through 14 precedent, stated in every one
    of those five close-outs. **Reversal cost:** none; a task that edits either has drifted.

## Addendum (pre-Phase-16, 2026-09-13)

Decisions taken while writing the Phase 16 section and its implementation plan. **As in Phases 8
through 15, they were taken without `AskUserQuestion` because the session ran under an autonomous
`/goal`.** These are the ones to revisit if that ruling was wrong, and each is listed with the
alternative rejected and what reversing it would cost.

**The two most worth revisiting are decision 56 — which takes the public maintenance-failure
diagnostics surface Phase 15 explicitly parked as "a maintainer's call" — and decision 57, which
chooses sixteen retained failures per source per minute as a fixed, unconfigurable rate limit.** The
first is a new public surface on a shipped interface's neighbourhood; the second is a number nobody
has operated against yet. Everything else here is a measurement or a small ruling.

**Numbering continues the pre-Phase-15 addendum, which ended at 52.**

53. **Phase 16 is ROADMAP 0.2 bullets 1 and 3, and nothing else.** Ten tasks: six for the conformance
    suites, one for the diagnostics surface, one for health checks and the telemetry audit, one sweep,
    one close-out. *Rejected: also taking bullet 2 (load diagnostics)*, which the controller's brief
    left conditional on the phase fitting nine tasks; it does not, and taking it would have shipped two
    health models a month apart, because bullet 2's "health summaries" and bullet 3's health check are
    the same idea arriving from two directions. It is parked as the first Phase 17 item and decision 61
    records how item 8 stays compatible with it. *Rejected: splitting conformance and observability into
    two phases*, which would be tidier and would leave the conformance suite — the thing ROADMAP 0.2's
    exit criterion actually names — waiting behind a feature. **Reversal cost:** none; the ten tasks are
    independent apart from the ordering decision 62 records.

54. **The conformance work is new coverage, not a lift, and no per-provider test is deleted.**
    Measured: the repository has exactly two provider-level conditional-write assertions
    (`RedisChangeFeedTests.cs:300-305` and `EntityFrameworkRetryStrategyTests.cs:217`) and three
    cancellation assertions (`TieredLedgerTests.cs:83`, `FileSystemChangeFeedTests.cs:218`,
    `RedisPartitionCatalogTests.cs:43`), and `AbstractionsAndFixtureCoverageTests.cs:157-161` — which
    reads like in-memory conditional-write coverage — exercises the `StateAppendResult.Conflict`
    factory and never touches a store. The controller's brief said "lift ... into
    `tests/Statesman.Conformance.Tests`" and asked which per-provider files the shared suite subsumes,
    per file. The honest answer is **none**. *Rejected: deleting the five existing assertions as
    "subsumed"*, which would trade five provider-specific tests (a paused-clock Redis race, a
    PostgreSQL lost-acknowledgement path) for generic ones that do not test those mechanisms.
    **Reversal cost:** none; nothing is removed, so nothing has to be restored.

55. **The conditional-write and cancellation suites are pinning suites with break-the-mechanism levers,
    not regression suites with a RED-at-baseline.** Measured by a direct probe over all five providers
    at `2b04fb5`: every conditional-write case produced byte-identical results on all five, and all
    thirty-nine already-cancelled-token call sites threw `OperationCanceledException`. A plan that
    promised RED at baseline for these would have promised a lever that cannot fire, which is the Phase
    15 Task 3 lesson. Each suite therefore ships with a deliberately broken store double it must fail
    against and a correct double it must pass against, following `BrokenLeaseProviderTests`' precedent
    exactly. *Rejected: writing the suites first and discovering the colour at implementation time*,
    which is how a task report ends up explaining an unexpected GREEN instead of proving discrimination.
    **Reversal cost:** none; the levers are test-only.

56. **The maintenance-failure diagnostics surface ships, as a discoverable interface rather than an
    `IStatesman` member.** Phase 15 parked this as a maintainer's call with three candidate shapes; this
    takes shape (i), the read-and-clear surface, because it is the only one that makes
    `docs/operations/observability.md:21` and `docs/reference/diagnostics.md:29` true without adding the
    logging dependency the core `Statesman` package deliberately does not have. The mechanism is
    `IStatesmanDiagnostics` plus
    `StatesmanDiagnosticsExtensions.TryGetDiagnostics(this IStatesman, out IStatesmanDiagnostics?)` —
    the repository's own `TryGetCapability` convention one level up. *Rejected: a member on
    `IStatesman`*, which is a `CP0006` break for any external implementer of a published interface and
    would need a suppression entry for a purely additive feature. *Rejected: keeping it internal and
    softening both documentation pages* (Phase 15's option (iii)), which Phase 15 already did as far as
    it honestly could and which leaves ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 3's third clause permanently unshippable.
    **What it costs if wrong:** four new public types in a baselined package, which cannot be removed
    again without a break. **Reversal cost:** high after a release, near zero before one — which is
    exactly why this is the decision to revisit.

57. **The rate limit is a per-source token bucket of sixteen tokens refilling every minute, fixed, not
    configurable, and it gates retention rather than acceptance.** An append stays authoritative whether
    or not its prune failed; the limiter only decides whether the exception is kept and which counter
    records it. Sixteen rather than sixty-four so that the limiter and the retention bound stay
    independently observable: at sixty-four, `statesman.maintenance.failures.dropped` could never fire
    inside one window and the two mechanisms would collapse into one number. *Rejected: rate-limiting
    the `statesman.maintenance.failures` counter itself*, which would make the total dishonest — the
    counter's whole job is to say how many failures happened. *Rejected: a configurable rate*, which
    needs a runtime options object `StatesmanDeclaration.CreateRuntime` does not have, turning an
    additive change into a builder change; the constants are public instead, so a consumer can assert
    against them. **What it costs if wrong:** a number nobody has operated against, chosen for
    testability and legibility rather than from production evidence. **Reversal cost:** one constant,
    plus whatever tests assert the arithmetic.

58. **A third counter, `statesman.maintenance.failures.suppressed`, rather than folding suppression
    into `.dropped`.** The two have different causes and different fixes: `dropped` means the retained
    set was full, `suppressed` means one source is failing faster than the limiter will keep. *Rejected:
    one counter with a `reason` tag*, which is the conventional OpenTelemetry shape but changes the
    existing `.dropped` instrument's tag set, and decision 60 rules against touching shipped
    instruments. **Reversal cost:** one instrument; adding a counter is additive.

59. **The health check ships inside `Statesman.Extensions.Hosting`, taking
    `Microsoft.Extensions.Diagnostics.HealthChecks.Abstractions` as a package reference.** Two
    measurements decided it. `Microsoft.Extensions.Diagnostics.HealthChecks.Abstractions` is present in
    the `Microsoft.AspNetCore.App` reference pack for all three target frameworks, so
    `Statesman.AspNetCore` could take it for free — and a health check only ASP.NET Core consumers can
    reach is the wrong shape, because worker services are where one is needed most. And **adding a
    package reference to a baselined package does not trip the package-validation gate**: `dotnet pack`
    on `Statesman.Extensions.Hosting` with the reference added and `PackageValidationBaselineVersion`
    at `0.3.0` succeeded, while a positive control that added a `bool` parameter to
    `AddStatesmanHosting` failed with `CP0002` once per target framework, proving the gate was live in
    that exact configuration. *Rejected: a new `Statesman.Extensions.Diagnostics.HealthChecks` package*,
    which gives the cleanest dependency story and costs a csproj with a blank baseline, a README, a
    documentation page, a table-of-contents entry, a solution entry, and a twenty-third package in the
    two documentation sentences that currently say "twenty-two" — for one class. *Rejected:
    `Statesman.AspNetCore`.* **Reversal cost:** moving one class and one extension method to a new
    package, which is a type-forwarding problem after a release and a file move before one.

60. **No shipped OpenTelemetry instrument is renamed or re-united; the audit lands as a documented
    deviation plus a pinning gate.** Two real deviations are recorded and kept: durations should be
    seconds with unit `s` and `statesman.operation.duration` uses `ms`; counters of discrete things
    should carry an annotation unit and the five shipped counters carry none. Changing the histogram's
    unit shifts every value by a factor of a thousand under an unchanged name, which is a silent
    dashboard break in a plausible direction; adding units to shipped counters changes instrument
    identity for backends that key on it, for no behavioural gain. *Rejected: correcting both*, which
    is what "semantic conventions" superficially asks for and what the ROADMAP bullet could be read to
    demand. *Rejected: correcting neither and writing nothing*, which leaves the bullet unaddressed. The
    gate — a test pinning every instrument's name, kind, unit and tag keys — is the shippable half, and
    it is the same treatment Phase 15 gave the public API. **Reversal cost:** the deviations can be
    corrected at 1.0 with a documented migration; the gate is what makes that a deliberate act.

61. **The health check reports only from facts that already exist, and its `Data` dictionary is the
    extension point bullet 2 will use.** Three inputs: `IStatesman.IsInitialized`, decision 56's
    diagnostics surface, and the loader metadata keys `docs/reference/diagnostics.md:15-19` already
    documents. *Rejected: a new `HealthSummary` model*, which is bullet 2's design question and would
    have to be re-opened when bullet 2 lands. Keying `Data` by the existing `statesman.*` metadata names
    means load diagnostics adds keys rather than changing a shape. **Reversal cost:** none; adding keys
    to a dictionary is additive by construction.

    *Amended (final review, 2026-09-13):* the shipped check reads only the first two inputs —
    `IStatesman.IsInitialized` and the diagnostics surface (`StatesmanHealthCheck.cs:55-71`). The third,
    per-root loader metadata keys, did not ship; there is no loader-metadata read in the shipped code,
    matching how ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 2 (load diagnostics) is parked to Phase 17 above. `Data`'s six keys
    are keyed by the same `statesman.` naming convention decision 56 and this decision describe, not by
    reuse of any existing metadata or meter key: two of the six (`…failures.suppressed`,
    `…failures.dropped`) happen to match meter instrument names, the other four
    (`statesman.roots`, `statesman.roots.uninitialized`, `statesman.maintenance.failures.retained`,
    `statesman.maintenance.stores.degraded`) are new, and none comes from the loader metadata. The
    additive claim in the **Reversal cost** line still holds: a future loader-metadata input adds keys
    to the same dictionary rather than changing its shape.

62. **`tests/Statesman.Hosting.Tests` is added, and it is this phase's only new project.**
    `Statesman.Extensions.Hosting` has no test project at all today — `AddStatesmanHosting` and
    `StatesmanHostedService` are referenced only by the two samples — so the health check would
    otherwise land in `Statesman.DependencyInjection.Tests`, which references a different package and
    would need two project references to reach it. *Rejected: `Statesman.EndToEnd.Tests`*, which is an
    ASP.NET Core web test project and would tie the health check's tests to a web host. *Rejected:
    shipping the health check untested*, which is not a real option. The cost is `eng/validate.py`'s
    `projects` metric moving 41 to 42 and one `Statesman.slnx` entry; `src/Directory.Build.props`'s
    net10-only exclusion list is not involved, because test projects take their single `net10.0` target
    from `tests/Directory.Build.props`. **Reversal cost:** delete a directory and one solution line.

63. **The shared corruption contract is the refusal half only, and the provider-specific reader tests
    stay where they are.** Measured: all four import targets call `StateRecord.Validate()` before
    touching the store (`InMemoryStateLedgerStore.cs:225`, `FileSystemStateLedgerStore.cs:294`,
    `RedisStateLedgerStore.cs:269`, `EntityFrameworkStateLedgerStore.cs:605`), so "an invalid record is
    refused and the store is unchanged" is a genuine five-provider contract. The other half — what a
    reader observes after a damaged record — is only meaningful on the filesystem provider, whose
    change log is the one textual on-disk structure a crash can leave half-written. *Rejected:
    inventing a damage mechanism per provider* so the shared suite could assert something on all five,
    which would test the test harness. *Rejected: relaxing the shared assertion until every provider
    passes it*, which is how a conformance suite stops discriminating. The controller's brief already
    anticipated this ("design the shared contract at the level the providers share"); this decision
    records where that level turned out to be. **Reversal cost:** none; nothing is moved.

64. **`IStatesmanDiagnostics` deliberately does not extend `IStateCapability`, and a test pins that.**
    `CapabilityMatrixTests`' completeness half reflects over every `IStateCapability` implementer in
    `Statesman.Abstractions` and requires a row in `docs/architecture/capabilities.md` and a tuple in
    the test for each. A runtime diagnostics surface is not a store capability, has no provider column,
    and has no forwarding-policy classification, because `TieredStateLedgerStore` never sees it. This is
    the same deliberate exclusion `IStateCapabilityProvider` already carries. *Rejected: making it a
    capability* so it inherits `TryGetCapability` for free, which would put a row in a store matrix for
    something no store implements. **Reversal cost:** none; the pinning test is three lines.

65. **The tiered distributed-capture conformance subclass uses an Entity Framework Core cold store, not
    the in-memory cold store every other tiered subclass uses.** Measured: tiered over in-memory throws
    `NotSupportedException` naming the cold tier for `CaptureAsync`, because capture always delegates to
    cold and the in-memory provider has no `IDistributedCapture`. A tiered store's capture behaviour is
    only observable over a capture-capable cold tier. *Rejected: skipping the tiered capture subclass*,
    which would leave the one provider whose capture behaviour is pure delegation untested. *Rejected:
    a Redis cold tier*, which would make the subclass skip whenever `STATESMAN_TEST_REDIS` is unset,
    where an Entity Framework Core SQLite cold tier always runs. **Reversal cost:** one fixture.

66. **`ConformanceProviders` becomes the single construction point, and the seven shipped subclasses are
    re-pointed at it in a task that changes no test count.** Six new suites times five providers is
    thirty more store constructions; inlining them would mean seven copies of each provider's setup, and
    the filesystem cleanup and Redis connection handling are exactly the things that rot when copied.
    *Rejected: leaving the two shipped suites alone and adding a second construction path*, which is the
    lower-risk edit and leaves the repository with two ways to build the same fixture. *Rejected: a
    `[Theory]` over provider factories instead of an abstract base per suite*, which would collapse
    thirty subclass files to six and is the shape a greenfield suite would take — but it turns each
    provider's skip reason into a runtime branch inside a data row, and it would leave the repository
    carrying two different conformance shapes at once. The exit check for this task is that
    `Statesman.Conformance.Tests` reports `total: 75` with the same twenty skips before and after.
    **Reversal cost:** mechanical either way.

## Addendum (pre-Phase-17, 2026-09-13)

Decisions taken while writing the Phase 17 section and its implementation plan. **As in Phases 8
through 16, they were taken without `AskUserQuestion` because the session ran under an autonomous
`/goal`.** These are the ones to revisit if that ruling was wrong, and each is listed with the
alternative rejected and what reversing it would cost.

**The two most worth revisiting are decision 72 — which changes the behaviour of a shipped public
method, `StateCapabilityExtensions.TryGetCapability<TCapability>`, without changing its shape, so no
gate can catch it — and decision 71, which lets a single degraded loader turn a whole root's health
check Degraded.** The first is the only change in this phase a consumer could notice without reading
the changelog; the second is a policy choice about what "degraded" means to an operator's alerting.

**Numbering continues the pre-Phase-16 addendum, which ended at 66.**

67. **Phase 17 is ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 2, the Phase 16 deferred-minors sweep, and the tiered
    capability-discovery defect. Nine tasks.** Two sweep tasks, one discovery task, four
    load-diagnostics tasks, one provider round-trip task, one close-out. *Rejected: also taking bullet 5
    (filesystem verify and repair) and the corruption runbook*, which the controller's brief left
    conditional on the phase fitting under the nine-task budget. It does not: a verify surface, a repair
    surface, their conformance coverage and an operator page is two to three tasks more, and the brief's
    own budget rule says park rather than squeeze. They are the first Phase 18 items, and the measured
    filesystem layout is written into this phase's parked list so Phase 18's planner starts from an
    inventory rather than a re-measurement. *Rejected: splitting the sweep out into its own phase*, which
    would leave two telemetry changes — the extended tag gate and the new load instrument — landing a
    phase apart on the same test file. **Reversal cost:** none; the tasks are independent apart from the
    ordering decision 70 records.

68. **Per-source timing splits: three bounded keys on the record, the per-source breakdown in the typed
    report and on a meter instrument.** The record gains `statesman.load.duration.ms`,
    `statesman.load.started` and `statesman.load.completed` — a fixed three, independent of how many
    sources a state declares. *Rejected: the controller's lean, a `statesman.source.<name>.duration` key
    beside the existing `.status` key.* That lean called itself "a small, bounded set" while naming a
    per-source key, which is unbounded in the number of declared sources. Measured:
    `StateRecord.Metadata` is serialized JSON on every provider — inside `head.json` and every
    `history/<revision>.json` on the filesystem, inside the Redis record blob, into the `MetadataJson`
    column on both the head and the history entity on Entity Framework Core — so a key costs roughly 38
    to 40 permanent bytes **per record, including every history record**, and no provider needs a schema
    change. The existing `.status` key already pays that unbounded term once; doubling it buys an
    operator a fact they can get from the live report or from a metrics backend, where high cardinality
    is an explicit opt-in rather than a storage cost nobody chose. *Rejected: no record keys at all, the
    side channel only*, which would mean a restart or an export loses every trace that a load was slow —
    the one thing a stored record is for. **What it costs if wrong:** an operator reading a stored
    history record can see that a load took four seconds but not which source was slow. **Reversal
    cost:** adding the per-source key later is additive and needs no migration, exactly because of the
    measurement above.

69. **The typed load surface is two new members on `IStatesmanDiagnostics`, not a new interface.**
    `ReadLoadDiagnostics()` and `ClearLoadDiagnostics()` join `ReadMaintenanceFailures()` and
    `ClearMaintenanceFailures()` behind the one `StatesmanDiagnosticsExtensions.TryGetDiagnostics` entry
    point. Measured free against the package-validation gate: `IStatesmanDiagnostics` shipped in Phase
    16, **after** the `0.3.0` tag that `PackageValidationBaselineVersion` names, so the baseline contains
    no such type and nothing about it can break. Proved by packing `Statesman.Abstractions` with a probe
    member added — zero `CP` diagnostics — beside a positive control that added a member to `IStatesman`
    and produced `error CP0006` once per target framework, confirming the gate was live in exactly that
    configuration. *Rejected: a separate `IStatesmanLoadDiagnostics`*, which is what the controller's
    brief hedged toward and which would give a consumer two discovery calls and two interfaces for one
    runtime's diagnostics. *Rejected: a member on `IStatesman`*, which is the `CP0006` the positive
    control just demonstrated. **What it costs if wrong:** any future external implementer of
    `IStatesmanDiagnostics` — there are none today — must implement four members rather than two.
    **Reversal cost:** high after the next release, near zero before one, since the interface is not yet
    in a published baseline.

    *Amended (final review, 2026-09-13):* neither the pre-existing maintenance-failure members nor the
    new load-diagnostics members call `ThrowIfDisposed()` on the shipped runtime. This is deliberate,
    not an oversight: the new members match the existing two exactly, and two methods on one interface
    behaving differently after disposal would be worse than all four being lenient. A post-dispose read
    returns a stale-but-valid snapshot and mutates nothing. If a later phase wants throwing behaviour, it
    must change **all four** members together — a breaking behaviour change on a shipped surface that
    needs its own decision, not a partial fix to one or two.

70. **One new instrument, `statesman.load.source.duration`, a `Histogram<double>` with unit `ms` and a
    fifth tag key.** It carries the four documented keys plus `statesman.source`, so an operator gets the
    per-source breakdown in their metrics backend where cardinality is their own choice. Milliseconds
    rather than seconds *deliberately*: `statesman.operation.duration` already records `ms`, and decision
    60 keeps that deviation through the whole `0.x` line, so shipping a second duration in seconds would
    put two unit conventions inside one meter and make the 1.0 correction two migrations instead of one.
    No shipped instrument is renamed or re-united, so decision 60 holds. *Rejected: no new instrument*,
    which would leave the per-source timing readable only in-process, through a bounded report that a
    restart clears. *Rejected: a whole-load histogram as well*, which measures the same interval
    `statesman.operation.duration` already measures under operation `refresh` — two names for one number.
    This is why the task extending the telemetry tag gate to all six per-state instruments must land
    **before** the task adding the seventh: the gate becomes a per-instrument map of expected tag keys,
    and the new instrument adds a row to it. **Reversal cost:** one instrument; adding a counter or
    histogram is additive, removing one is not.

71. **A latest load report whose completeness is `partial` or `initial-fallback` makes
    `StatesmanHealthCheck` Degraded.** It joins the two existing degraded conditions — maintenance
    failures retained, and a store maintaining without a lease — and matches the reading the check's own
    remarks already give them: "the authoritative transitions are still landing while something around
    them is not." Not Unhealthy, because the state is usable and authoritative; `complete`, `seeded` and
    `retained` are healthy. It self-corrects without a drain call, because reports are retained
    latest-per-address and the next complete refresh of that address replaces the degraded one.
    *Rejected: no status effect, `Data` keys only*, which would ship a health check that reads load
    diagnostics and then ignores what they say — the shape the ROADMAP bullet's "health summaries" is
    asking not to get. *Rejected: Unhealthy on `initial-fallback`*, which would take a root out of a load
    balancer for serving a declared initial value, which is the fallback working as designed. **What it
    costs if wrong:** an operator whose loader fails transiently sees Degraded until that address next
    refreshes, which on a long freshness window can be a while. **Reversal cost:** one condition in one
    method, plus the tests that assert it.

72. **A store that implements `IStateCapabilityProvider` is authoritative for its own capability
    discovery, and `TieredStateLedgerStore` answers a delegated capability only when the tier it
    delegates to can back it.** Two coordinated edits, because either alone is inert:
    `StateCapabilityExtensions.TryGetCapability<TCapability>` casts the store first and returns on
    success, so for a Tiered store — which declares five capability interfaces on its own type — the
    `IStateCapabilityProvider` branch below that cast is dead for exactly those five. The extension
    therefore asks an `IStateCapabilityProvider` first and does **not** fall back to the cast when it
    answers no, since a fallback would re-answer `this` and undo the fix. *Rejected: fixing
    `TieredStateLedgerStore.TryGetCapability` alone*, which is what the controller's brief proposed and
    which would have shipped a change no caller could observe. *Rejected: removing the five interfaces
    from `TieredStateLedgerStore`'s declaration*, which is the structurally honest answer and is a
    `CP0006`/`CP0008` break on a published, baselined type, needing a suppression entry for what this
    repository treats as a correctness fix; it would also flip cells in
    `docs/architecture/capabilities.md` and rewrite `CapabilityMatrixTests`. **Measured blast radius:
    exactly one shipped test fails**,
    `TieredChangeNotifierTests.SubscribeAsync_throws_naming_the_cold_tier_when_cold_has_no_notifier`,
    whose comment states the old contract in words ("Discovery still succeeds ... and the honest answer
    arrives at first use") and which is rewritten rather than deleted; `Statesman.Conformance.Tests` did
    not move at `total: 219` with or without live Redis, and `Statesman.Capabilities.Tests` stayed
    `3`/`0` because its matrix is type reflection, not runtime discovery. `dotnet pack` produced zero
    `CP` diagnostics on both affected packages. **What it costs if wrong:** the package-validation gate
    cannot see a behaviour-only change, so a consumer who wrote
    `if (tiered.TryGetCapability(out IDistributedCapture? c))` and relied on the true answer followed by
    a `NotSupportedException` at first use now takes the else branch instead. That is the defect being
    fixed, and it is a `### Changed` changelog entry rather than a silent improvement. **Reversal cost:**
    two methods; no stored data and no API shape is involved.

73. **Load reports are retained latest-per-address, bounded at
    `StatesmanDiagnostics.MaxRetainedLoadReports` = 64, evicting the oldest-completed first.** A
    partitioned state's address space is unbounded, so a map keyed by address with no bound is the same
    leak Phase 15 closed for maintenance failures and would be a worse one, because a report holds a
    per-source list. Sixty-four matches `MaxRetainedMaintenanceFailures` so an operator learns one
    number rather than two. *Rejected: one report per declared state path rather than per address*, which
    bounds the map by the manifest but throws away the partition that was slow — the fact most worth
    having on a partitioned state. *Rejected: no bound, on the argument that a report is small*, which is
    the argument Phase 15 already disproved for exceptions. **Reversal cost:** one constant, plus the
    tests that assert the eviction arithmetic.

74. **A load that throws records no report.** `RequireAll` with any source failure, and a `BestEffort`
    load where no source produced state and no initial value applied, both throw out of
    `RuntimeDefinition.LoadAsync` before an outcome exists; `StateHandle.RefreshAsync` catches them and
    writes a fault snapshot, which `statesman.faults` and the snapshot's own `StateError` already
    surface. A `partial` or `initial-fallback` load completes and does record one, which is where the
    faulted-source detail an operator wants actually lives. *Rejected: threading a report sink through
    the throw paths*, which adds a parameter and a `finally` to every exit of a method whose failure mode
    is already reported twice. **Reversal cost:** a sink parameter on one internal method.

    *Amended (final review, 2026-09-13):* the mitigation sentence overstated what actually reaches an
    operator. The fault is counted on the `statesman.faults` counter and the failed sources' names do
    reach the snapshot's own `StateError` message — but no per-source timing is recorded for that
    attempt on any channel: not the record, not a retained report, not the
    `statesman.load.source.duration` histogram, because the per-source histogram loop
    (`StateHandle.cs:322-327`) runs only after `LoadAsync` returns, which a throw never does. This is a
    design limit the decision accepted deliberately, not a Phase 17 defect. If closed, the cheap shape
    is attaching the already-populated `sourceReports` to the thrown `AggregateException` (via `Data`,
    or a small internal exception type) so `StateHandle.RefreshAsync`'s existing `catch` can record the
    histogram measurements without threading a sink parameter through `LoadAsync`'s signature — a
    Phase 18 candidate, not a change to this decision.

75. **The telemetry tag gate is extended to all six per-state instruments; the documentation is not
    weakened.** `docs/reference/telemetry.md:33-35` claims the four tag keys on `statesman.reads`,
    `.commits`, `.refreshes`, `.conflicts`, `.faults` and `.operation.duration`, and the claim is **true
    in code** — all six call sites pass `StatesmanTelemetry.Tags(...)`. The gate observed only
    `statesman.commits`. *Rejected: narrowing the documentation sentence to the one gated instrument*,
    which would make a true statement false to make a test's job easier. The extended gate drives one
    runtime through a read, a commit, a refresh, a conflict (a mismatched `ExpectedRevision`, counted
    before `StateConcurrencyException` is thrown) and a fault (a source that throws under `RequireAll`).
    The same task replaces the instrument set's `HashSet` equality — whose failure message truncates —
    with sorted `name|kind|unit` string arrays, identical semantics and a readable diff. **Reversal
    cost:** test-only.

76. **`BrokenStoreConformanceTests.cs` splits by capability into seven files.** Measured mechanical
    rather than assumed: 511 lines, one sealed class, seven self-contained sections of two facts plus
    their doubles, and **no file-level shared helper** — the last thirty lines are the final double's
    members and the closing braces. Fourteen facts stay fourteen. *Rejected: leaving it*, which is what Phase 16's
    final review deferred the judgement on and which grows again the next time a shared suite lands.
    *Rejected: splitting the doubles into one file and the facts into another*, which separates each
    proof from the thing it proves. **Reversal cost:** mechanical either way.

77. **The shared `PruneFailingStore` goes to `tests/Shared/`, not to `Statesman.Testing`.**
    `tests/Shared/` is the established mechanism — `PausingTimeProvider` and
    `EntityFrameworkTestProvider` already live there in `namespace Statesman.TestHelpers`, linked by
    `<Compile Include ... Link ...>` — and the Hosting copy's own doc comment, claiming the assemblies
    "have no shared test helper", is the false premise this corrects. *Rejected: `Statesman.Testing`*,
    which is a **shipped package**; `PausingTimeProvider`'s doc comment already records that ruling in
    the same words ("the shipped testing package should not grow a concurrency gadget"). The shared
    double takes the stronger of the three shapes: the failure index comes from the
    `Interlocked.Increment` return value, not from a separate read afterwards, which is a real race in
    the `RuntimeMaintenanceFailureBoundTests` copy. **Reversal cost:** one file and two
    `<Compile Include>` lines.

78. **The import-rejection assertion is strengthened so its comment becomes true, rather than the
    comment being deleted.** `ImportRejectionConformanceTests.cs:37-38` claims revision zero is
    `StateRecord.Validate`'s first numeric guard; reading `Ledger.cs:48-54` confirms it, and the
    assertion — `Assert.ThrowsAnyAsync<ArgumentException>` — could not tell that guard from any other.
    It becomes `Assert.ThrowsAsync<ArgumentOutOfRangeException>` with `ParamName` `Revision`. Measured
    green at baseline on all four import targets with live Redis (`total: 15`, `failed: 0`, three tiered
    skips), and the negative half still discriminates because the validation-skipping double throws
    nothing at all. *Rejected: deleting the guard-order claim from the comment*, which is the cheaper fix
    and leaves the suite one assertion weaker. **Reversal cost:** one assertion.

79. **`_maintenanceFailuresReported` moves under `_maintenanceFailuresGate`, with no test, and the phase
    says so.** `StatesmanRuntime.cs:448` increments it before the lock that `ReadMaintenanceFailures`
    reads all three counters and the queue under, so a concurrent reader can see `Reported` ahead of
    `Retained` for a handful of instructions. *Rejected: a test that tries to observe the window*, which
    would be timing-dependent on a few instructions and would be the flaky test this effort has twice
    ruled against writing. *Rejected: leaving it, since it self-corrects*, which keeps a
    self-inconsistent read reachable in a snapshot API whose whole promise is consistency. The evidence
    is that the increment now sits under the same gate as the read, and that `Statesman.Tests` stays at
    `total: 105`. **Reversal cost:** one line.

## Addendum (pre-Phase-18, 2026-09-13)

Decisions taken while writing the Phase 18 section and its implementation plan. **As in Phases 8
through 17, they were taken without `AskUserQuestion` because the session ran under an autonomous
`/goal`.** These are the ones to revisit if that ruling was wrong, and each is listed with the
alternative rejected and what reversing it would cost.

**The two most worth revisiting are decision 83 — which lets repair *write* two kinds of record file
rather than only report them, so a repair pass creates data an operator did not hand it — and decision
82, whose `RepairAsync()` parameterless overload is a dry run where `CompactChangeLogAsync()`'s
parameterless overload applies, a deliberate asymmetry inside one provider's maintenance surface.**
Everything else this phase decides is additive, report-only, or test-only.

**Numbering continues the pre-Phase-17 addendum, which ended at 79.**

80. **Verify and repair are methods on `FileSystemStateLedgerStore`, not on `Statesman.Tooling`.**
    Measured rather than preferred: `Statesman.Tooling.csproj` references only
    `Statesman.Abstractions`, the repository holds **zero** `[InternalsVisibleTo]` attributes, and the
    pair needs nine private members of the provider — `StreamDirectory`, `HistoryFile`, `HeadFile`,
    `ChangeFeedFile`, `TryParseChangeFeedLine`, `ReadFileAsync`, `AtomicWriteAsync`, `_changeFeedGate`
    and `ResetChangeFeedIndexUnsafe`.

    *Amended (Phase 18 close-out, 2026-09-13):* the shipped `FileSystemLedgerRecovery.cs` consumes
    **thirteen** private symbols of the provider, not nine — measured by reading every identifier in
    that file that resolves to a `FileSystemStateLedgerStore` member and confirming each one's
    accessibility in `FileSystemStateLedgerStore.cs`: the fields `_rootDirectory` and
    `_changeFeedGate`; the methods `Gate`, `ReadFileAsync`, `AtomicWriteAsync`, `StreamDirectory`,
    `HeadFile`, `HistoryFile`, `TryParseChangeFeedLine`, `DecodeChangeFeedLine` and
    `ReadLatestUnsafeAsync`; the property `ChangeFeedFile`; and the private nested record type
    `FileRecord` itself, whose `Root`/`Path`/`Partition`/`Revision`/`GlobalPosition` members
    `VerifyStreamAsync`, `RewriteHeadAsync` and `RestoreHistoryFileAsync` all read or construct
    directly. The pre-flight estimate undercounted four (`_rootDirectory`, `Gate`,
    `DecodeChangeFeedLine`, `ReadLatestUnsafeAsync` — all needed once repair, not just verify, was
    designed) and listed one the shipped code never calls directly
    (`ResetChangeFeedIndexUnsafe`, reached only indirectly through the public `CompactChangeLogAsync`
    repair delegates to). The decision's conclusion is unaffected — a larger private surface makes the
    case for keeping verify and repair on the provider itself stronger, not weaker. *Rejected: `Statesman.Tooling`*, which is what the controller's
    brief offered as the alternative to its own lean, and which costs either a provider project
    reference inside the portable export/restore package — breaking the property that `Statesman.Tooling`
    composes only capability interfaces, which `docs/providers/index.md:181` states as its whole
    guarantee — or nine new public members that exist solely to be consumed once, from one caller, in
    one assembly. *Rejected: a first `[InternalsVisibleTo]`*, which would spend a property preserved
    through eighteen phases on a convenience. **What it costs if wrong:** an operator who wants to
    verify a store directory without instantiating the provider cannot; they must construct a
    `FileSystemStateLedgerStore` over the directory first, which is one line and what every other
    maintenance call on this provider already requires. **Reversal cost:** high — moving the surface
    later is a package-level break on a baselined type.

81. **Nine finding kinds, not the six Phase 17 listed: one of the six is dropped and four are added.**
    *Dropped:* "a generation that moved under a live reader", which is not a durable on-disk state at
    all but an in-flight race already closed by the seqlock at `FileSystemStateLedgerStore.cs:1357-1431`
    — the artifact it leaves behind, `_changes.gen` holding a higher number, is the healthy result of
    every compaction, so no verify pass can tell the two apart. *Added:* `MalformedChangeLogLine`,
    because a non-final malformed line makes `ReadAsync` and `ListPartitionsAsync` **throw** rather than
    degrade and is therefore the most severe state in the set; `UnreadableRecordFile`, because one
    unparseable history file throws `JsonException` out of an uncapped feed read for the **whole store**,
    which `FileSystemChangeFeedTests.cs:667-687` already proves in shipped code; `OrphanedTemporaryFile`,
    because `AtomicWriteAsync`'s crash residue is invisible to every read path and accumulates forever;
    and `MisplacedStreamDirectory`, because a stream whose directory name is not the SHA-256 of its own
    records' address is reachable only by a directory walk, which is the one thing a verify pass does
    that no shipped code path does. *Rejected: reporting exactly the parked list's six*, which would
    ship a verify pass that cannot see the two states most likely to take a store down. **Reversal
    cost:** each kind is an enum member and a detection block; adding one later is additive, removing
    one is a break.

82. **`RepairAsync(CancellationToken)` is a dry run. `CompactChangeLogAsync(CancellationToken)` applies.**
    A deliberate asymmetry inside one maintenance surface, and the doc comment says so in words rather
    than leaving a reader to discover it. The reason is what the two methods can do at their worst:
    compaction only ever removes lines it has proved dereference to nothing, so a misread call costs a
    log rewrite and a re-scan, while repair renames record files and writes new ones, so a misread call
    costs an operator a change they did not ask for on a store they already believe is damaged.
    *Rejected: matching compaction, so that `RepairAsync()` applies*, which makes the surface uniform at
    the price of making the destructive member the easy one to call by accident. *Rejected: no
    parameterless overload at all, forcing every caller to write `RepairAsync(dryRun: false)`*, which is
    the unambiguous shape and was the close second — it loses the property that the zero-argument call
    an operator reaches for first is the safe one, which is the property actually being bought here.
    **What it costs if wrong:** an operator who writes `await store.RepairAsync()` expecting a repair
    gets a report and has to call again. **Reversal cost:** one overload body, plus the tests that
    assert the default.

83. **Repair writes `head.json` from the newest history file, and writes a missing history file from
    the head — it does not merely report them.** Both directions restore an invariant from data already
    on disk and destroy nothing, and one of them is closing real data loss rather than tidying: a stream
    with history but no head is skipped by `ListPartitionsAsync` (`:820-824`), so `Statesman.Tooling`,
    which enumerates that catalog, silently omits the entire stream from an export while every one of
    its records is still readable by address. The missing-history direction is safe for a separately
    measured reason: `PruneAsync` provably cannot delete the latest revision's file — `records.Count <= 1`
    returns early (`:372-375`), `MaxAge` and the tombstone filter both exempt `Revision == latest`
    (`:381`, `:387`), `MaxRevisions` keeps the tail of a revision-ascending list (`:391-394`), and the
    `MaxBytes` loop admits its first `OrderByDescending(revision)` entry unconditionally (`:400-410`) —
    so a head whose revision has no history file is anomalous rather than a normal retention outcome,
    and restoring it cannot resurrect something retention deliberately removed. *Rejected: report-only
    for both*, which is the conservative default and which would leave the export gap open with a
    report that names it — a recovery tool that can fix a backup-invisible stream and declines to is not
    recovery tooling. *Rejected: writing the head but not the history file*, which would leave the
    asymmetry unexplained and would leave a restorable `DanglingChangeLogLine` to be dropped by the
    compaction step instead of healed. **What it costs if wrong:** a repair pass creates two files an
    operator did not hand it, both byte-derived from records already in the same stream directory.
    **Reversal cost:** two branches and their tests.

84. **"Index rebuild" is the existing generation bump plus `ResetChangeFeedIndexUnsafe`, and ships as a
    ruling with no new member.** The change-log index is in-process and never persisted (`:68-116`), so
    there is nothing on disk to rebuild — but "documented no-op" would be wrong, because a log rewrite
    genuinely must invalidate this store's index, every other index in this process, and every reader's
    index in every other process, and each already has a mechanism: `ResetChangeFeedIndexUnsafe()`
    (`:1639`) under `_changeFeedGate` for the first two, and the `_changes.gen` bump taken **before** the
    rename (`:1078-1082`) for the third. Repair inherits both by routing its only log rewrite through
    `CompactChangeLogAsync`. The measurement that makes this more than bookkeeping: the index's
    length-plus-tail invalidation rule **cannot** see an out-of-band rewrite that preserves both the file
    length and the last line's bytes, which is precisely the shape of a hand-edit that corrects a field
    in place — so a repair that wrote the log itself, without the bump, would leave every other process
    serving lines that no longer exist. *Rejected: a public `RebuildIndexAsync`*, which is dead weight
    against a rule that already fires on every path a caller can reach through the provider, and which
    would be a new baselined member that can never be removed. *Rejected: documenting it as a no-op*,
    which is the answer the brief hedged toward and which is false in the one case that matters.
    **Reversal cost:** none; adding the member later is additive.

85. **The `RequireAll` per-source reports travel on `AggregateException.Data` under an internal const
    key, not on an exception subclass.** Decision 74's amendment named both shapes. Measured: no test in
    the repository references `AggregateException` at all, so the subclass is not blocked by a test —
    but `StateHandle.RecordFaultAsync` (`:617-620`) stamps `exception.GetType().FullName` into the fault
    snapshot's `StateError.Type`, and that snapshot is **written to the ledger** and surfaced to
    operators through the reason codes `docs/reference/diagnostics.md:7` documents. A subclass would
    therefore silently change a persisted, operator-facing field on every `RequireAll` fault, on a
    provider-independent code path, with no gate able to see it — the same class of invisible behaviour
    change decision 72 took deliberately and recorded loudly, taken here for a strictly smaller benefit.
    *Rejected: the internal exception subclass*, for that reason. *Rejected: threading a report sink
    through `LoadAsync`'s signature*, which decision 74 already rejected and which adds a parameter and a
    `finally` to four exits. **What it costs if wrong:** the reports reach `StateHandle` through an
    untyped `IDictionary` lookup that a rename cannot break at compile time, so the key and its single
    reader are pinned by a test rather than by the compiler. **Reversal cost:** two call sites and one
    const.

86. **None of the four `IStatesmanDiagnostics` members throws after disposal, and a test now pins it
    with one lever per member.** Decision 69's amendment ruled this deliberate on consistency grounds —
    two methods on one interface behaving differently after disposal would be worse than all four being
    lenient. This phase adds the measurement that makes it more than a preference:
    `StatesmanHealthCheck.CheckHealthAsync` calls `ReadMaintenanceFailures()` and `ReadLoadDiagnostics()`
    on every runtime in `_registry.All` (`:74`, `:77`) with no disposal check of its own, so a throwing
    surface would turn a health probe over a registry still holding a disposed root into an exception
    rather than a report — which is the opposite of what a health check is for. A post-dispose read
    returns a stale-but-valid snapshot and mutates nothing. *Rejected: making all four throw*, which is
    the other half of the amendment's "all four together" and which breaks the shipped health check.
    *Rejected: leaving it unpinned*, which is the status quo and which lets the next phase add
    `ThrowIfDisposed()` to one member without anything failing. **Reversal cost:** four lines and one
    test, plus a `### Changed` changelog entry, because it is a behaviour change on a shipped surface.

87. **`eng/validate.py` gains a `tracked_files` metric from `git ls-files`; `all_files` keeps its
    definition and is documented as monotone-only.** Phase 17's parked note blamed "untracked local
    files" for the drift. Measured at `e22dad8`: this machine has **zero** untracked files
    (`git status --porcelain -uall` prints nothing) and still shows 443 against `git ls-files`' 430. The
    thirteen are **git-ignored** local tool artefacts — `.claude/` (2), `.idea/` (6),
    `Statesman.sln.DotSettings.user`, `TestResults/` (4) — which `git status` never showed precisely
    because it honours `.gitignore`, and which `_ignored` (`:371-375`) does not cover. *Rejected:
    redefining `all_files` to mean tracked files*, which would silently invalidate the `all_files`
    numbers recorded in this spec's Phase 15, 16 and 17 exit criteria and in roughly thirty task reports
    — every one of them a measurement someone took. *Rejected: extending `_ignored` with `.idea`,
    `.claude`, `TestResults` and `*.user`*, a hand-maintained list of other people's tool directories
    that the next tool breaks, where `git ls-files` is already the exact answer and is already how this
    repository decides what a file is. *Rejected: doing nothing and stating monotonicity*, which the
    brief offered as the alternative and which leaves every future phase reconciling a number two agents
    cannot agree on. **What it costs if wrong:** the validator shells out to `git` once, so it is no
    longer strictly process-free; it stays offline, and `tracked_files` is **omitted** rather than
    guessed when `git` is absent or the call fails, so a source archive with no `.git` still validates.
    **Reversal cost:** one method and one metric key.

88. **Repair runs record-file repairs before the change-log compaction, and a `MalformedChangeLogLine`
    suppresses the change-log half while the record-file half still runs.** Two ordering rules, both
    load-bearing rather than incidental. The first: a `DanglingChangeLogLine` whose history file the
    same pass can restore from the head must be **healed**, not dropped — compaction would drop it,
    permanently losing that record's feed position, if it ran first. The second: `CompactChangeLogAsync`
    **throws** `CorruptChangeFeedLine` on a non-final malformed line rather than skipping it (`:572-578`),
    so repair must not call it in that state; and it must not silently drop the line either, because a
    malformed line may name a record that exists and dropping it loses a feed position silently — the
    exact outcome `CorruptChangeFeedLine` exists to prevent. The repair report carries a dedicated field
    saying the change-log half was skipped and why, rather than surfacing the exception. *Rejected:
    compaction first, then record repairs*, which is the order that reads more naturally and which
    destroys the recoverable case. *Rejected: letting the `InvalidDataException` propagate out of
    `RepairAsync`*, which would throw away the record-file repairs the same call had already completed
    and would give an operator an exception where they asked for a report. **Reversal cost:** the order
    of two calls, plus one field and the two facts that pin each operand.

89. **Phase 18 is ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 5, the corruption runbook, and the four Phase 17 carry-forwards.
    Nine tasks.** Four verify-and-repair tasks, one runbook task, three carry-forward tasks, one
    close-out. *Rejected: also taking retention conformance*, bullet 1's remainder, which was measured
    for this phase exactly as Phase 17 measured it and is still not a one-task lift: pruning uniformity
    is a per-provider question about eviction order and byte accounting, needing its own pre-flight probe
    of the kind Phase 16 ran before writing each shared suite. *Rejected: splitting verify and repair
    into two phases*, which would ship a verify pass with no cure for a whole release and would make the
    runbook — the thing that closes the 0.2 exit criterion — wait behind it for no gain. **Reversal
    cost:** none; tasks 1 to 4 are a chain, 6, 7 and 8 are independent of everything, and 5 needs only
    that 4 has landed.

## Addendum (pre-Phase-19, 2026-09-14)

Decisions taken while writing the Phase 19 section and its implementation plan. **As in Phases 8
through 18, they were taken without `AskUserQuestion` because the session ran under an autonomous
`/goal`.** These are the ones to revisit if that ruling was wrong, and each is listed with the
alternative rejected and what reversing it would cost.

**The two most worth revisiting are decision 90 — which pins a `MaxBytes` survivor set that is not a
contiguous suffix of the revisions, making a behaviour nobody has ever documented permanent by test —
and decision 98, which declares ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 1 shipped while one half of its own sentence is
covered by a design ruling rather than by the shared suite it names.** Everything else this phase
decides is test-only, documentation-only, or a fixture constant.

**Numbering continues the pre-Phase-18 addendum, which ended at 89.**

90. **`MaxBytes`'s non-contiguous survivor set is intended behaviour and is pinned, not changed.** The
    byte walk iterates revisions newest-first and uses `continue`, not `break`, so a record that would
    overflow the budget is skipped and older, smaller records are still considered. Measured with
    payloads `5,5,5,100,10,10` under a 30-byte budget: all five providers return `[2,3,5,6]`, a set with
    a hole in it. Four independent implementations — in-memory, filesystem, Redis and Entity Framework
    Core — contain four independent `continue`s, which is what makes this deliberate rather than a
    coincidence, and the behaviour retains strictly more revisions under the same budget, which is what
    the option exists for. *Rejected: changing the `continue` to a `break`*, which would make the
    survivor set a contiguous suffix and easier to describe in one sentence, at the price of a silent
    behaviour break on four providers with no complainant and fewer revisions retained under every
    budget where the set is not already contiguous. *Rejected: leaving it unpinned and undocumented*,
    which is the status quo and which lets the next phase "tidy" the loop into a `break` with nothing
    failing. **What it costs if wrong:** a user who assumed retention keeps a contiguous window now has
    a test and a documentation sentence telling them otherwise, rather than discovering it from a
    surprising history read. **Reversal cost:** one operator per provider, one assertion, one sentence
    — but it is a behaviour change on a shipped option and would need a `### Changed` entry.

91. **`MaxBytes` counts the serialized payload and nothing else, and `docs/concepts/ledger.md` says so
    explicitly.** Measured: in-memory and Redis use `StateRecord.PayloadLength`
    (`Payload?.LongLength ?? 0`), filesystem and Entity Framework Core compute the same expression
    inline. No file size, no key size, no JSON envelope, no metadata, no per-record overhead is counted
    anywhere, and a tombstone therefore costs zero bytes. The suite pins it with a fact that survives
    only under payload-only accounting: five revisions carrying twenty payload bytes in total, three of
    them tombstones, under a twenty-five byte budget — all five survive, where any provider charging
    its own on-disk or on-wire overhead would evict the tombstones. *Rejected: counting on-disk size*,
    which is arguably what a filesystem user means by "bytes" and which would make one option's meaning
    provider-dependent, the exact opposite of a conformance suite's purpose. *Rejected: pinning it but
    not documenting it*, on the reading that "maximum retained payload bytes" already says it — true
    for a careful reader, and inadequate for the filesystem provider, whose on-disk cost for a retained
    revision is much larger than its payload. **What it costs if wrong:** an operator sizing a byte
    budget against disk usage will under-count, and the documentation now tells them to. **Reversal
    cost:** high — it is the meaning of a shipped option.

92. **`ConformanceProviders.TieredOverInMemoryAsync` passes the injected clock to the hot tier as well
    as the cold one.** A one-word fixture change. Today cold gets the clock and hot is constructed with
    the default constructor, so hot runs on `TimeProvider.System` while cold runs on the test's clock;
    under `MaxAge` with a `ManualTimeProvider` the two tiers would compute different cutoffs. It is
    invisible through `ReadHistoryAsync`, which delegates straight to cold, which is why three phases of
    suites never noticed. Measured: no existing suite depends on hot's clock, and the task that makes
    the change runs the **whole** conformance project rather than only the new suite, so the claim is
    tested rather than asserted. *Rejected: asserting only through the tiered façade*, which would let
    the suite pass while the two tiers disagreed — a shared suite that arranges not to ask the awkward
    question is worth less than no suite. *Rejected: changing `TieredStateLedgerStore` to propagate a
    clock*, which is production code for a test-fixture defect. **What it costs if wrong:** a tiered
    fixture whose hot tier now moves with the test clock could change an unrelated suite's timing
    expectations; the full-project run is what would catch that. **Reversal cost:** one word.

93. **The lease conformance suite's single `LeaseTtl` splits into `HoldTtl` and `ExpiringTtl`.** The
    measured cause of the flake is that the stale-holder fact spends **two** Redis round trips between
    the successor's acquire and its renew, inside a TTL chosen to be short enough to expire during a
    different test's wait. Proof both ways, ten iterations per cell: with both roles on 200 ms an
    injected 250 ms stall fails 10/10; with the hold role on 30 s a 1000 ms stall passes 10/10.
    *Rejected: a polling `ExpireAsync` that watches the key disappear instead of sleeping 400 ms*,
    which the brief offered and which measurement shows cannot close this flake at all — it shortens
    the wait *before* the successor acquires and widens nothing after it — and which cannot even be
    expressed without new fixture surface, since `ExpireAsync()` takes no parameters and
    `ConformanceStore` exposes no connection or key name. *Rejected: scaling both TTLs up together*,
    which changes nothing: the budget and the wait scale in lockstep. *Rejected: a server-side
    `PEXPIRE key 1`*, which would make expiry instantaneous but would gut
    `Renew_returns_false_after_the_lease_expired_with_nobody_racing`, whose whole point is that the TTL
    bounds the hold rather than that a deleted key behaves like an expired one. **What it costs if
    wrong:** the suite acquires 30-second Redis leases under unique GUID ids and disposes every one of
    them, so a crashed run leaves at most a handful of keys that expire on their own within half a
    minute. **Reversal cost:** one abstract member, two subclass overrides, and re-pointing the
    acquisitions.

94. **Prune cancellation is cited from the retention suite, not re-asserted in it.**
    `CancellationConformanceTests.An_already_cancelled_token_is_honoured_by_every_store_operation`
    already asserts it for `PruneAsync` on all five providers. *Rejected: re-asserting it in the
    retention suite*, on the argument that a reader of the retention suite should find retention's whole
    contract there — which buys navigability and pays for it with two assertions of one rule that can
    drift apart, the exact failure mode decision 68 avoided for a different duplicated statement.
    **What it costs if wrong:** a reader of `RetentionConformanceTests` has to follow a cross-reference
    in the class remark. **Reversal cost:** one fact.

95. **The retention suite runs against Redis in CI's existing `redis-tests` job, with no workflow
    edit.** `ConformanceProviders.RedisAsync` already returns null without `STATESMAN_TEST_REDIS`, the
    suite turns that into an `Assert.SkipUnless` with a reason, and `redis-tests` already sets the
    variable and already runs this project. Measured locally both ways: `total: 311` with `24` skips
    against live Redis and `84` skips without it, the difference being exactly the fourteen Redis
    retention facts. *Rejected: a new job or a new environment variable*, which would be
    infrastructure for a suite the existing job already covers. **What it costs if wrong:** nothing
    silently — an unset variable produces a named skip with a reason, never a pass. **Reversal cost:**
    none.

96. **The three near-identical `OrphanedTemporaryFile` loops are not refactored, and Phase 18's minor
    M-a is closed rather than carried forward.** Measured rather than judged: a shared
    `AddOrphanedTemporaries(directory, findings)` helper is eight lines replacing fifteen with three, a
    net saving of **four lines**, and the middle loop sits inside a `Directory.Exists(historyDirectory)`
    guard the other two do not have, so the three are not actually identical in the context that
    matters. *Rejected: taking the refactor because the minor was recorded*, which treats a carried
    note as an obligation rather than a hypothesis; a deferred minor's next phase is entitled to
    measure it and decline. **What it costs if wrong:** a fourth stream-walk site would want the
    helper; adding it then is the same eight lines. **Reversal cost:** none.

    *Amended (Phase 21, 2026-09-14):* the saving is **three** lines, not four, and the helper does
    compile — both measured rather than reasoned. Written from the three loops it would replace,
    `dotnet build src/Statesman.Persistence.FileSystem` is `0 errors, 0 warnings`,
    `Statesman.FileSystem.Tests` is unchanged at `total: 84`, and `git diff --stat` is
    `12 insertions(+), 15 deletions(-)`. The body is eight lines as claimed; the blank line separating
    it from its neighbour makes nine. The conclusion, decline it, is unaffected. The Phase 20 caveat
    "whether the declined `AddOrphanedTemporaries` helper compiles" is therefore closed and is not
    carried forward.

97. **Phase 18's minor M-c is closed by a lever-only step that ships no code.** No dedicated
    break-the-mechanism lever isolates "`MisplacedStreamDirectory` is never acted on", because it shares
    repair's `default` switch arm with `OrphanedTemporaryFile`, which does have one. The fact that
    would assert it already exists —
    `FileSystemLedgerRepairTests.A_torn_tail_a_temporary_file_and_a_misplaced_directory_are_reported_unrepaired`
    — so what was missing was the proof that it discriminates, not the assertion. The task therefore
    makes the `default` arm swallow that one kind, observes the exact failure, and reverts. Measured
    while planning: `Assert.Equal() Failure: Collections differ … Expected: [TornChangeLogTail,
    OrphanedTemporaryFile, MisplacedStreamDirectory] Actual: [TornChangeLogTail,
    OrphanedTemporaryFile]`. *Rejected: adding a dedicated test*, which would assert the same thing the
    existing fact asserts. *Rejected: deferring it a second time*, which is what the previous phase did
    and which is why it is still open. **What it costs if wrong:** nothing ships, so nothing can be
    wrong in the repository; the risk is that the lever is left applied, which the task's own re-run
    and the commit's diff both catch. **Reversal cost:** none.

98. **ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 1 is declared shipped, with its corruption half stated as a design ruling
    rather than left as an implied shortfall.** The bullet names six contracts — compare-and-swap,
    exact imports, retention, ordering, cancellation, corruption behaviour. Five are in the shared
    suite after this phase. The sixth is covered in two parts: the import-refusal half is shared
    (`ImportRejectionConformanceTests`), and everything beyond it is per-provider **by decision**, Phase
    16 decision 63, because the other four providers have no textual structure a crash can leave
    half-written and a shared suite would have to invent a damage mechanism for them — a ruling Phase 18
    then acted on by shipping the filesystem provider's own verify, repair and runbook. A bullet whose
    last qualifier is a recorded design decision with delivered per-provider coverage behind it is
    shipped, not partial. *Rejected: leaving bullet 1 partial until corruption is shared*, which keeps
    a bullet open against a thing this effort has ruled four times it will not build, and which makes
    "0.2 is not complete" say less each time because the reason stops being a gap and becomes a
    footnote. *Rejected: declaring the 0.2 exit criterion "all built-in providers pass one conformance
    suite" met without qualification*, which would overstate: the criterion is met for every contract
    the suite covers, and the sentence must keep saying which contract it does not. **What it costs if
    wrong:** a reader takes "bullet 1 ships" as "corruption is cross-provider", which is why the bullet
    text must carry the ruling in the same sentence. **Reversal cost:** one paragraph, and it is
    documentation only.

99. **Phase 20's target is ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 8, analyzer code fixes. Bullet 6 needs a key-layout
    ruling before it can be a phase, and bullet 4 stays parked.** Bullet 8 is the only one of the three
    that is both bounded and free of a storage-format or infrastructure design decision: three rules
    (`STM001`, `STM002`, `STM003`), one new `Statesman.CodeFixes` project referencing
    `Microsoft.CodeAnalysis.CSharp.Workspaces` and packaged into the analyzer nupkg, one new test
    package reference, no storage format, no CI service. `STM002` has an obvious fix
    (`{ get; set; }` to `{ get; init; }`), `STM003` a plausible one, and `STM001` none that is safe to
    automate, so that rule should ship documentation rather than a fix. Bullet 6 is a **design change
    to the stored key shape** — a hash tag around six key builders, with two deliberately cross-slot
    `ITransaction` sites and two Lua scripts whose `KEYS` span four different keys — with a CI job
    attached, not a CI job alone. Bullet 4 is a storage-format break touching every provider's stored
    shape and `Statesman.Tooling`'s export format. *Rejected: Redis Cluster as Phase 20*, which would
    start a phase on an unanswered design question. *Rejected: serializer envelopes as Phase 20*, which
    is the only remaining 0.2 item that can break a store already on disk and belongs with a planned
    format break. **What it costs if wrong:** none of the three is urgent; the ordering is a scheduling
    claim, not a commitment. **Reversal cost:** none.

100. **Phase 19 is nine tasks: four on one new shared suite, one documentation task, one lease fix, two
     carry-forward sweeps, and a close-out.** Tasks 1 to 4 are a chain on one file — scaffolding and
     `MaxRevisions`, then `MaxAge` and `MaxBytes`, then feed, head and catalog parity, then the
     discrimination doubles — because each appends to the same abstract class and its static entry
     points. Tasks 6, 7 and 8 are disjoint from that chain and from each other. Task 5 needs Tasks 1 to
     4 landed so the documentation describes what shipped. *Rejected: folding the four suite tasks into
     one*, which would put roughly twenty facts and five subclasses behind a single review and is the
     shape Phases 16 and 18 both split for good reason. *Rejected: taking Redis Cluster or the analyzer
     code fixes in the same phase*, which would give one phase two unrelated exit criteria. **Reversal
     cost:** none; the task boundaries are review checkpoints, not code boundaries.

## Addendum (pre-Phase-20, 2026-09-14)

Decisions taken while writing the Phase 20 section and its implementation plan. **As in Phases 8 through
19, they were taken without `AskUserQuestion` because the session ran under an autonomous `/goal`.** These
are the ones to revisit if that ruling was wrong, and each is listed with the alternative rejected and what
reversing it would cost.

**The two most worth revisiting are decision 105 — which widens a shipped rule's reach, the only behaviour
change in the phase and the only one that can newly warn a consumer who changed nothing — and decision 108,
which ships a five-type `catch` set of which two types are justified by a measured inventory rather than by
any test that fails without them.**

**Numbering continues the pre-Phase-19 addendum, which ended at 100.**

101. **`Statesman.CodeFixes` is one new project inside the existing `Statesman.Analyzers` package, not a
     twenty-third package.** Decision 99's two halves — "one new `Statesman.CodeFixes` project" and "packaged
     into the analyzer nupkg" — are resolved in favour of the second, and the spec's "one new package" at
     the Phase 19 parked bullet is amended in place. A second **assembly** is mandatory and measured:
     RS1038 forbids an analyzer assembly from referencing `Microsoft.CodeAnalysis.Workspaces`, and
     `EnforceExtendedAnalyzerRules` plus `TreatWarningsAsErrors` makes it an error, one per analyzer type. A
     second **package** is not forced by anything. Measured end to end: 22 nupkgs, both DLLs under
     `analyzers/dotnet/cs`, zero `CP` diagnostics, and `dotnet restore Statesman.slnx --force` clean with no
     NU1608 against the six `Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.Design` pins, which each already set
     `CentralPackageTransitivePinningEnabled=false` for precisely that conflict. *Rejected: a separate
     `Statesman.CodeFixes` nupkg*, which would make consumers install two packages to get the light bulb for
     a diagnostic they already receive, add a twenty-third package to
     `docs/reference/api-compatibility.md`'s "fifteen of twenty-two" arithmetic, and need its own
     `PackageValidationBaselineVersion` blank. *Rejected: putting the provider in the analyzer assembly*,
     which does not compile. **What it costs if wrong:** a consumer who wants the analyzers without the
     Workspaces-dependent code fixes cannot decline them; the DLL is 14 KB and loads only in an IDE.
     **Reversal cost:** flip `IsPackable`, delete one `None` item, add a baseline blank, and update the
     package count in three documents.

102. **The test harness is the flavour-agnostic `Microsoft.CodeAnalysis.CSharp.CodeFix.Testing` at 1.1.4,
     with `DefaultVerifier` and a local no-op provider.** The version is measured rather than assumed: the
     research pinned 1.1.2 without checking, and the NuGet flat-container index lists exactly 1.1.0 through
     **1.1.4**, with 1.1.4 the newest stable. It restores, compiles and runs under xunit v3 here. The
     `.XUnit` flavour is not an option at all: it depends on `xunit.assert` 2.3.0 alongside this
     repository's `xunit.v3.assert` 4.0.0, producing `CS0433` on every existing `Assert` call site, and its
     `XUnitVerifier` is `[Obsolete]`, which `TreatWarningsAsErrors` turns into an error. One measured
     consequence the plan carries: the package supplies `CSharpCodeFixTest<,,>` but **no** C# analyzer-only
     test, which lives in the separate `Microsoft.CodeAnalysis.CSharp.Analyzer.Testing` package. Rather than
     pin a second package, the harness ships a six-line `NoFixProvider`, because a `CSharpCodeFixTest` whose
     provider fixes nothing and whose fixed source equals its test source is exactly an analyzer test.
     *Rejected: pinning the second package*, one more central pin for six lines of local code. *Rejected: the
     hand-rolled `CodeFixTestHost` the research also proved end to end*, which needs no new package but would
     have to grow its own fix-all verification across three scopes, and it was a shared `equivalenceKey`
     lever in exactly that machinery that caught a real defect. **What it costs if wrong:** one test-only
     package reference, `PrivateAssets` by default in a test project, reaching no consumer.
     **Reversal cost:** one pin, one reference, and the harness file.

103. **STM003 ships a "how to fix" paragraph and a `helpLinkUri`, and no code fix.** Measured decisively:
     `SemanticModel.GetConstantValue` already folds literal concatenation, `const` fields, `nameof` and
     fully-constant interpolation, so every shape a hoisting fix would target is a shape the rule never
     fired on. Of the six shapes that do fire, the only mechanically repairable one is
     `static readonly string` to `const string`, which has **zero** occurrences in this repository, and
     which is a poor fix on its own terms: the diagnostic points at the *argument* while the edit lands on a
     *different member*, possibly in a different file, and is correct only if that field's initializer is
     itself constant and nothing else assigns it. The repository has 43 `StateKey.Define` call sites and
     exactly two non-constant ones, both deliberate — the library's own forwarding overload, which the
     analyzer is not applied to, and the analyzer's own negative test case. *Rejected: shipping the
     `static readonly` hoist anyway*, on the argument that a rule with a fix is more useful than one without
     — which buys a light bulb nobody in this repository can press and pays for it with a cross-member edit
     that can be silently wrong. **What it costs if wrong:** a consumer with that shape fixes it by hand,
     guided by a paragraph that names all five accepted shapes. **Reversal cost:** one provider and its
     facts.

104. **STM004 is a new diagnostic id, a new analyzer class, and design option (i): a receiver-chain walk
     with no data flow and no alias tracking.** The id is new rather than a widening of STM001 for three
     measured reasons: the two documentation files are one row and one `##` section per id and those slugs
     are the published anchors; a consumer with `<NoWarn>STM001</NoWarn>` during migration decided about
     *assignments*, not about `List<T>.Add`; and the two message formats cannot merge without going vague.
     Option (i) fires on 18 of 18 measured mutation shapes and stays silent on 10 of 10 must-not-fire
     shapes. *Rejected: folding it into STM001*, which would make the anchor
     `stm001-direct-managed-state-mutation` a lie and would either newly fire on a migrating consumer or
     newly go silent for them, depending on which way they configured it. *Rejected: option (ii), one-hop
     alias tracking*, sized **M** on its own and parked with its reason. *Rejected: option (iii), full data
     flow*, whose false-positive surface grows with every path. **What it costs if wrong:** a default-on
     warning fires on code that was legal yesterday; measured, it fires nowhere in this repository.
     **Reversal cost:** one file and one descriptor; a consumer can `<NoWarn>STM004</NoWarn>` without
     touching STM001, which is the whole point of the separate id.

105. **STM001's ownership becomes the same receiver-chain walk, and both rules call one shared
     `internal static ManagedStateOwnership` helper.** `AnalyzeMutation` asked only the *immediately
     containing* type for `[ManagedState]`, so `state.Plain.Value = 1` was silent where
     `state.Plain.Items.Add(1)` would report STM004 — the same `Plain`, on the same managed type, treated two
     ways. Shipping STM004 without this would ship that disagreement. Measured safe on the only code in this
     repository the analyzer is applied to: `samples/Statesman.Sample.Migration` builds with **zero**
     warnings from a cleared `obj` under both new rules, and that pass was proved non-vacuous by a lever that
     made STM004 fire in it. The walk stops at the nearest managed link, so a plain type's own constructor
     and own methods writing its own members are reached by no managed chain and stay silent — which is what
     keeps this from reporting on every plain class in a codebase, and it has its own fact. *Rejected:
     leaving STM001 alone and documenting the asymmetry*, which asks a reader to accept that a write is
     unmanaged and a collection mutation through the same member is managed. *Rejected: duplicating the walk
     in both analyzers*, which is two definitions of ownership that can drift. **What it costs if wrong:** a
     consumer's build newly warns at a write they had not been warned about, with `TreatWarningsAsErrors`
     turning it into a break — which is why it is a `### Changed` CHANGELOG entry and not a quiet
     improvement. **Reversal cost:** low in code (one call site) and high in expectation, since a rule that
     widened and narrowed again in consecutive releases is worse than one that never widened.

106. **Immutable-collection builders fire, and the discriminator is `type.ContainingType is null`, not a
     namespace test and not a return-type filter.** The controller's brief asked for the
     `type.ContainingType?.ContainingNamespace` clause to be dropped and the outer-namespace clause kept, on
     the reading that the two clauses separate `ImmutableList<T>` from `ImmutableList<T>.Builder`. Measured:
     they do not. Roslyn's `ContainingNamespace` for a nested type is the namespace of its *containing type*,
     so `ImmutableList<T>.Builder` reports `System.Collections.Immutable` too and either clause alone
     excludes it. Excluding only types with no containing type admits the builders and keeps
     `ImmutableList<T>.Add` silent, and both facts are measured. *Rejected: a return-type filter* — "fires
     only when the member returns void" — which looks like the natural rule and fails twice:
     `Builder.Add` returns `void` and would be admitted correctly, but `ImmutableList<T>` does implement
     `IList<T>` explicitly, so its throwing `void Add` would be admitted too. *Rejected: an explicit
     allow-list of builder type names*, which goes stale with every new immutable collection. **What it
     costs if wrong:** a mutation of a builder held by managed state is warned about, which is the correct
