     answer — a builder mutates in place, and holding one *as state* is the thing worth flagging.
     **Reversal cost:** one clause.

107. **The `readonly` field arm ships as-is, with its `CS0191` consequence documented rather than gated.**
     Measured: a field written from outside its declaring constructor goes from `[STM001, STM002]` to no
     analyzer diagnostic at all plus a compiler `CS0191` at the write site. So the fix can trade a warning
     for a build break. It ships anyway, on three grounds: Roslyn previews a code action before applying it,
     `CS0191` names the offending write precisely, and that write was already an STM001 warning, so the fix
     makes an existing complaint louder rather than inventing one. *Rejected: gating the arm behind
     `SymbolFinder.FindReferencesAsync` so it is offered only when every write is inside the declaring
     constructor*, which is the careful answer and was not prototyped — it makes a single-member fix do a
     solution-wide search, and it is parked rather than declined. *Rejected: dropping the field arm and
     shipping only the property arm*, which would leave the field half of STM002's own reported shape with
     no fix at all. **What it costs if wrong:** a developer applies a fix and gets a compile error naming the
     exact line to change next. **Reversal cost:** one guard, or one arm.

108. **The Redis fix is two guarded catches in `SubscribeAsync`, with no change to `DisposeAsync`, and two
     of the five caught types are honestly unpinned.** The throwing call site was named by stack trace: the
     `await subscriber.SubscribeAsync(...)`, which has no `catch` at HEAD, not the `MoveNextAsync` the read
     loop already guards. Both guards are narrowed by which token was cancelled, never by exception type
     alone, which is what keeps a genuine failure propagating — proved by lever, 5/5 deterministic. The
     stalled fact fails 10/10 with the fix reverted and 38/38 in the research, so the fix itself is pinned.
     But with `IOException` and `SocketException` removed from the catch set the stalled fact still passes,
     5/5 here and 30/30 in the research, because that shape appeared once in thirty baseline iterations.
     Those two types stay, on the strength of a measured exception inventory rather than a discriminating
     test, and the spec records it as a coverage gap. *Rejected: shipping only the three types a lever
     pins*, which would look correct for a long time and then let the rarer shape escape — the research
     measured exactly that trap. *Rejected: `ISubscriber.UnsubscribeAllAsync()` in `DisposeAsync`*, which
     would make a `ValueTask.CompletedTask` genuinely asynchronous and is itself a round trip that hangs
     under the stalled condition the fix has to survive. *Rejected: passing a cancellation token to
     `SubscribeAsync`*, which StackExchange.Redis 3.1.31 does not accept. **What it costs if wrong:** a
     socket failure during the store's own disposal is swallowed where it should have surfaced — bounded by
     the token condition, which requires the store's disposal to be the cancelling party. **Reversal cost:**
     two `catch` clauses.

109. **STM004's descriptor stays in the `internal static DiagnosticDescriptors` class, so this phase's public
     delta is exactly two types.** The research prototype declared `CollectionMutation` as a
     `public static readonly DiagnosticDescriptor` field on the analyzer class, which is how many analyzer
     packages expose their descriptors. That is deliberately not what ships: the three existing descriptors
     are internal fields in one file, and a public descriptor is an API commitment made for no consumer,
     since nothing outside the assembly reads them. *Rejected: following the prototype*, which would add a
     public field whose type is a Roslyn type, in a package whose validation gate cannot see it either way —
     an unchecked commitment is worse than a checked one. **What it costs if wrong:** a consumer who wants to
     reference the descriptor programmatically cannot. **Reversal cost:** one accessibility keyword, and it
     is purely additive to make it public later.

110. **Phase 20 is eight tasks, and the two lever-only tasks the research proposed are folded into the tasks
     whose mechanism they falsify.** The research's nine-task list had a dedicated task for STM002's levers
     and another for STM004's. Each is a one-line "apply, observe the named failure, revert" step with no
     diff at the end, so each would cost a dispatch and a review for an empty commit. They are folded in,
     and the controller's ruling permitted exactly this. Task 2, the harness, is kept as its own task
     despite having no shipped behaviour of its own, because decision 102's `NoFixProvider` gives it a real
     fact a reviewer can accept or reject; had the harness needed the code-fix provider to be testable at
     all, it would have been folded into Task 3. *Rejected: keeping the lever-only tasks*, which is process
     for its own sake. *Rejected: folding Tasks 4 and 5 (STM004 and STM001's walk) together*, which would
     put a new default-on rule and a behaviour change to a shipped rule behind one review. **Reversal
     cost:** none; task boundaries are review checkpoints, not code boundaries.

## Addendum (pre-Phase-21, 2026-09-14)

Decisions taken while writing the Phase 21 section and its implementation plan. **As in Phases 8
through 20, they were taken without `AskUserQuestion` because the session ran under an autonomous
`/goal`.** These are the ones to revisit if that ruling was wrong, and each is listed with the
alternative rejected and what reversing it would cost.

**The two most worth revisiting are decision 118 — the `RedisKeyLayout` option, which is the answer
to a product question about whether a consumer's stored keys may ever move — and decision 119, the
`redis-cluster` CI job it enables, which adds a job to every push and pull request and makes `pack`
wait on it.** Both are marked below as **the user should revisit**.

**Numbering continues the pre-Phase-20 addendum, which ended at 110.**

111. **Phase 21 is "STM004 completeness, the ownership walk, the Redis read-loop fact, and Redis
     Cluster", and nothing the research measured as ready is re-parked.** The research's Tasks 1
     through 9 all ship, plus a Dependabot hygiene task and the close-out: ten tasks. The standing
     preference is completeness over the cheaper option, and that nothing parked wastes away. *Rejected:
     the research's own fallback*, which was to cut its Tasks 8 and 9 (the key layout and the cluster
     job) together and keep the ruling as documentation only — a defensible cut that would have left
     bullet 6 open for a fourth phase with the measurement already in hand. **What it costs if wrong:**
     a larger phase, with its last two tasks the only ones needing live infrastructure. **Reversal
     cost:** those two tasks are the last two before the close-out and are separable at their task
     boundaries.

112. **STM004's membership becomes one interface rule with two operands, not a type whitelist and not
     a longer list of names alone.** A type mutates in place when it implements or is
     `System.Collections.Generic.ICollection<T>` **or** `System.Collections.ICollection`, and is not a
     top-level type in `System.Collections.Immutable`. Measured: the two operands discriminate
     different rows — removing the non-generic one fails 10 facts and removing the generic one fails 2
     — so neither implies the other, and `ISet<T>`/`IList<T>`/`IDictionary<,>` genuinely do not extend
     the non-generic interface. `Mutators` grows from 16 names to 42 in the same change, because the
     boundary was partly by name even on types the rule already analysed. *Rejected: a type
     whitelist*, which goes stale with every new collection type in the BCL. *Rejected: widening the
     name set alone*, which would leave every `Queue<T>` and every concurrent collection silent.
     *Rejected: dropping `Take` and `GetOrAdd` as too ambiguous* — `Take`'s ambiguity is with
     `Enumerable.Take`, whose declaring type is not a collection, and the type test is what guards it;
     a fact pins that. **What it costs if wrong:** a default-on rule newly warns on code that was legal
     yesterday; measured, it warns nowhere in this repository, and nowhere in a five-project build with
     the analyzer wired into four more projects than the one that gates it. **Reversal cost:** one
     method and one list, in one file.

113. **The ownership walk gains three conversion arms, and the null-forgiving arm ships even though
     the obvious shapes for it do not need one.** Measured: `s.Plain!.Value = 1` and
     `s.Plain!.Items.Add(1)` already report, because Roslyn's `GetSymbolInfo` on a suppression answers
     with the operand's symbol, so the walk finds the owner without stepping. The arm is reached only
     when the suppressed expression has no symbol of its own — `((PlainBag)s.Plain)!.Value = 1` and
     `(s.Coll as List<int>)!.Add(1)` — and those are the facts that ship, because they are the ones
     that discriminate. *Rejected: shipping the two obvious rows as the arm's facts*, which is what the
     research's task list implied and which would have put a green, non-discriminating fact in front of
     a reviewer as coverage. *Rejected: dropping the arm as unreachable*, which the first measurement
     appeared to justify and the second disproved. **What it costs if wrong:** one `switch` arm that
     could be dead. **Reversal cost:** three lines and two facts.

114. **Alias tracking ships as option (ii) with four bail-outs, and the research prototype's lambda
     and local-function bail-out is dropped as redundant.** Every rebind a lambda can perform is
     already an assignment or an `out`/`ref` argument, both of which the shipped checks catch wherever
     they appear — pinned by a fact whose lever is the assignment check. What the dropped bail-out
     uniquely suppressed was a lambda that only **reads** the alias, which defers the mutation without
     changing which object is mutated: a true positive. The prototype's unary check is narrowed to the
     four increment and decrement `SyntaxKind`s for the same class of reason: a postfix `!` is a
     `PostfixUnaryExpressionSyntax` too, so testing the node type alone would have silenced every
     null-forgiving mention of an alias. *Rejected: shipping the prototype's eight bail-outs verbatim*,
     which would have shipped two operands that fire on nothing against this effort's own "one lever
     per operand" rule, and one that loses a true positive. *Rejected: option (iii)*, re-parked.
     **What it costs if wrong:** an alias rebound in a way none of the four checks sees is reported
     against the wrong object. **Reversal cost:** one method; the `IdentifierNameSyntax` arm can be
     deleted to restore Phase 20's behaviour exactly.

115. **The read-loop fact is a test-only seam over StackExchange.Redis's internals, reached by
     reflection, with no production change and no `[InternalsVisibleTo]`.** The store already takes an
     `IConnectionMultiplexer`, so a `DispatchProxy` covers the two interfaces in about forty lines; the
     one concrete type is built through its internal constructor and faulted through its internal
     `MarkCompleted`. The dependency this creates is named rather than hidden: **the internals were
     re-measured at StackExchange.Redis 3.2.1**, the version this repository moved to after the
     research ran, and are unchanged. *Rejected: adding `[InternalsVisibleTo]`*, which would break a
     deliberate, zero-count repository convention for a test. *Rejected: a production seam* — an
     injectable factory for the subscription — which is public API added for a test. *Rejected: a
     third `StallProxy` mode*, which the Phase 20 measurements show cannot reach the read loop at all.
     **What it costs if wrong:** a future StackExchange.Redis release renames a private member and the
     fact fails loudly at build or run time, naming the member. **Reversal cost:** delete one file.

116. **The `event` add/remove asymmetry is documented, not fixed.** An event subscription genuinely is
     not a state write, and making it one would fire on every idiomatic observer wiring. One sentence
     in the guide's STM001 section and one pinning fact per rule, each naming the different mechanism
     that holds it. Measured while writing those facts: a **third** guard holds `s.Changed` itself —
     the ownership walk's owner test admits only properties and fields — so both levers fire on
     `s.Plain.Changed += …` alone. *Rejected: adding `IEventSymbol` to `AnalyzeMutation`'s symbol test*
     (about two lines plus four facts), which would report on every `+=` against a managed type's
     event. **What it costs if wrong:** a genuine mutation performed inside an event handler is still
     caught at the mutation site; only the subscription is silent. **Reversal cost:** one pattern,
     and the facts invert.

117. **A deterministic second `StallProxy` stall mode is closed by measurement, not carried.** The
     research re-ran Phase 20's Lever 12 and it failed **2 of 20**, confirming the fix wave's 3 of 20;
     the existing stalled fact does discriminate both halves of the catch set. And decision 115's seam
     obtains determinism on the same surface without any stall mode. Recorded in "Explicitly parked" as
     closed-by-measurement, the way decision 96 was. *Rejected: carrying it a fourth time.* **What it
     costs if wrong:** a socket-level reproduction of the `IOException`/`SocketException` shape remains
     intermittent rather than on demand. **Reversal cost:** none; it can be written later.

118. **`RedisKeyLayout` ships as an option on `RedisStateLedgerStoreOptions` defaulting to `Legacy`,
     with a per-store hash tag as `SingleSlot`. (The user should revisit.)** Measured: the per-store
     tag is the only granularity that works, because `AppendScript` touches three per-address keys and
     three per-store keys in one `EVAL` and `CaptureAsync` spans arbitrarily many addresses in one
     `MULTI`/`EXEC`; and it takes the conformance suite from 47 failures to zero against a real
     cluster, identical to its standalone result. Keys move under it, so it is opt-in and the changelog
     entry is `### Added`. The cost is stated rather than buried: one store's key space occupies one
     slot and therefore one master node, so a cluster buys availability and multi-tenancy, not
     per-store write scale-out. *Rejected: a `Statesman.Tooling` migration that renames keys*, with no
     atomic primitive to do it under a live writer when export and import already exist. *Rejected: a
     documented breaking change*, a hard break on an unreleased alpha for a feature nobody has asked
     for. *Rejected: making the tag unconditional later*, strictly worse than shipping the option now,
     because the option is what lets a consumer opt in on their own schedule. *Rejected: an option on
     `Statesman.Outbox.Redis` too*, measured unnecessary — every one of its operations is single-key
     and it passes 17 of 17 on a cluster unchanged. **What it costs if wrong:** one enum and one
     property of public surface that nobody sets. **Reversal cost:** the property can be removed only
     as a breaking change once released, so this is the decision with the highest reversal cost in the
     phase.

119. **The `redis-cluster` job runs `docker run` in a step, selects the layout through a new
     `STATESMAN_TEST_REDIS_KEY_LAYOUT` environment variable read in one shared place, and joins
     `pack`'s `needs:`. (The user should revisit.)** Actions passes a `services:` block only
     `docker create` options, so it can supply neither the `redis-server` arguments that enable cluster
     mode nor the `CLUSTER ADDSLOTS` that must follow the server starting; the exact shell was composed
     and run locally and reaches `cluster_state:ok`. The variable lives in
     `tests/Shared/RedisTestLayout.cs`, linked into the three test projects that construct a store, so
     forty construction sites read one decision. Adding the job to `pack`'s `needs:` matches every
     other infrastructure job and is what makes the gate real. *Rejected: a `services:` block*, which
     cannot start a cluster. *Rejected: a subset of the four projects*, when the full set takes about
     the same time as the existing `redis-tests` job and the conformance suite is the measurement that
     matters. *Rejected: leaving the job out of `pack`'s `needs:`*, which would let a red cluster job
     ship a package. *Rejected: reading the variable per test project*, which is how
     `STATESMAN_TEST_REDIS` is read today and is why forty call sites exist to sweep. **What it costs
     if wrong:** one more required job on every push, roughly the duration of `redis-tests`.
     **Reversal cost:** delete the job and one `needs:` line; the shared helper stays useful.

120. **Roslyn's three packages are ignored by Dependabot and move by hand with `global.json`.** The
     version `Statesman.Analyzers` references is its consumers' compiler floor, which is a
     consumer-facing consequence no bot can weigh. Measured during the dependency refresh: the pinned
     SDK 10.0.303 ships the 5.6.x compiler, and `dotnet nuget why` shows nothing in the restore graph
     requires more than 5.0.0, so no conflict forces a bump. Pull requests #12 and #13 both proposed
     5.9.0 as a `VersionOverride`, a shape `eng/validate.py` rejects outright, and both were closed.
     The three names are exact, so `Microsoft.CodeAnalysis.CSharp.CodeFix.Testing` keeps getting
     updates. *Rejected: leaving Dependabot to keep proposing it*, which costs a triage every week and
     has already produced the same rejected diff twice. *Rejected: ignoring the whole
     `Microsoft.CodeAnalysis.*` prefix*, which would also silence the test-only harness package.
     **What it costs if wrong:** a Roslyn security fix has to be noticed by hand. **Reversal cost:**
     delete three lines.

121. **Phase 21 is ten tasks, in an order that puts every live-infrastructure step last.** Tasks 1
     through 7 need no container at all; Task 8 needs standalone Redis; Task 9 needs the cluster.
     Documentation that three earlier tasks each leave pending is collected into Task 7 rather than
     split across them, because the guide's STM004 section has to be rewritten once with all three
     changes in view rather than three times. Implementers are Sonnet for every task that compiles or
     runs code and Haiku only for Task 1 and pure transcription; reviewers are Sonnet; the final
     whole-branch review is Opus with live Redis standalone **and** the single-node cluster.
     *Rejected: folding Tasks 2, 3 and 5 together*, which would put three behaviour changes to shipped
     rules behind one review. *Rejected: writing each task's documentation inside that task*, which
     would rewrite one guide section three times and make the third rewrite a merge rather than an
     edit. **Reversal cost:** none; task boundaries are review checkpoints, not code boundaries.

## Addendum (pre-Phase-22, 2026-09-15)

Decisions taken while writing the Phase 22 section and its implementation plan. **As in Phases 8
through 21, they were taken without `AskUserQuestion` because the session ran under an autonomous
`/goal`.** These are the ones to revisit if that ruling was wrong, and each is listed with the
alternative rejected and what reversing it would cost.

**The three most worth revisiting are decision 123 — overturning a ruling Phase 21's fix wave made the
week before, and inverting the test that carried it — decision 124, the envelope's storage format,
which is the shape of a persisted contract and the one decision here with a real reversal cost once
released, and decision 129, declaring ROADMAP 0.2's first exit criterion met on a ruling rather than on
new coverage.** All three are marked below as **the user should revisit**.

**Numbering continues the pre-Phase-21 addendum, which ended at 121.**

*Amended (Phase 22, 2026-09-15):* decisions **135 and 136** were added after items 1 through 4 had
landed, when Task 4's review found that the outbox message never read the record's envelope. They are
the only decisions in this addendum taken mid-phase rather than while the section was written.

122. **Phase 22 is "Serializer envelopes, the analyzer's last three gaps, and closing ROADMAP 0.2", and
     nothing the research measured as ready is re-parked.** The research's section 8 tasks 1 through 9
     all ship, plus the section 5 Redis `ImportAsync` Lua script as its own task and the close-out: ten
     tasks, in an order that puts every live-infrastructure step last. *Amended (Phase 22, 2026-09-15):*
     eleven, once decision 135 folded the outbox in as Task 9b. The standing preference is
     completeness over the cheaper option, and that nothing parked wastes away. *Rejected: the
     research's own framing of the import script as "the first thing to drop if time is short"* — it
     was prototyped in planning, went GREEN, and is RED at baseline on 10 of 10 runs, so dropping it
     would discard a measured fix for a documented hazard. *Rejected: splitting the envelope across two
     phases*, provider by provider, which would leave a half-written storage format on `main` between
     them. **What it costs if wrong:** a large phase whose middle six tasks are one feature. **Reversal
     cost:** the envelope tasks are separable at provider boundaries, and the two analyzer tasks and the
     import script are independent of everything.

123. **STM001 sees a deconstructing assignment, and the Phase 21 fact that pinned the gap is renamed
     and inverted in the same task. (The user should revisit.)** The gap is an asymmetry on the same
     member — `q.Value = 1` reports and `(q.Value, _) = (1, 0)` did not — rather than a scope limit,
     and the fix is one small method with zero measured false positives across a ten-shape adversarial
     sweep and a repository-wide search that finds the shape nowhere in `src`, `tests` or `samples`. The
     tension this resolves was live rather than settled: Phase 21's fix wave landed a fact whose name
     contains "never" while the handover simultaneously listed M5 as a Phase 22 candidate to fix.
     *Rejected: keeping the fact and strengthening its comment to say the gap is permanent*, which is
     the other coherent outcome and which the research named; it would leave a real false negative in a
     default-on rule for no measured benefit. **What it costs if wrong:** a shipped rule newly warns
     code that has not changed, on a shape the repository itself never writes; and a test whose name
     asserted "never" now asserts the opposite, which a reader of the git history has to reconcile.
     **Reversal cost:** delete one method, restore one call, and invert one fact back — the same edit in
     the other direction.

124. **The envelope's storage format: one nullable `StateEnvelope` record, a nested JSON object on the
     document providers, one nullable JSON column per Entity Framework Core entity. (The user should
     revisit.)** Measured against three alternatives. *Rejected: three flat members on the record*
     (`ContentType`, `SerializerId`, `Fingerprint`), which measured identically on build, pack and
     failing facts, and costs four extra Entity Framework Core columns now and a migration on all three
     engines for **every future envelope member**; it also has eight states where one nullable member
     has two, seven of them meaningless. *Rejected: a framing prefix inside `Payload`*, which fails
     **zero** facts across ten test projects while breaking every 0.3.0 reader one-way, moving
     `MaxBytes` accounting by 40 bytes per record and making a downgrade data loss. *Rejected: making
     the members `required`*, measured at 35-plus construction sites across 22-plus files and a source
     break for every consumer. *Rejected: the research's positional `StateEnvelope`*, fine for a probe
     and wrong for a public type whose member set may grow, because a positional record's constructor
     is a signature the package-validation gate then holds forever. **What it costs if wrong:** the
     nested object and the JSON column are a persisted shape; changing them after release is a data
     migration, which is why this is the decision with the highest reversal cost in the phase.
     **Reversal cost:** before release, one member and two mapping lines per provider plus one
     migration; after release, a storage-format change.

125. **`SerializerId` and `ContentType` ship as default interface members with stable string values,
     and the `Migrate` path does not stamp a migrated-from schema version.** The first half is measured
     additive: zero `CP`, no implementer break, and a hand-rolled serializer implementing only the four
     original methods still compiles and still reports a non-empty identity. `JsonStateSerializer`'s
     values are public constants and are documented as a persisted contract, so a future incompatible
     encoding change ships as a new id. The second half is measured **declined**: migration happens on
     read, in `CreateTypedSnapshot`, producing a public `StateSnapshot<T>` with no member to carry
     provenance, and the main write path serializes a value the caller supplies rather than the
     migrated one — so stamping it would be a public API addition that asserts something false.
     *Rejected: an abstract member*, which breaks every implementer. *Rejected: a `[Obsolete]`-guarded
     interface split*, surface for no behaviour. *Rejected: carrying migrated-from anyway on the
     keep-value paths only*, which would make one member true on two of three write points and silently
     absent on the third. **What it costs if wrong:** "migration provenance" in the ROADMAP bullet is
     satisfied by fingerprint plus format version beside the record's existing schema version, which a
     reader may consider thinner than the word implies. **Reversal cost:** one nullable member and one
     public snapshot member, once there is a write path that genuinely knows the answer.

126. **The Redis member ordering trap is a named step with its own fact, and the brief's reason for it
     is corrected.** The member is declared before `GlobalPosition` and the plan says why, with the
     exact `PositionPrefix` message. This phase's brief states that a round-trip test alone does not
     catch the trap; measured, that is true of an **import**-based round trip, which does not go through
     `PositionPrefix`, and false of an **append**-based one: with the member misplaced the shared
     conformance suite's Redis rows go `failed: 2`. The dedicated fact ships anyway, because it pins the
     mechanism — the stored member still ends `"globalPosition":1}` — rather than only the round trip.
     *Rejected: relying on the conformance suite alone*, which would leave the invariant unnamed and its
     failure undiagnosed. *Rejected: asserting only that the envelope round-trips*, which is the half
     that passes with the member in the wrong place on any path that skips the append script. **What it
     costs if wrong:** one extra Redis fact. **Reversal cost:** delete it.

127. **Legacy-read facts are seeded with bytes captured from the pre-envelope tree, not hand-written.**
     Five provider facts plus one pinning the stream hash both the filesystem and Redis derive. The
     bytes were captured by running `df50d12` and dumping what each provider wrote, because a
     hand-written seed proves only that the test author and the reader agree about the format. Their
     discrimination proof is one stated edit: add an envelope key and all five must stop passing,
     measured. *Rejected: asserting the legacy behaviour through the shared conformance suite's
     "written without an envelope" row*, which exercises **this** release's writer and therefore proves
     nothing about the previous one's bytes; that row ships too, for a different reason. *Rejected: a
     shared legacy suite*, when each provider's pre-envelope shape is a property of its own storage
     format and the point of each fact is the literal bytes. **What it costs if wrong:** six facts whose
     seeds are frozen text that a future storage change would have to update deliberately, which is the
     intent. **Reversal cost:** delete one file.

128. **The shared conformance suite carries four facts, and the fourth is the null half.** "A record
     written without an envelope reads back with none" is not padding: a provider that materialised an
     empty envelope, or round-tripped null through a default instance, would break every pre-release
     record while failing nothing else in the suite. `BrokenSerializerEnvelopeConformanceTests` is the
     discrimination proof in the repository's existing shape, and its own lever — making the wrong
     double stop dropping the envelope — was fired and observed. *Rejected: three facts*, which would
     have left the null half unpinned on four storage formats. **What it costs if wrong:** five more
     rows. **Reversal cost:** delete one fact from the base class.

129. **Per-provider corruption coverage satisfies ROADMAP 0.2's first exit criterion, and the criterion's
     wording is amended to say so; the target-framework criterion is narrowed to the measured truth.
     (The user should revisit.)** Decision 98 parks general corruption conformance **by design**, on the
     reasoning that each provider's failure shape is a property of its own storage format, and Phase 16
     decision 63 keeps verify and repair per-provider for the same reason. Without this ruling, bullet 4
     shipping still leaves 0.2 unable to be declared complete, which would be a poor outcome for a phase
     whose whole point is closing it. The second half is smaller and costs only honesty: CI builds
     net8.0, net9.0 and net10.0 for thirteen packages and **tests net10.0 only**. *Rejected: building a
     shared corruption suite*, sized **L** and contradicting a standing decision. *Rejected: leaving the
     criterion's wording as it is and declaring 0.2 complete anyway*, which would make the milestone's
     own exit criteria disagree with the milestone. *Rejected: adding a net8.0/net9.0 test-run job in
     this phase*, which is a change to every test project's framework set rather than a workflow edit;
     it is recorded as a Phase 23 candidate. **What it costs if wrong:** a milestone is declared
     complete on a ruling rather than on new coverage. **Reversal cost:** the wording, and the milestone
     line, are two paragraphs in `ROADMAP.md`.

130. **The alias hop's scope falls back to the compilation unit, the arrow fallback is deleted with no
     lever, and `AnalyzeTarget` keeps its parenthesis arm because that arm moves a reported span.** Two
     measurements drive this. The arrow fallback is unreachable, proved directly rather than from the
     research's syntax sweep: with it deleted and nothing put in its place, the analyzer suite reports
     exactly the same failures as with it present, so **no lever can exist** and the plan says so
     instead of inventing one. The parenthesis arm is the mirror image: it does **not** change whether a
     parenthesized deconstruction element reports, because Roslyn resolves a parenthesized expression to
     its operand's symbol, but it does change the reported span from `(s.Child.Value)` to
     `s.Child.Value`. *Rejected: shipping the arrow fallback beside the new one*, a branch measured to
     reach nothing, in a phase that deletes one for exactly that reason. *Rejected: dropping the
     parenthesis arm* once it was measured not to change any diagnostic, which was the honest reading
     until the span was measured too. **What it costs if wrong:** one task ships a deletion with no
     failing test behind it, which is a deviation from this effort's own discipline and is stated as
     one. **Reversal cost:** two lines each.

131. **The Entity Framework Core migration ships in the three ledger packages, its ids are recorded
     rather than pinned, and the drift test's ALL SIX message is corrected in the same task.** The
     outbox context is untouched, measured. Migration ids are UTC timestamps taken at generation, so the
     plan cannot contain them and says so; the implementer records the three it gets and the close-out
     carries them into the docs page. The drift message's instruction would have produced three empty
     outbox migrations whose ids the same message calls a permanent public contract. *Rejected:
     generating six migrations to match the message*, which is the defect the message causes. *Rejected:
     pinning expected ids in the plan*, which no implementer could satisfy. *Rejected: four typed
     columns instead of one JSON column*, see decision 124. **What it costs if wrong:** a contributor
     reading the new message adds three migrations where a future change genuinely needs six, which the
     drift facts then catch per package. **Reversal cost:** one string.

132. **The Redis `ImportAsync` Lua script ships, and its fact forces the interleaving with a proxy gate
     rather than with timing.** Addendum decision 50 named this script as "the only real fix" and took
     the documented-sentence option because a cluster could not run it; `SingleSlot` removed that
     obstacle in Phase 21. The script is atomic on the server, so the read of the stale member and the
     writes that depend on it cannot be separated, and the condition-guarded retry loop disappears with
     it. The fact's gate pauses the first import on a `TaskCompletionSource` in the window a
     transactional import has between its read and its write; once the import is one script that window
     does not exist, the gate never trips, and the fact is written to proceed either way. *Rejected: a
     timing-based race*, which the Phase 4 lesson in the handover's "Read before trusting" section rules
     out. *Rejected: keeping the optimistic retry loop around the script*, which would be a retry for a
     conflict that can no longer occur. *Rejected: leaving decision 50's documented sentence in place*,
     when the fix is now both feasible and measured. **What it costs if wrong:** the one write path
     Phase 8 deliberately left alone has changed mechanism, on a provider where member identity is the
     serialized bytes. **Reversal cost:** restore one method body; the script is additive text.

133. **Every sentence this phase falsifies is amended in place, and the two Phase 21 caveats are retired
     with numbers rather than carried.** The Phase 21 parked entry for M5, the handover's M5 candidate
     line, the `MessageId` item's recorded premise, and the drift test's message each get an in-place
     `*Amended (Phase 22, 2026-09-15):*` pointer in the task that changes them, never a silent rewrite.
     The two caveats are closed with the research's section 3 numbers. *Rejected: carrying the caveats a
     third time*, which the Phase 19 lesson — a thrice-parked item's premise was never measured — rules
     out. **What it costs if wrong:** the spec grows amendment pointers rather than staying short.
     **Reversal cost:** none.

134. **Ten tasks; Sonnet implements everything that compiles or runs code, Haiku only Task 3; the final
     whole-branch review is Opus with live Redis standalone, a single-node cluster, SQL Server and
     PostgreSQL.** Tasks 1 through 7 need no container; Task 8 needs standalone Redis; Task 9 needs
     standalone Redis and the cluster; Task 10 needs all four engines. *Amended (Phase 22, 2026-09-15):*
     eleven tasks, and Task 9b is Sonnet like every other task that compiles code. It needs no container
     of its own; its one live run, `Statesman.Outbox.Redis.Tests`, is a regression check that skips
     honestly without a server. *Rejected: folding items 5, 6 and
     8 into one provider task*, which would put three storage-format changes behind one review.
     *Rejected: Haiku for Task 1*, which edits an analyzer and adds a compile path to the test host.
     **Reversal cost:** none; task boundaries are review checkpoints, not code boundaries.

135. **The outbox message reads the record's envelope, and it gains a `SerializerId` field to do it
     properly. Folded into this phase as Task 9b rather than deferred.** *Taken 2026-09-15, after items
     1 through 4 had landed, on Task 4's reviewer's finding and verified against
     `src/Statesman.Outbox/StateChangeMessage.cs`.* `FromRecord` never read `record.Envelope`: the
     content type came from `OutboxOptions.PayloadContentType` and the fingerprint from
     `OutboxOptions.Fingerprint`, so from the moment item 4 landed a published message could assert
     something the record it was built from contradicted, and no fact in the repository would have
     failed. The outbox is also the **only** place the envelope reaches a consumer outside this
     repository, and this phase's own research named it the clearest consumer of ROADMAP 0.2 bullet 4,
     quoting the type's doc comment as the plainest statement of what the bullet buys. Shipping the
     ledger half of that gap and leaving the outbox half open would have been the phase's own
     rejected-design failure mode: a change nothing can distinguish from a no-op. *Rejected: deferring
     it to Phase 23*, which would ship a release in which the feature contradicts itself at its one
     outward-facing edge. *Rejected: adding it to item 4 retroactively*, when item 4 has landed,
     been reviewed and been committed. *Rejected: renumbering items 5 through 10 to make room*, when
     five of those tasks are dispatched or in flight under their current numbers — hence the letter
     suffix. *Rejected: a `SerializerId` fallback on `OutboxOptions`*, which would recreate exactly the
     defect this decision fixes, an outbox asserting an encoding fact it was told rather than one the
     writer recorded. *Rejected: bumping `StateChangeMessageFormat.Version`*, measured unnecessary: the
     format's serializer options omit a null property, so a message from a record with no envelope
     carries no `serializerId` key and is byte identical to what previous releases wrote.
     **What it costs if wrong:** one nullable field of public surface and of wire surface that a
     consumer may ignore; and a consumer who had been relying on `OutboxOptions.PayloadContentType`
     overriding whatever the writer recorded now gets the writer's answer instead, which is the
     behaviour change and is recorded as a `### Changed` entry. **Reversal cost:** three initializer
     lines and one property, before release; after release the field is public and wire surface, so
     removing it is a breaking change.

136. **A null `Fingerprint` inside a populated envelope still falls back to `OutboxOptions.Fingerprint`,
     and `SerializerId` is gated on the payload exactly as `ContentType` is.** Two small rulings inside
     decision 135, each with its own fact. The first: `StateEnvelope.Fingerprint` is null when the
     writer had no declaration to name, which is not a statement that the outbox's configured
     fingerprint is wrong, so overriding it to null would discard information for nothing. The second:
     `ContentType`'s documented invariant is "null exactly when there is no payload", that invariant is
     a wire contract, and a serializer id describes the same absent bytes — so both are gated, and the
     lever that removes the gate fires the Phase 7 fact that has guarded it since Phase 7 as well as the
     new one. *Rejected: envelope-wins-absolutely for the fingerprint*, which loses the option's value
     on a record whose writer simply had no manifest. *Rejected: leaving `SerializerId` ungated*, which
     would have made the pair asymmetric on the one shape where neither field means anything.
     **What it costs if wrong:** a message built from a foreign record with a fingerprint-less envelope
     names the outbox's declaration rather than nothing. **Reversal cost:** one `??` and one ternary.
